## A.2 Project/Programme Executive Summary (max 300 words)

The key objective of the project is to strengthen resilience in smallholder communities, particularly women, in southern Zimbabwe to increasing climate risks and impacts. The project will support the Government of Zimbabwe in strengthening the resilience of agricultural livelihoods of vulnerable communities, particularly women, in southern Zimbabwe to increasing climate risks and impacts. The project will benefit an estimated 150,624 direct (and approximately 543,620 direct and 1,758,500 indirect beneficiaries) across Manicaland, Matabeleland South provinces.

The proposed project will enhance the water security for smallholder farmers in light of evolving climate risks by enabling revitalization and climate-proofing of irrigation schemes and improving water use efficiency through modern management practices. The project will also support the Government of Zimbabwe to strengthen resilience of smallholder farmers through Farmer Field Schools and peer-to-peer support to scale up climate-resilient agriculture, with access to resilient inputs, markets, and actionable climate information. The project will be implemented in three provinces: Manicaland, Matabeleland South and Masvingo. It supports sustainable agriculture – including value chain actors and financial intermediaries – to make a transformative shift away from subsistence livelihoods towards more productive and profitable agricultural enterprises. The project is designed specifically for smallholder farmers in the three provinces so that it is directed towards climate-resilient varieties. The project yields significant ecosystem benefits, including improved water retention capacity of soils, reduced soil erosion, and increased biodiversity.

The proposed project aligns with the Government’s priorities outlined in its Nationally Determined Contributions (NDC) and climate change plans and strategies including: strengthening management of water resources and irrigation in the face of climate change; strengthening capacities to generate new forms of empirical knowledge, provision of technologies (including conservation agriculture), and improved access to information on climate variability and change through a national network of meteorological and hydrological services to provide timely climate data. The proposed project is prioritised for inclusion in the country’s NDCs and climate change plans and strategies as it will contribute significantly towards achieving the objectives, including extensive stakeholder consultations involving civil society, bilateral donors, and communities. The NDA has issued a no objection letter.

B.1. Description of Financial Elements of the Project / Program

The Government of Zimbabwe seeks maximum concessionality in the form of grant financing for the proposed urgent alleviation of climate change impacts on smallholder farmers in the most vulnerable Agro-Ecological Regions (IV and V) of southern Zimbabwe to adapt to increasing temperatures and rainfall variability.

GCF funding will be used to address barriers related to:

- **i)** Water management for smallholder farmers by enabling revitalization and climate-proofing of existing irrigation schemes, improving water use efficiency and enhancing soil moisture management on rain-fed lands (Output 1).
- **ii)** Multi-level capacity building for the Government of Zimbabwe, including training of smallholder farmers in Farmer Field Schools and support to peer-to-peer extension of the packages of climate-resilient innovations to current practice (Output 2).
- **iii)** Access to climate-resilient crops through their participation in localized Innovation Platforms involving all stakeholders—including buyers and credit providers—to extend the value chain (Output 3).

The project will be implemented by the Government of Zimbabwe, with co-financing from GCF. The project will develop the network of hydro-meteorological stations in the catchments of the project area and build the capacity of institutional personnel to compile and analyze data for climate monitoring and early warning systems. This will include the use of mobile devices including SMS and other communication channels (Output 3).

### Co-Financing

The co-financing for the project is USD 21,348,300, which includes USD 20,918,620 (95% of the total project costs) to be provided by the Government of Zimbabwe and USD 429,680 from GCF under the project duration of seven years.

### Government Co-financing

Of the total GCF co-financing, USD 17,347,000 is in cash and USD 5,701,300 is in kind. Co-financing from the GoZ will complement GCF resources for investments in:

- **i)** Revitalization and climate-proofing of 23 climate-vulnerable irrigation schemes (Activity 1.1), Output 1).
- **ii)** Scaling up climate-resilient agricultural practices by 50,000 smallholder farmers and beneficiary farmers in 137 climate vulnerable watersheds (Activity 2.2, Output 2).
- **iii)** Multi-level capacity building for the Government of Zimbabwe to develop and implement of climate-risk planning and management, knowledge generation and learning to build long-term adaptive capacities of institutions and smallholder farmers (Activity 2.2, Output 2).
- **iv)** A national early warning system for monitoring and information systems benefiting farmers directly, together with the rural population of the targeted watersheds in the 15 districts (Activity 3.1 and Activity 3.2, Output 3); and
- **v)** Project Management to facilitate the implementation of project activities.

In-kind contributions include staff time from AGRITEK, DURDZVU, and DDBS will support climate-proofing of irrigation schemes and setting up of the maintenance funds for post-project GMRM. The establishment of Farmer Field Schools and facilitation of climate-resilient agricultural practices by 50,000 smallholder farmers in 137 climate-vulnerable watersheds (Activity 2.2) will be supervised by lead farmer engagement with smallholders; mentoring and engagement with private sector entrepreneurs and other entities (Activity 2.2). The national early warning system for monitoring and information systems benefiting farmers directly, together with the rural population of the targeted watersheds in the 15 districts (Activity 3.1 and Activity 3.2) will be supervised by lead farmer engagement with smallholders; mentoring and engagement with private sector entrepreneurs and other entities (Activity 3.1 and Activity 3.2). Project Management to facilitate the implementation of project activities (Activity 4).

The total co-financing being provided by GoZ for the project is USD 20,918,620 of which USD 16,267,580 is in cash and USD 4,651,040 is in kind. In addition, USD 30,000 will be provided by GCF as contributions towards post-project GMRM for the lead water infrastructure for a period of 10 years after the project ends.

# C.1. Strategic Context

## 11. Southern Zimbabwe is home to 30% of the country’s 14.5 million** people and 45% of the country’s rural population**, including most of the country’s poor.

## 12. About 7.1 million people in Zimbabwe depend on unfarmable land, most of whom are women. Over the past five years, Zimbabwe has experienced a sharp decline in the rate of economic growth from 13.9% to 5.3% to 0.5%. This decline is largely due to droughts and floods, which have reduced agricultural production by about 20%.

## 13. The country’s food security situation is heavily impacted by the quality and quantity of rainfall with extreme events such as droughts or floods being the most damaging.

## 14. While climate change affects the entire country, impacts are experienced most intensely in the southern provinces, where the majority of smallholder farmers are extremely vulnerable to increasing climate hazards as a result of poverty and weak access to services and resources.

## 15. The agricultural sector is dominated by small-scale farming (90% of farms have less than five hectares). The Matabeleland South – falls within Agro-Ecological Regions (AERs) IV and V, which have the lowest agricultural potential in terms of quality and quantity of rainfall. These regions are also characterized by poor soils, low levels of irrigation infrastructure with very limited access to irrigation – only about 10,000 ha out of the 180,000 ha of irrigated land in southern Zimbabwe are found on commercial lands**. The remaining farmers are dependent on rain-fed agriculture**.

## 16. These regions also have a high proportion of food-insecure households and poor agricultural infrastructure, making rain-fed maize production – the primary staple - a significant challenge**. With increasing climate risks (see below), water is the key limiting factor for agricultural productivity in these areas. Climate change has led to a decrease in rainfall intensity especially in Matabeleland South where rainfall in AR is increasingly variable, characterized by erratic and unpredictable rains (short sharp, isolated showers). Crop yields are extremely low, and the rate of crop failure is increasing to one in three years. The effects of climate-induced droughts, exemplified by the 2015/16 season, have been devastating with over 3 million people requiring food aid.

## 17. According to the 2015 Zimstats statistics, the highest proportion of food insecure households at peak hunger period can be found in Mashonaland West (Mashonaland West, Masvingo and Midlands) provinces**. Zimbabwe spends an average of US$200 million on food relief every year, with expenditures rising to USD 50 million in 2016 when a 3.5 million food-insecure people were assisted as a direct result of the drought. The country is also increasingly vulnerable to climate change and its impacts due to increasing variability and changing climatic conditions. The increasing growth and strength of climate hazards have significant implications for household food security, especially in the most vulnerable communities in southern Zimbabwe.

## Key Government Strategies on National Climate Change Response

### 15. The Zimbabwe Government has established a five-year economic plan (2013-2018) called “the Zimbabwe Agenda for Sustainable Socio-Economic Transformation (ZimAsset)**. The plan’s vision is to move towards an empowered society and a growing economy that provides decent jobs, affordable housing, access to quality education and health services, and social transformation to the people of Zimbabwe**. ZimAsset is an integrated plan with four clusters: a) Food Security and Nutrition; b) Social Services and Human Capital Development; c) Economic Transformation; d) Environmental Sustainability. The following points are directly relevant to the agricultural sector: “ii) Revitalizing agriculture and the agro-processing value chains”; b) Advancing Beneficiation

# C2. Project / Programme Objective against Baseline

## Climate Vulnerability Box**4**

### (a) Retrospective Analysis of Historical Data

#### Changes in historical data measured at stations and projected model changes of the future can be used* (see TS section 1.6.2):

- **Daily minimum and maximum temperatures have risen by 2.9°C and 2.2°C respectively over the last century**;
- **Historical temperature trends show that increases in daily minimum and maximum temperatures are occurring more rapidly than maximum temperatures**;
- The period from 1980 to 2005 has been the warmest on record since October 2015—February 2016, with a warming rate of 3.4°C per century (compared to 1971–2000);
- Projections anticipate further warming rates of 0.5°C to 2°C in the 2020s, 1.1–1.5°C by 2030 and 3–4°C by 2100 (compared to the 1961–1990 baseline), assuming an AZ greenhouse gas emissions pathway**;
- This is consistent with the World Bank Climate Change Knowledge Portal for Development Practitioners and Policy Makers, which projects a warming rate of between 0.5°C per decade in 2030 and 2050**, all of which suggest a warming rate of 0.2°C per decade to over 0.5°C per century.

#### Historical trends and future projections suggest that increases in temperature are accompanied by increases in potential evapotranspiration, leading to increased water stress for the region;

- Temperature data is available for fewer weather stations in southern Zimbabwe (5 stations as opposed to 23 stations recording rainfall), highlighting the need to improve weather and climate monitoring infrastructure in the region.

- Later onset of the rains, increases in the frequency and intensity of heavy rainfall events (decreases in low intensity rainfall events), and an increase in the proportion of low rainfall years.[10]
- Over the next decade, there is a high probability of significant permanent reductions in annual rainfall (particularly during JJA, SON (beginning of rainfall season) and MAM (end of rainfall season)), which indicate shorter rainfall seasons on average.
- Increases in maximum consecutive dry days and average rainfall on a rainy day indicate shorter rainfall seasons and less rainfall days, though with greater intensity (which is seen in increases in annual maximum daily rainfall see [1] pages 24-25).
- Indices of rainfall - PET + FEI are progressively more negative since 1971, indicating that the potential contribution of rainfall to water resources has been progressively reduced.
- Rainfall is predicted to continue to decline in all seasons (except DJF) in the future, with more consistency between model projections and observed data.
- Mean Annual Precipitation will decline, mostly in the southern Zimbabwean catchments, by up to 16% by 2050, whether or not climate change is considered. The most affected catchments are those in the south of Zimbabwe (Mongwane, Runde catchments) where MAP could decline to as low as 700 mm.
- In southern Zimbabwe, future projections suggest increases in actual evapotranspiration during the whole year which decreases water availability and increases runoff variability. This is expected to be more variable with greater changes in the extreme percentiles than the mean annual data[12].
- In northern Zimbabwe, future projections show a decrease in rainfall particularly during the winter and early rainfall season (SON). Maximum discharges (proxy for flooding) will increase (see Fig 3.10), while minimum discharges are expected to decline by up to 50%.
- The most affected catchments are those in the north of Zimbabwe (Mongwane, Runde, Sanyati and Save) where MAP is expected to decrease by more than 20%. This region also has high levels of vulnerability with regard to water resources. In this region, rainfall patterns are tending to change, occurring later in the year, with heavier bursts of rainfall over a shorter time period and with longer non-rainfall periods.
- The following trends are predicted per targeted river basin catchment (see Appendix 4 of the F5 for a detailed analysis of the impacts of climate change on water resources in Southern Zimbabwe).
    - By 2050, Mean Annual Precipitation (MAP) is predicted to decrease in all catchments, except for Manyame where it could increase by up to 16%.
    - The most affected catchments are also in the south of Zimbabwe (Mongwane, Runde catchments), where MAP could decline to as low as 700 mm and PET + FEI will be more negative than observed data.
    - MAP would continue to decline in Gweve, Mongwane, Runde, Sanyati and Save between 2030 and 2080 if a 'business as usual' emissions scenario is considered.
- Decrease in the mean annual precipitation at 15% in Mongwane catchment by 2050 under the business as usual case.

## (ii) Extreme climate events
Extreme events have historically affected Zimbabwe adversely, predominantly in the form of droughts, mid-season dry spells (leading to agricultural losses), floods and tropical storms (cyclones). The Zimbabwean Building Information System (ZBIS) has identified 10 hazards that are likely to increase under climate change. It is noted that southern areas of Zimbabwe, are highly prone to extreme climate events including drought. This region also has high levels of other determinants of vulnerability, such as HIV/AIDS rates, crop yields and livestock diseases. Coupled with climate projections that


---
**Page 16**
---

predicts increases in the frequency and intensity of drought and extreme rainfall, makes southern areas highly vulnerable**[1]**. Furthermore, from the above records of both observed station data [and associated trends], as well as the CMIP5 projections and simulation results, it is clear that climate change will exacerbate these issues. The impacts of climate change on Zimbabwe have already started to exacerbate these risks and will continue to do so, even without increases in vulnerability due to poverty and health. Droughts are a recurring feature of life for many people living in southern Africa, particularly in the countries of Botswana, Namibia, South Africa, and Zimbabwe. In 2017, drought hit southern areas. Drought has caused six of the ten worst droughts between 1991 and 2019**[3]**. Mid-season dry spells are defined as prolonged periods with little or no rainfall during the growing season (October to March). These mid-season dry spells pose severe threats to crops that are heavily dependent on rain and without access to irrigation, as during these episodes crops are subject to water stress and can be severely damaged by heat stress**[4]**. Clearly southern areas of Zimbabwe, extending to the north-east and west are most at risk of droughts and mid-season dry spells.

20. Flooding occurs in Zimbabwe during the summer months (December to March) when the country receives most of its rain. The most severe floods tend to be experienced in the southern and northern low-lying areas of the country**[5]**. Both the observed increase in rainfall intensity and projected increases in extreme precipitation events will exacerbate this problem, as well as the flooding risk now increasing and will continue to do so in the future. Furthermore, flooding is often associated with tropical cyclones from the Indian Ocean. The 2013 Cyclone Idai was one of the most devastating tropical cyclones ever recorded on the Mozambique coast. In 2007 and 2008 widespread flooding in Zimbabwe (as well as other Southern African countries), resulted in the loss of lives and livestock, damage to infrastructure, and displacement of people from their homes. The floods were estimated at US$153–469 million of humanitarian assistance. It is estimated that nearly 50,000 farming households were affected and a 1.8 million hectares of arable land were destroyed. Road infrastructure was destroyed, which disrupted market access. Damage to livestock, crops, food stocks and agricultural inputs was estimated at US$23–46 million. The total economic damage from the floods in Zimbabwe was estimated at US$557-767 million. For Cyclone Idai, the RNAH notes that almost 120 mm of rain fell over a period of three days (May 19 to May 21). This is more than double the average rainfall for this time of year in Zimbabwe and is expected to occur under climate change), which prevented the torrential rainfall of Cyclone Idai from being absorbed. This exacerbated the run-off and worsened flash floods.

20. Southern Zimbabwe, including the agricultural provinces of Manicaland, Matabeleland North, as projected to experience more extreme heat impacts than the rest of the country**, while also being home to the majority of vulnerable rural populations. Rainfall is predicted to decrease by 15% and runoff by 20% in these areas. As highlighted in a recent report from the World Bank, Zimbabwe’s agricultural sector is highly dependent on rainfall for irrigation and livestock production, and thus increases water stress and the vulnerability of irrigation and rain-fed farming communities in southern Zimbabwe**[6]**. Agro-Ecological Regions (AER) IV and V, which dominate these provinces, are characterized by low rainfall and poor soil quality.

**Impacts of CC on agriculture and farming livelihoods in southern Zimbabwe**

22. The key climate change risks are increasing temperatures, more variable and extreme precipitation, increasing aridity, and the intensification of droughts and floods. All of these changes in climatic patterns have led to reduced crop-yield livestock outputs, which has a negative impact on Zimbabwe’s agrarian-based economy. Increasing temperature, coupled with reduced rainfall and increased evapotranspiration, lead to increased aridity, the expansion of marginal lands and decreasing water availability. These climate change risks are expected to increase in intensity over time. The impacts are projected to cause changes to the growing season and crop patterns, having significant implications to yields and national revenues. Increased temperature is also likely to increase the incidence of pests and diseases, including livestock diseases such as those of diseases, as well as rangeland productivity, which have particular implications on livestock production systems. These climate change impacts are expected to reduce the resilience of crop and livestock systems; threaten agricultural based livelihoods and place further strain on already scarce water resources.

23. Increasing climatic variability has year-on-year reduced three million of the rural farming population food and water insecurity. This study examines the impact of climate change on Zimbabwe’s agriculture sector, with a focus on smallholder irrigation schemes and rain-fed farming systems reveal that maize yields have dropped from an average 5.09t/ha to as low as 0.87t/ha and 13.0t/ha, respectively, as a result of climate-induced moisture deficit and temperature stress. Rainfed agriculture is the dominant form of production in Zimbabwe’s agricultural sector. Maize is extremely susceptible to changes in water availability because of its high yield sensitivity to water stress. Analysis by Nkunda et al., (2015) shows that maize yields are likely to decline by 34% under climate change, even with current drought tolerant varieties. Increasing temperatures and rainfall variability, leading to increasing evapotranspiration, will reduce the amount of water available for crops in Zimbabwe’s agricultural conditions. This has direct consequences for the food and income security of over 3 million people (or 702,800 households).

24. The impact of climate change on crop water requirements, water availability (river runoff), irrigation demand, irrigation potential and yield is likely to be significant in Zimbabwe’s agricultural sector. In general, maize yields are expected to decline as water requirements will increase, whilst water availability will decrease 40%. Irrigation demand is expected to increase 5-20% for most crops, which can be satisfied through increases in runoff except at the end of the dry season (see Fig. F13.).

factor on rain-fed agriculture yield potential[28]. The results indicate that there is a consistently significant negative linear relationship between the number of dry dekads and average maize yield for the four consecutive wet seasons considered in the study (from 2005 to 2009). This implies that as the frequency of drought increases, so does the risk of crop failure. In addition, the drought-related stress that the crops experience, results in poor crop yields. This implies that the vast majority of farmers practicing rain-fed agriculture are at high risk of crop failure and food insecurity during periods of drought. The number of mid-season dry spells are projected to increase in frequency and intensity in southern, semi-arid and areas under climate change[29].

Zimbabwe suffers from both physical and economic water scarcity with intensified climate change. Physical water scarcity refers to the availability of water for irrigation and domestic use while economic water scarcity is the inability of farmers to develop water resources[30]. Under climate change, less water will be available either as green water – the fraction of water that infiltrates into soil and becomes available for plant uptake or blue water – surface water that can be used directly for runoff or indirectly through deep drainage to groundwater that eventually flows to rivers in base flow, as well as water stored in dams and reservoirs[31]. This will have a significant impact on irrigation[32]. Groundwater will be most affected in the southern catchments of Mzingwane and Runde and least affected in the northern catchments.

28. The magnitude of the percentage negative change in irrigation potential will be the same for all basins irrespective of the crops grown[3]. However, the irrigation potential may be reduced to as low as this[4] if measures improving water use efficiency, in light of the climate change risks, are not implemented[5]. Climate change-induced water stress - in the absence of soil moisture conservation practices under rain-fed agriculture or climate-resilient irrigation infrastructure - forces rural farmers to reduce their crop yields and income. The Zimbabwe Irrigation Development Authority (ZIDA) has reported that the benefits from years of investment in smallholder rural irrigation development intended to boost food production have not been realized due to poor water management, lack of maintenance, inadequate institutional capacity, poor governance and situation on rivers and dams, thereby reducing water storage capacity and damaging or destroying irrigation infrastructure.

29. Livestock have traditionally provided a major source of insurance against seasonal climate-induced crop failures for rural households in Zimbabwe[6]. However, the livestock sector has also been negatively affected by climate change over recent years. However, with increasing intensity of droughts, this traditional coping strategy may no longer suffice in future. Lack of livestock feed and water will result in reduced livestock productivity and increased mortality rates.

30. The 2015 Zimbabwe National Climate Change Communication to the UNFCCC indicates that projected decreases in precipitation, coupled with increases in temperature, will cause a corresponding decrease in net Primary Productivity of grazing lands, which will reduce livestock productivity in Zimbabwe[7]. Severe droughts and floods have also been experienced across the country. For example, between 2014 and 2015, the South province alone lost over 32,000 cattle. In 2015, El Niño caused a loss of over 15,000 cattle in three provinces in southern Zimbabwe. Periodic disease outbreaks are also exacerbated by climate change and pose a significant risk to livestock production.

**Page 20**
---

30. Disproportionate impacts of climate change on women smallholders: In Zimbabwe, women are the majority of the smallholder farming population due to a number of factors, including climate-induced migration of men. Women, as important food producers and processors, are more vulnerable than men in terms of access to resources such as land, water, and inputs. This is due to productive assets and resources relative to their male counterparts. Women are exposed to gender-specific vulnerabilities given their unequal access to resources.

31. These gender differences in property rights and access to information, together with their cultural, social and economic roles make women more susceptible to climate change impacts. Recurring drought and low rainfall patterns negatively impact water supplies for irrigation and livestock grazing. Women are also more vulnerable than men because they are disproportionately marginalized in terms of adaptation strategies to climate change, especially women and child-headed households who lack access to credit facilities and extension services. The 2013 Zimbabwe Demographic Health Survey (ZDHS) indicates that women experience exacerbations incidences of tropical diseases such as malaria, typhoid and cholera, which impacts women more due to limited access to medical services and their reproductive role, including caring for the sick.

**Baseline efforts and challenges**

32. The Government of Zimbabwe (GoZ), with donor support, has been investing in agriculture and water supply to enhance food security and smallholder farmer livelihoods. In 2013, the GoZ developed the Zimbabwe Agricultural Investment Plan (ZAIP) for the period 2014-2017. The ZAIP is a multi-sectoral plan that seeks to mobilize resources from both domestic and international partners' investment in the agriculture sector with the aim of enhancing achievement of the objectives of the Zimbabwe Comprehensive Agriculture Policy Framework (2012-2016), Food and Nutrition Security Policy (FNSP) (2012)and Zimbabwe Agenda for Sustainable Development (ZIM-Agenda 2030). The ZAIP seeks to achieve this through a number of interventions including mechanized agricultural production, application of agricultural technologies and practices, including infield and off-site rainwater harvesting, irrigation development, and the promotion of climate-resilient crop varieties. Investments are also being made to strengthen research on small grains and drought-tolerant maize seed varieties in cooperation between the Zimbabwe Agricultural Research Institute (ZARI), the International Maize and Wheat Improvement Center (CIMMYT) and the International Maize and Wheat Improvement Center (CIMMYT). In recent years, the focus has shifted to promoting sustainable approaches, such as CRA, building on Conservation Agriculture (CA) practices and interventions.

33. Government budget allocations for irrigation development have continued to increase over time. The GoZ has continued to invest in stabilizing and enhancing water supply and security, of which irrigation forms a primary component, whether in relation to construction or new schemes or rehabilitation of existing ones. For example, the 2017-2018 budget allocation for the Water Development Programme (WDP) is US$35 million, compared to US$6.4 million in 2016 and US$9.5 million in 2015. Government budget allocations for smallholder irrigation for fiscal years 2013-2017 amounted to US$1.6 million. Priority was given to the rehabilitation of existing schemes (e.g., Matabeleland North, Masvingo and Manicaland). GoZ is currently investing US$ 2.5 million annually in the proposed project areas of southern Zimbabwe (provinces of Mashonaland West and Mashonaland South) for development of smallholder irrigation infrastructure and ongoing rehabilitation.

34. A series of other agricultural support programs, backed by donors such as the Swiss Agency for Development Cooperation, World Meteorological Organization (WMO), DFID, the United Nations, the International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD), and the Japan International Cooperation Agency (JICA), are rehabilitating, modernizing and, in some cases, constructing new irrigation schemes. The rehabilitation of existing schemes has been a priority as it is more cost-effective than construction. Priorities given to rehabilitation has been either repair the physical infrastructure, including the conveyance and distribution systems, to original design specifications, or to undertake modernization of the irrigation scheme by installing more water-efficient irrigation systems, such as sprinkler and drip systems, as reported in Matabeleland and Mashonaland South provinces. The 10 Smallholder Irrigation


---
**Page 21**

Support Programme includes a training component, and the International Fund for Agriculture Development focuses on capacity building of extension service providers, local youth, and businesses in irrigation service provision. The private sector and NGOs are also implementing projects to improve access to water resources through rehabilitation of existing infrastructure such as extension services, mechanization of smallholder farms, irrigation maintenance and rehabilitation, and market development. Investment in irrigation infrastructure is also being made by the government (e.g., the Irrigation Development Project in Zimbabwe [Stichting Nederlandse Vrienden], Foundation of Netherlands Volunteers), and farm mechanization support under the Irrigation Development Project.

Despite the significant development investments highlighted above, there has been inadequate attention to climate change-related impacts, and the related gender dimensions of climate vulnerability, in the design and implementation of baseline measures. For example, while irrigation infrastructure can provide a buffer against droughts, it is not necessarily resilient enough to prevent cold-induced impairment from flooding caused by extreme weather events, composed mainly of soil erosion from conventional farming practices. As climate change increases the frequency and intensity of such extreme weather events, it is increasing the burden on GoZ, as development gains are repeatedly jeopardized by these same events (e.g., in droughts and floods). In turn, these impacts further exacerbate the vulnerability of the poor and extremely poor rural smallholder populations. There is limited evidence addressing specific climate change-related risks, both observed and projected, through incremental or full adaptation solutions.

Adaptation Solution

36. With continued intensification of climate variability and change, Zimbabwe’s current coping strategies for the agriculture and water sectors are becoming increasingly ineffective, requiring essential adaptation investments to achieve lasting climate resilience at scale among vulnerable rural farming households. A paradigm shift in addressing the adaptation needs of smallholder farmers in southern Africa is required if these communities are to be resilient to future climate change impacts. The evidence suggests that it will not be possible for individual farmers or even small groups of farmers to adapt successfully to such scale. These technical, institutional, economic, agronomic and capacity factors form a dynamic interactive system which can only be addressed by implementing a multi-stakeholder, market-driven approach to climate adaptation of vulnerable smallholder agriculturalists.

37. The Feasibility Study, carried out in preparation of this project, identified specific requirements to be met in order to reach sustainability at scale for the proposed interventions. These include: (a) access to water resources and irrigation infrastructure; (b) outputs required to overcome these barriers [in particular, see sections 4 and 5 of the FS]. These refer primarily to access to water in sufficient quantity and quality, as well as to other inputs such as seeds, fertilizers, tools, etc. The project will support improved agricultural practices, improved stakeholder coordination and access to markets for climate-resilient crops, and an accessible supply of actionable climate information so that smallholders can manage climate risk.

38. Adaptation at scale requires a multi-stakeholder approach which requires resources and capacities for adaptive management of their agro-ecosystems with the aim of stabilizing, increasing and sustaining agricultural yields and incomes. In areas that are becoming drier and hotter, these resources include access to, as a priority, sufficient, dependable water and a diversity of climate-resilient crops; in areas where rainfall is more variable, they also include soil moisture management practices and stable crop yield by allowing multiple cropping year-round. Irrigated crop production enhances farm income from sale of surplus produce and provides an alternative livelihood option for smallholder farmers. However, the agricultural sector in Zimbabwe is particularly vulnerable to climate change, and smallholders need to manage available soil moisture efficiently, harvest and store rainfall as well as diversify their crops. In addition, they will require information on weather patterns and climate risks, including crop diversification. In areas where traditional staples are under pressure from the pace of evolving climate change, diversification and accompanying investments in new technologies for climate-resilient agriculture are crucial to transformational adaptation outcomes. Finally, farmers will also benefit from access to reliable and up-to-date information on weather, climate and hydrological resources which will allow them to plan agricultural tasks and manage crops, soil and water resources more effectively.

39. The current preferred staple is maize. Maize is becoming increasingly unsuitable for rainfed/dryland farming in light of increasing temperatures and greater variability of rainfall – higher temperatures affect seed set, and maize is generally not as drought resistant as other crops. For dryland/rainfed farmers, the project is promoting small grains (e.g., millet, sorghum)

### Smallholders' Limited Institutional Capacities and Technical Knowledge for Farmers to adopt their Production practices to climate-driven droughts and floods

#### Barriers and Gaps

1. **Limited institutional capacities and technical knowledge** for farmers to adopt their production practices to climate-driven droughts and floods.

2. **Smallholders**, especially rain-fed farmers, face increasing risks linked to productivity and yield stability of their dryland agroecosystems. However, they are generally unaware of climate-resilient agricultural production practices or cropping systems for drylands. They continue to rely on traditional farming systems that are not well adapted to the changing climate.

3. **Smallholders prioritize food security above all else**, though this has been historically linked with low productivity and yield stability in dryland agroecosystems, especially when compared to sorghum and millet. In farm production is still largely based on traditional crop management, despite the need for climate-resilient practices such as intercropping systems, which mitigated risk of drought and floods. Small grains like maize are widely known (e.g., wheat) but have limited market access due to insufficient generating sufficient revenue to sustain food year-round yield. Small grains, though well-known and traditionally grown in hotter, drier areas, suffer from low yields under climate change.

4. **Institutional services providers** such as AGRETA, the agricultural extension agency or DDUVS/DVS have limited knowledge of climate-resilient practices for smallholder farmers at the district level and smaller community level. The service providers lack the technical capacity to provide relevant advice to smallholders on appropriate climate-resilient practices.

5. **Limited access** to reliable climate information, early warning and climate risk-informed disaster planning and climate-resilient agricultural advice (please see the Feasibility Study for analysis of institutional capacities based on JMPR assessments). Although Government has already begun efforts to develop a national climate change adaptation strategy, the implementation is still in progress. The extension service platforms are still weak. The pluralist nature of technical assistance services, with many entities (Government, NGOs, farmer associations, local and international research institutions, private sector and church organizations) emerging as

## Limited access to credit, insurance, and inputs makes smallholders shift away from subsistence to climate-resilient agricultural livelihoods

### Smallerholder farmers are less adapted to adopt sustainable agricultural practices by the project of improved food security from livestock profits and the potential for larger incomes from the sale of any surplus, which can then be used to purchase additional needed foodstuffs as required. Smallerholders are, however, unable to take advantage of market incentives for adopting climate-resilient agricultural systems because they lack access to credit, insurance, and inputs. This is especially true in crop and livestock breeds they. These farmers few market incentives for diversification towards climate-resilient agricultural systems given their insufficient integration into agricultural value chains, characterized by weak market linkages, little understanding of alternative markets, and limited access to information on market opportunities. The lack of credit, insurance, and inputs enable partnerships for scaling up. Weak market linkages limit commercialization and market development for climate-resilient staple and non-staple crops as well as livestock. ### Project Objectives, Outcomes, Impacts

The key problem the project proposes to address is the threat to agricultural livelihoods of vulnerable smallholders in southern Zimbabwe, especially women, from impacts of climate change-induced water scarcity.

#### Project Objective:
50. The objective of the proposed project is to support the Government of Zimbabwe in strengthening the resilience of agricultural livelihoods of vulnerable smallholder farmers, particularly women, through improved access to information and services that reduce risks and enhance adaptive capacity.

#### Project Outcomes:
- Increased resilience of vulnerable people and communities
- Improved health and well-being of vulnerable people

#### Project Impacts:
- Strengthened adaptive capacity
- Reduced exposure to climate risks for vulnerable smallholder farmers, especially women, in southern Zimbabwe

# Project Outcome: Strengthened resilience of agricultural livelihoods of vulnerable communities, particularly women

## Output 1:
- **Outcome:** Increased access to alternative water sources and efficient water resource management.
- **Impact:** Enhanced water security in the face of climate change.

## Output 2:
- **Outcome:** Improved agricultural practices for diversified crop systems adaptation.
- **Impact:** Enhanced food production and income generation through climate-resilient agricultural practices.

## Output 3:
- **Outcome:** Strengthened capacity to adapt and respond to climate-related disasters.
- **Impact:** Increased resilience of the most vulnerable in the region.

### Resilience-enhancing measures
- Soil management to maintain or augment soil fertility, low input systems, shade soils, mulching, composting, heat tolerant crops, crop rotation for crop diversification, heat-tolerant breeds and varieties, short and early forage systems.
- Increase in evapotranspiration.
- Rain fall: Dams to maintain or increase water availability; agroforestry to provide shade with high water use efficient crops; short, heat-resistant crops.

# Theory of Change

## Barrier #1: Smallholder farmers face increasing water stress and reduced soil moisture due to climate change, leading to yield stability of their dryland rainfed agro-ecosystems.

### The project will:
- **Improve the capacities** of smallholder farmers, particularly women, to enhance agricultural resilience and productivity by managing rainfall and soil moisture efficiently and reducing or potentially eliminating water stress during dry spells.
- **Train rain-fed farmers** to manage soil, water, and biomass to maximize water availability through in-field or off-site water harvesting and to enhance productivity of water through the use of water-efficient technologies such as drip irrigation.

## Barrier #1a: Smallholder farmers do not have access to sufficient, reliable water resources and technical knowledge for climate change adaptation of their agro-ecosystems.

### The project will:
- **Train rain-fed farmers** to manage soil, water, and biomass to maximize water availability through in-field or off-site water harvesting and to enhance productivity of water through the use of water-efficient technologies such as drip irrigation.
- **Extend smallholder knowledge** through organized farmer-to-farmer exchanges.

## Barrier #2: Smallholder farmers lack technical and organizational capacity to plan, manage, and operate irrigation schemes efficiently and effectively

### The project will:
- **Implement climate-proof 21 community-based irrigation schemes;**
- **Train smallholder Irrigation Management Committees in water harvesting, management, and operation of irrigation infrastructure and equipment.**

## At the same time, the project will counteract climate-driven declines in agricultural productivity and yield stability in agro-ecosystems across southern Zimbabwe through technical assistance to the smallholders (organized in FPs) to apply resilience-enhancing practices and technologies that are adapted to local conditions and can be scaled up with minimal external inputs. The project will also support smallholder farmers to adapt their farming systems to climate-driven changes by providing them with access to information on innovations changing conditions. The project will promote recovery of traditional knowledge of cropping systems and seed varieties, as well as the use of improved seeds and other technologies. Training will be provided in participatory evaluation of CRA practices and other methods and techniques. Training will also build organizational, financial and business development skills, with a special focus on empowering women's groups in the development of small businesses.


---
**Page 27**
---

54. The project will overcome the barrier (Barrier #2, above) to adequate investment in climate-proof irrigation by revitalizing and climate-proofing 21 community-based irrigation schemes and training smallholder Irrigation Management Committees in their effective operation and maintenance. This will ensure that smallholder farmers, particularly women farmers who are often the water users themselves, especially women, are able to access secure, reliable sources of water through the climate proofing of their irrigation schemes. The project will also establish multi-stakeholder Innovation Platforms (IPs) for building and coordinating ongoing public and private sector support to enhance the capacity of smallholder farmers in efficient and climate-resilient soil and water management practices.

**Barrier #3:** Smallholders are unable to take advantage of market incentives for adopting climate adaptation inputs and practices, including improved seeds, drought-tolerant agricultural practices, and climate-resilient crop and livestock breeds. The project will:

- establish multi-stakeholder Innovation Platforms to build and coordinate ongoing public and private sector support to enhance the capacity of smallholder farmers in efficient and climate-resilient soil and water management practices.

55. In overcoming limited access to knowledge, markets and value chains (Barrier #3) that would enable a transformative shift from subsistence to climate-resilient agricultural livelihoods, the project establishes multi-stakeholder Innovation Platforms to build and coordinate ongoing public and private sector support to enhance the capacity of smallholder farmers in efficient and climate-resilient soil and water management practices. The platforms will provide a forum for more effective communication, strategy development, planning, access to finance, and partnership development. They will also facilitate knowledge generation and sharing of best practices and lessons learned for climate-risk informed water resource management and agricultural planning. Lessons from the project will be disseminated through the Southern Zimbabwe School. Experiences under the project will be systematized, codified and disseminated to smallholder farmers throughout southern Zimbabwe, as well as in a multi-sectoral upscaling platform at national level for potential policy analysis, dialogue and reform.

The project will:

- establish the network of hydro-meteorological stations in key catchments of southern Zimbabwe.
- train field staff to operate and maintain them.
- train field staff to operate and maintain them.
- establish and operate a system for development of new advisories and warnings for smallholder farmers, including their dissemination through the media, including SMS and radio.
- support the engagement of government, the private sector, Catchment and Sub-Catchment Management Committees and others to understand and utilize climate/weather/hydrological information products for managing climate-related risks.

*As appropriate, Innovation Platforms will adopt the approach outlined in the Operational Guide for Making Markets Work for the Poor Approach (MWP) (2015) prepared by the Swiss Agency for Development Cooperation (SDC) and the US Department for International Development (USAID).

**Page 28**
---

56\. Finally, the project will overcome the barrier (Barrier 84\above) effective to climate risk management through access to and use of climate information services. The project will provide a suite of climate information services that will strengthen the network of hydro-meteorological stations in key catchments of southern Zimbabwe and two flood stations to operate and maintain them, while also strengthening forecasting capacities of MSF staff\. The project will establish and operate a system for disseminating climate information through mobile phone text messages, radio broadcasts, television, newspapers, the media, including SMS and radio. The project will support smallholders, local governments, the private sector, Catchment and Sub-Catchment Management Committees (SCMCs), and other stakeholders in Zimbabwe to access and use climate information for managing climate-related risks\. These elements will support effective implementation by smallholders of CRA and water resource management in mitigating the risk of climate-driven ruralfarmer vulnerability through more detailed, comprehensible and timely information.

57\. Building on best practices and coordination with key initiators: the proposed project builds on and scales up best practices from several initiatives that have effectively demonstrated how to build resilience at household level by promoting resilient agriculture, and public-private-partnerships to enable market access for climate-resilient crops. Please refer to the Feasibility Study\. Annex II, for details on these efforts, including the following, in particular:

58\. Enhancing community-based adaptation: The project will support communities in Zimbabwe at increasing the capacity of communities to monetize development gains in the face of recurrent climate and non-climate related shocks and stresses. The fund provides a strong national platform for coordinated and synergistic resilience-building efforts in the country, bringing together key stakeholders in the public sector, civil society organizations, private sector, and international partners under the Zimbabwe Strategic Resilience Framework; and, furthermore, the ZRBF investments, especially in value-chain and market development, present an opportunity to scale up existing initiatives that have demonstrated how to build resilience at household level. The fund contributions (EUR 205.5 million, GfID Pounds Sterling 21.5 million, Sida USD 8.0 million), as well as future contributions to the Fund\. The project proposed here will operate under a coordinated implementation structure with ZRBF, as detailed in Section C-7.

59\. The Climate-resilient infrastructure Development Facility (CRIDF) funded by DFID, focuses on working with organizations to show them how to integrate climate change adaptation into their work. CRIDF works with partners to identify and disseminate proven techniques and work processes to local engineers, masons, puppeteers and others\. By sharing technical expertise with local engineers, CRIDF helps to build the capacity of these groups to design and construct climate-resilient infrastructure that empowers marginalized groups\. The proposed project was developed in technical partnership with CRIDF and draws on CRIDF's experience with development of water infrastructure, local capacity development and climate-proofing in pilot projects in southern Africa. The CRIDF approach will be used as a guide for the design of the project. The approach to water management for sustained access to water will be based on the tried-and-tested, CRIDF approach to new and existing irrigation schemes in Zimbabwe.

60\. The UNDP project Coping with Drought and Climate Change (2008-2012) demonstrated a range of gender-sensitive approaches to adaptation to climate change among rural agricultural communities in vulnerable areas of Chiredzi District as a national pilot. The project focused on the following key areas: water management, soil conservation, crop diversification, livestock management, irrigation systems, livelihood enhancement and community-based NRM\. These experiences led to the UNDP/UNICEF Scaling Up Adaptation approach (2014-2018) which combined climate-proofing of agricultural

The ZRBF is designed around three strategic pillars: 5. application of evidence in policy making for resilience building, ii\. grants for climate-resilient infrastructure development, iii\. capacity building and technical assistance to support the implementation of resilience building projects in the most vulnerable areas; and iv\. timely and cost-effective response to emergencies to safeguard development gains made by smallholder farmers and other vulnerable groups. The ZRBF will provide a national platform for information sharing and knowledge management, including climate risk assessment, disaster risk management, weather information, market linkages and engagement of district level stakeholders to improve service delivery. The ZRBF will also support the implementation of the Zimbabwe Strategic Resilience Framework (ZSRF) which is based on informing policy as well as resilience building interventions\. Interventions and target areas are based on mappings of current and historic climate and non-climate related hazards and risks affecting rural households

### C.3. Project / Programme Description


---
**Page 30**
---

61. The objective of the proposed project is to strengthen resilience of agricultural livelihoods of vulnerable communities, particularly women, in southern Zimbabwe in the face of increasing climate risks and impacts through the following outputs:

(i) Improved access to climate-resilient seeds, improved irrigation systems, and better soil moisture management

(ii) Scaled up climate-resilient agricultural production and diversification through increased access to climate-resilient inputs, practices, and markets; and

(iii) Improved weather monitoring and hydrological information for climate-resilient agriculture.

62. Consistent with the above Outputs, climate impacts will be addressed with the following measures:

63. For the projected rise in temperature the project will promote soil management to maintain or augment soil moisture; agroforestry systems to shade soils; crop diversification towards heat-tolerant crops; heat-tolerant breeds and varieties of pasture species; cut-and-carry forage systems; revitalisation of irrigation schemes to enhance water availability to counteract increased evapotranspiration rates, and drip irrigation; market incentives for crop diversification and climate-resilient climate risk management.

64. For the projected increase in rainfall variability the project will work with farmers on rain-fed (dryland) and the few who have access to irrigation systems to promote soil moisture, agroforestry systems to shade soils, crop diversification towards drought-tolerant crops; more efficient irrigation systems through provision of equipment (sprinklers, drip systems); market incentives for crop diversification and climate-resilient climate risk management.

65. For the projected increase in intensity and frequency of extreme events the project will climate-proof irrigation systems against flooding dams; promote on-farm soil conservation measures; and provide access to agro-climate information for climate risk management.

Project geographical location and beneficiaries

66. The proposed project will be implemented in 137 wards in 15 districts of southern Zimbabwe in the semi-arid Eco-Regional Areas (ERAs) IV and V of the provinces of Manicaland, Masvingo and Matabeleland South (See Figure 1-2 below). Project geographical location is based on the following criteria: i) areas with high vulnerability to climate change; ii) areas with high rainfall variability; iii) areas with high rainfall intensity; iv) areas with high frequency of extreme events; v) areas with high food insecurity; vi) areas with high poverty levels; vii) areas with high prevalence of malnutrition; viii) areas with high prevalence of HIV/AIDS; ix) areas with high prevalence of malaria; x) areas with high prevalence of waterborne diseases; xi) areas with high prevalence of vector-borne diseases; xii) areas with high prevalence of respiratory diseases; xiii) areas with high prevalence of non-communicable diseases; and xiv) areas with high prevalence of mental health disorders. The project will be implemented in 137 wards in 15 districts, covering a total population of approximately 2.8 million people (of which 60% are women). Of these, 49% live below the poverty line; 20% have access to piped water and sanitation facilities; 20% have access to electricity; 37% have access to health services; 15% have access to education; and 8% have access to financial services. The project will be implemented in 49 wards (covering a total population of approximately 600,000 people) in the three southern provinces of Manicaland, Masvingo and Matabeleland South.

67. AFR II and IV are characterised by new rainfall and significant exposure to climate risks as depicted in past current and projected scenarios. These regions suffer from persistent high food deficits, the highest number of drought-related livestock deaths, high food prices in lean seasons and, in specific areas, a high risk of climate-induced flooding. The selected project area is predicted to offer opportunities for diversification into more climate-resilient crops such as sorghum, pearl millet and pigeon pea which are better suited to the climate risks faced by vulnerable communities there. In line with this identification, the Climate Resilience Study Sub-assessment - informing the Feasibility Study - underlines an analysis of drought scenarios in this area and employed a selection approach based on vulnerability and exposure to climate change (see Figure 1-2 below). The project will be implemented in 30 rural population locations.

68. The targeting exercise identified 15 priority districts in the southern provinces, comprising 136 wards. Of these total number of 137 climate vulnerable wards (determined to be priority areas, see Feasibility Study for more detail) have been specifically targeted for climate resilience investments. The Ministry of Environment, Water and Climate recommended focusing on contiguous

All consultations are documented in the Feasibility Study in Annex II, and detailed maps can also be found in Annex II to this proposal.

These 13 Districts and 137 Wards are: Maninjau Province, Bukit (13 wards), Chirang (15 wards); in Masinagdi Province, Mavinga (9 wards), Bika (4 wards), Zaka (14 wards) Chiru (9 wards), Chito (8 wards) Meenwa (5 wards); and in Malinthak Province, Kachan (20 wards), Lohit (37 wards), Tawang (16 wards), Tawang (16 wards), Uniteengtse (12 wards), Manger (9 wards).

## 70. Project Outputs and Activities described below are outlined in the Feasibility Study, in Section 5.1: Climate-proofing and Rehabilitation of Irrigation Schemes. These first outlines contain implementation priorities to achieve paradigm shifting, transformational change, and then provides detailed intervention and costing recommendations. Finally, the chapter outlines innovation, cost effectiveness and sustainability.

### Output 1: Increased access to water for agriculture through diversified irrigation systems and water resource management

#### 72. This Output addresses Barmera B2, B3, and B4 above. This output focuses on enabling vulnerable smallholder farmers, particularly women, to access sufficient, reliable water resources to address increasing climate risks to agricultural productivity.

#### 73. The project will also invest in revitalization and climate-proofing of 15 community-level irrigation schemes, including water delivery infrastructure and more efficient crop irrigation equipment. Climate-proofing will be based on the CREDIF project approach (Refer to Feasibility Study – Annex II – the irrigation sub-assignment report) to new and existing irrigation schemes. The focus is on climate-proofing of irrigation infrastructure in order to provide a resilient, sustainable water supply for smallholders in the face of increasing climate risks.

#### 74. Smallholders in each irrigation scheme will improve their technical and organizational capacities to plan, manage and operate their schemes in light of evolving climate risks through more effective Irrigation Management Committees (IMCs). Historical and projected climate data will be used by IMCs to develop a water management plan that supports adaptive planning and management of these investments by farmers and institutional stakeholders under this Output.

#### Activity 1.1: Climate-proofing irrigation infrastructure for enhanced water security in the face of climate change

Activity 2.1: Climate-proofing irrigation infrastructure for enhanced food security in the face of climate change

This activity aims at ensuring water availability, increasing water storage capacity, reducing water loss and enhancing water use efficiency through revitalization of existing irrigation schemes through climate-proofing and upgrading of irrigation infrastructure and water supply. The irrigation systems will be equipped with appropriate instruments for water purchase. It will also involve Irrigation Management Committees (IMCs) on climate-adapted O&M and monitoring, and establishment of O&M funds as well as facilitating the development of a Climate Change Adaptation Plan for the irrigation system. This project involves resilient irrigation systems. "Climate-proofing" under this project involves infrastructure improvements, landscaping/hoi-er fencing, solar pumping, and water-use efficient irrigation technologies (strip, sprinkler systems). SGP resources will cover over the cost of climate-proofing and other related costs such as training, capacity building, and the use of energy-efficient technology, while the GuC contribution will cover non-climate related costs, for instance, in the electrification of the schemes.

75. As such, this activity will provide sustained access to water for an estimated 5,300 smallholder farmers and their households (with a total population of about 21,846) in the three provinces. The project will also benefit over 900,000 people from the three southern provinces through upgrading of water provision equipment and infrastructure, together with more effective and efficient irrigation systems. This activity will be implemented by the Ministry of Water Resources and Irrigation. In response to existing irrigation schemes that overcome challenges faced by previous irrigation investments to sustainably increase sources of income for smallholder farmers in the face of climate change, this project will provide a cost-effective approach to existing irrigation schemes that overcome challenges faced by previous irrigation investments to sustainably increase sources of income for smallholder farmers in the face of climate change. This project will also reduce the frequency or duration of flooding. Climate-proofing techniques that protect irrigation and related infrastructure will be employed as a means to manage climate-induced flooding risk. Best practice in designing and implementing climate-proofing of irrigation systems is provided by the World Bank (2013). For more information on climate-proofing, please see the Climate Sub-Action in the Feasibility Study, flood prevention, identification and application of appropriate infrastructure, water management, and land use planning, including the use of integrated rural development and inclusion assessments, and political economy assessments. Methods of climate-proofing infrastructure against floods include river or watercourse bank reinforcement, levee-engineering, gabions, gravel/rocks, structure anchoring, use of sediment traps above flood levels of electrical, mechanical and other equipment, diversion and rerouting of existing water channels, land abstraction

### Climate-proofing irrigation schemes

#### 1.2 Field-based training and technology investments for farmers on rain-fed farmlands for climate-resilient water management

This activity includes the following sub-activities:

* **1.2.1 Climate-proofing and revitalizing existing irrigation infrastructure and equipment in 21 irrigation schemes (see Irrigation Sub-Acitivity 3.1)**
* **1.2.2 Training of 21 Irrigation Management Committees (IMCs) in climate-adapted O&M, monitoring, and establishment of O&M plans**

#### Activity 1.2: Field-based training and technology investments for farmers on rain-fed farmlands for climate-resilient water management

# Activity 2.1: Field-based training and technology investments for farmers on rain-fed farms for climate-resilient water management

## Rainfall variability/extreme events

### Enhance agricultural resilience by managing rainfall and soil moisture efficiently to reduce dry spells and potentially eliminate dry stress during dry spells of limited duration ("dry dekads")

#### Maximise penetration of water/reduce runoff and soil loss
- In-field water harvesting, including infiltration pits, contour cultivation, grass-stabilized bunds, no-till cultivation, mulching, agroforestry

#### Irrigation, where possible
- In-field water harvesting and storage systems, scheduling, etc.

#### Reduce impact of rainfall on soils, reduce soil temperatures and carbon loss
- Cover crops, agroforestry systems, mulching, no-till

### The project involves building the capacities of smallholder farmers, particularly women, to enhance agricultural resilience by managing rainfall and soil moisture efficiently, reducing dry spells and potentially eliminating dry stress during "dry dekads". Specifically, the project will run field-based training in water harvesting, soil moisture management techniques and water efficiency practices, procure and install technology-rich water resources management, and run participatory research approaches. The project will also provide hands-on training to farmers on the implementation of climate-resilient water resource management for rain-fed farms. An integrated approach to water resource management will be promoted that includes: maximizing rainfall capture and storage; optimizing use of water through efficient cropping practices and systems. Through hands-on training lead farmers will develop the skills needed to implement these techniques and technologies, including the construction of water harvesting structures and optimum use of water through efficient cropping practices and systems.

### The project will promote climate-resilience enhancing soil and water management techniques for in-field water harvesting, including infiltration pits, contour cultivation, grass-stabilized bunds, no-till cultivation, mulching, and agroforestry among other research-based technologies. These techniques will be demonstrated to farmers through hands-on training.

### The project ensures uptake of these new techniques and practices by using a participatory action-research approach in Farmer Field Schools (FFS) or through the development of demonstration plots that showcase the potential benefits of these techniques or practices to apply. This participatory approach builds ownership and commitment, self-confidence and stakeholder engagement. FFS is a tried-and-tested method for promoting adoption of new technologies and practices among smallholder farming systems.

### This project will effectively scale up these resource management techniques beyond FFS plots to smallholders' own fields across southern Zimbabwe. Note that this Activity is implemented in conjunction with Activity 2.2, below.


This activity includes the following sub-activities:


---
**Page 35**
---

1.2.3 Field-based training of 6,500 lead-rain-fed farmers in 230 Farmer Field Schools in rainwater harvesting, soil moisture management techniques and water efficiency practices

1.2.4 Procurement and installation by farmers of technologies to implement climate-resilient water-resource management in lead-farmers.

1.2.5 Participatory workshops and on-site assistance by lead farmers to facilitate farmer-to-farmer learning to scale up implementation of climate-resilient water resource management (Two open community learning days per FFS, under AGIRTEX supervision).

Output 2: Scale up climate resilient agricultural production and diversification through increased access to climate-resilient inputs, practices, and markets

80. This Output addresses Barriers #1, #3a, and #3b above. This output focuses on enabling vulnerable smallholder farmers, particularly women, on both rain-fed and irrigated farms, to strengthen their capacities to adopt and implement climate-resilient agricultural practices and technologies that will enable them to increase the resilience of their livelihoods.

81. To achieve this objective, a series of interventions were designed and implemented by GCF resources, along with co-financing from the Government of India (GoI) through its extension agency, to train lead farmers in Farmer Field Schools to analyze the climate vulnerability of their agro-ecosystems and identify relevant climate-resilient agricultural practices and technologies. These practices and technologies were then integrated into project preparation (see Annex IIb). These packages of CCA practices were identified during project design based on analysis and discussion with stakeholders, including farmers, extension workers, researchers, and other experts. The analysis considered climate vulnerability, market studies, previous project experience and best practices. This resulted in a mapping of crop and livestock potential for climate resilience throughout the region coupled with identification of wards with comparative climate vulnerability.

82. To ensure support to lead farmers in scaling up their adoption of climate-resilient agricultural practices, they will improve their technical capacities to implement selected CCA practices aimed at increasing yields and quality of specific staple and high-value crops, as well as livestock production. This will be achieved through on-farm demonstrations, training, and extension community under the supervision of the ward-level AGIRTEX extension workers.

83. To ensure support for long-term continued application/adaptation of climate-resilient agricultural practices and shifts towards market-oriented production systems, a series of interventions will be used to build the capacity of lead farmers and operationalize multi-stakeholder Innovation Platforms (IPs). These will be built in selected agricultural colleges and DRIBSS research stations. The IPs will provide a platform for the exchange of information on climate-resilient practices and development of the value chain of the selected climate resilient crops. Each IP will draw stakeholders from different value chains in 34 districts. The project will also support the establishment of an innovation network to facilitate knowledge sharing, technical support to establish linkages (e.g., through technical assistance/meeting/training), as well as civil society organizations and academic/research organisms with specialized expertise (e.g., ICRISAT). Selected staff members from AGIRTEX or DRIBSS will mentor farmers in the IPs. The IPs will also provide a platform for the exchange of information on climate-resilient practices.

84. Their IPs will complement and align with the BRFIP approach to market development and financial inclusion. Each module will include an IP that will be established at each FFS, which will serve as a focal point for farmers in the FFS to discuss how they can improve the market linkages in each value chain to enable access by vulnerable smallholder farmers, Smallholders, particularly women, as well as financially excluded groups. The IPs will also provide a platform for the exchange of information on climate-resilient practices and the project. These platforms will enable a transformative shift towards climate-resilient agricultural livelihoods for the smallholders with increased technical support from AGIRTEX, development of partnerships with private enterprises, greater coordination among value chain stakeholders and improved access to markets and finance.


---
**Page 36**
---

83. The project will generate extensive knowledge of water management, climate-resilient agriculture, and the development of climate-resilient value chains. Under this Output, experience with climate-resilient water resource management and agriculture innovation platforms will be synthesized into a common framework. The impact of climate change on crop yield patterns and water management will be analyzed and ultimately synthesized with conventional modern scientific methods and approaches to develop an integrated approach for climate-resilient agriculture.

**Activity 2.1: Establish transformative multi-stakeholder innovation platforms for diversified climate-resilient agriculture and markets**

84. It is expected that increased water security through Activities 1.1 and 1.2, and climate-resilient production techniques introduced in Activity 2.2, below, will enable crop diversification as well as production of a marketable surplus of staple and non-staple crops. The adoption of climate-resilient agricultural practices and technologies at scale is significantly enhanced if farmers are motivated to adopt them through market incentives. By creating an enabling environment for the private sector in this way, the project can sustain transformative changes in production and increase climate resilience of agro-ecosystems by enabling market linkages that leverage market incentives to motivate farmers to adopt and periodically innovate climate-resilient agricultural practices.

85. Strengthening of climate-resilient value chains requires the collaboration of all stakeholders in this chain. This project will convene a multi-stakeholder platform for each of the 15 districts, which will include representatives from government agencies, government authorities, government service providers, financial institutions, NGO technical assistance providers and others across the 15 districts. The Innovation Platforms will be established as partnerships between farmers’ organizations, civil society entities (NGOs), private sector, international research organization and will meet at a central location e.g. agricultural college. Each Innovation Platform will target the development of a specific value chain (horticulture, livestock, small grains, sesame, identified in Annex B to this document) that is most relevant for the district. The Innovation Platforms will be established with the aim to build the climate-resilience and productivity of the specific value chains through the FFS. Each Innovation Platform will support the development of a business plan for each value chain, including identification of market opportunities, climate-resilience, resilience of production and post-production barriers, and discussion of marketing strategies. A value-chain analysis and market assessment will be conducted as part of the feasibility study. The innovation platforms will also build on successful experiences from ZMRF and Scaling Up Adoption project or other similar projects. These partnerships between farmers’ private sector and financial institutions. During project implementation, user-centric, community-based value addition, marketing, and financing strategies will be developed as part of each Innovation Platform’s work.

# Figure 3: Innovation Platforms organizational framework

This Activity will enable a range of platforms to broker and coordinate partnerships between producers, processors, buyers, input suppliers, and other actors with the aim of lowering the risk of mismatches between product supply and demand. They will be supported through technical advisories and workshops for legal support services and meetings to provide networking among partners on innovation platforms. The platforms will also facilitate access to public and private sector investments through out-grower schemes, linking farmers with private "off-takers" through contract farming and multiparty agreements between farmers, private sector buyers and microfinance institutions for loans for inputs and productive asset investments.

On the provincial level, the Innovation Platforms will be supported by a provincial-level innovation platform. The provincial multi-stakeholder Innovation Platforms for upscaling diversified climate resilient production and access to markets, run platform-level workshops and meetings to provide technical assistance (including legal support services) to farmers and other climate-resilient market producers and access technical assistance (including legal support services to farmer organizations) to facilitate and formalize public-private partnerships across value chain actors, provide technical assistance and business planning and mentoring for smallholder farmers and other climate-resilient market producers. The provincial Innovation Platforms will also lead the development of a provincial-level action plan on scaling up climate-resilient agriculture.

The Activity will build on both CRIOD's small-scale infrastructure program and the previous Seeds and Markets Initiative (SMI) in which CRIOD has been working with farmers, processors, buyers, input suppliers, and other actors to provide technical assistance and expertise in regard to value chain support in the ongoing project Scaling Up Adaptation being implemented by UNDP/Defra and partners on climate-resilient value chain development and engagement of market actors for irrigated horticulture and livestock. The platforms will also build on the experience gained from the SMI, which has worked with a range of engaged agri-businesses such as Matunuka for bananas, Cairns for tomatoes and beans, and Schweepes and CEXH for citrus fruits. The project will utilize the work of EASAG and CIIMRT on Innovation Platforms for co-development of agriculture practices and market linkages, as well as the experience of ZIMR consortia for horticulture, livestock, sesame and similar dry land crops.
