Therefore, while the proposed project itself does not invest in value-chain development, it coordinates with and complements these various efforts.

### B6. GCF and UNDP resources will be used for training to enable smallholder farmers, particularly women, to build their technical and business skills. Specifically, this includes training on climate-resilient agricultural practices, as well as providing financial advisory support to financial intermediaries on how to appraise and invest in climate-resilient production and value chains. Specifically, the project will provide training for 251 Field Farmer Schools (FFS) leaders over four years and at least five times during the implementation of the empowerment training programme. The project will build on the efforts of the UNDP supported SSCP project on coordinating with existing financial intermediaries to develop a framework for providing climate-resilient agricultural finance to smallholder farmers and various financial intermediaries. These financial intermediaries would also be engaged in the Innovation Platforms established under the GCF project. This training will include training on how to identify and assess climate-resilient production systems, awareness and how these intermediaries can put in place various packages of options for farmers to access finance, e.g., policies for group lending schemes; mechanisms for flexible collateral requirements etc., to facilitate women to invest in climate resilient production.

This activity includes the following sub-activities:

* **2.1.1 Technical assistance:** 9 trainings and meetings to establish, operationalize, and coordinate five multi-stakeholder Innovation Platforms (one per province) that will provide a platform for smallholder farmers to access finance through financial intermediaries (two meetings over four years) for upscaling diversified climate resilient production and access to markets
* **2.1.2 Develop crop-specific products and market strategies for use by all relevant value chain actors for climate-resilient production systems, including the development of a marketing strategy for each of the three crops over 4 years and at least five plans)
* **2.1.3 Technical assistance (including legal support services to farmers' organizations) to establish public-private partnerships across value-chain actors to upscale climate resilient/agricultural markets
* **2.1.4 Technical assistance and business planning and management training to smallholder farmers, particularly women (under a wide-based approach) in the implementation of the empowerment training programme (three streams of women's program: 1) leadership development; 2) financial literacy; and 3) climate-resilient agricultural production)
* **2.1.5 Technical assistance and business planning and management training to smallholder farmers, particularly women under a wide-based approach) in the implementation of the empowerment training programme (three streams of women's program: 1) leadership development; 2) financial literacy; and 3) climate-resilient agricultural production)

### Activity 2.7 Investments in inputs, logistics and field-based training to scale up the implementation of climate-resilient agricultural production in the face of increasing climate hazards (rain-fed and irrigated farms)

This activity will support government extension services – using a participatory methodology - to refine, adopt, and widely disseminate climate-resilient agricultural practices for rain-fed and irrigated crops. This will be done through training of trainers in southern Zimbabwe. Smallholder farmers, particularly women, with over 251 Field Farmer Schools across 13 districts and at least three years of experience will be trained on climate-resilient agricultural practices that are more productive, more resilient to climate change, and more sustainable yields of staples and high-value crops in the face of increasing climate hazards. Through this activity, the project will run Training of Trainers workshops for those who will conduct Field Farmer Schools, organize and activate Field Farmer Schools for promoting climate-resilient agricultural practices. The training will be conducted by experienced trainers with at least five years experience as lead farmers to facilitate farmer-to-farmer learning to scale up implementation of climate-resilient agricultural practices in the face of increasing climate hazards. The trained trainers will then train other lead farmers from target provinces by leading farm leaders from the different wards who, once thoroughly trained in FFS, will return to their communities and engage other farmers in applying the acquired practices and techniques on their plots. AGFTEX field staff will supervise and support the implementation of this activity.

### Activity 87. This activity will therefore invest in training of trainers for Farmer Field Schools (FFS), as well as in the training of lead farmers in the implementation of CRA packages on centralized FFS plots of 0.5 hectares each on average, these lead farmers will then go on to


---
**Page 39**
---

to engage their neighbors in practicing climate-resilient agriculture on their own fields (0.50 hectares on average). Each of the thirty farmers participating in each FIS during two seasons of CRA activities will disseminate lessons and best practice to at least ten of their peers, who are not part of the project. This will result in a total of 300 farmers being trained by the end of the project period. This activity aims at final adoption of rain-fed CRA packages by 6900 farm leaders and 81,000 beneficiary farmers – a total of 75,900 households. The project will also support the development of climate-resilient agricultural practices and their adaptation to different smallholder agro-ecological conditions. This includes government influence on agriculture in the target provinces towards climate resilient agriculture. Specifically, the project will influence the adoption of rain-fed CRA packages by 51,900 households (73% of total) through the development of climate-resilient varieties. Government extension and research staff in the three provinces benefitting from awareness and training provided by the project will support indirect beneficiaries to adopt and implement climate resilient practices, technologies and crop varieties.

**8A. CRA packages for rain-fed agriculture**

The CRA package development process is based on participatory mapping as part of FFS and analysis of farming systems and agro-ecological principles under actual smallholder farming conditions.60 During project design, consultations were held with AGRITEK, DSSAS, Oxfam, ICRST and other staff in Harare as well as in the field to identify climate-resilient agricultural practices and technologies that are appropriate for different agro-ecological zones and other factors. Identified practices were then grouped in CRA packages for specific crops and conditions and onward confirmation, implementation and scaling up of these practices was supported by the project. The CRA package development process is based on experience with Climate-resilient Agriculture in Zimbabwe, the Conservation Agriculture program in Zimbabwe, studies of CRA practices in South Africa, Kenya, Tanzania, Uganda, Ethiopia, India, Nepal, Bangladesh, Pakistan, Malawi, Zambia, CIMMYT, Dfam and other institutions, and analysis of traditional knowledge related to coping with or adaptation to climate variability. Overall, 100 CRA sub-projects were developed for the three provinces, each focusing on specific climate-resilient agricultural practices to participate in the Innovation Platforms, particularly in regard to agroecology and integrated approaches to addressing the food-energy-water-ecosystems nexus at landscape level.61 The CRA sub-assignment in the Feasibility study provides further details.

**8B. CRA packages include practices such as soil conservation, low or no till and other appropriate tillage techniques, targeted fertilization, incorporation of trees and shrubs into cropping systems, multiple cropping/polycultures, integrated crop-livestock systems, intercropping, relay cropping, use of drought-tolerant crop varieties, water harvesting, fodder production and practices. The CRA packages also include use of drought-tolerant crop varieties - identified as a priority adaptation strategy in Zimbabwe – and the development of climate-resilient agricultural practices and technologies to address climate change.62 Coping with Drought Project in Chirinda District in southeastern Zimbabwe and other dry areas. Irrigation and rain-fed CRA will be encouraged where appropriate, but only if it is economically viable and sustainable. The project will support the development of water harvesting, fodder production, and hay or silage making using irrigated crop residue. Note that this Activity is implemented in conjunction with Activities 1.2, 2.0.

The activities carried out during GIN will be certified and disseminated to smallholders in communities and districts across Zimbabwe through exchange visits, easy-to-access information circulars and other means organized by AGRITEK.

This activity includes the following two sub-activities:

**2.3 Training of Trainers (GSS provincial, national, district and ward level AGRITEK staff), particularly women, to conduct Farmer Field Schools in 15 target Districts of southern Zimbabwe (10 workshops at the national and provincial level, and 85 trainings at the district and ward level)

**2.3.2 Organisation and activation of 251 Farmer Field Schools for promotion of climate-resilient agriculture in the 15 Districts

2.2.3 Procurement of inputs and technologies (e.g., seeds, tools, fertilizers) to implement CRA packages on 6,900 lead farmer pilots

2.2.4 Workshops and on-site assistance by lead farmers to facilitate farmer-to-farmer learning to scale up implementation of climate-resilient agricultural practices and technologies. Lead farmers will be selected from the top 15% of performers in FY2 under AGRA/USDA supervision, each lead farmer engages additional 10 farmers each through workshops and on-site assistance.

The targeted beneficiary community wards for the provision of the climate resilient practices under Activity 2.2 were identified based on the following criteria: (i) presence of a lead farmer; (ii) critical mass of farmers (Level 3); and (iii) to be reviewed and updated at the outset of project implementation for purposes of the Executive Entity's selection of such beneficiaries and practices:

* **Most climate vulnerable wards:** Application of climate vulnerability analysis in selection of wards to target most climate-vulnerable districts, mainly in relation to risk of end-of-season dry spells, droughts and other extreme weather phenomena.

**Partnership opportunities:**
* Analysis of potentials for synergies and complementarities with similar project interventions and avoidance of overlaps and doubling efforts. Both large scale projects and local community initiatives are considered.
* Ownership: Analysis of target population's social and economic vulnerabilities and capabilities; their level of ownership and investment of time and resources in interventions is key to ensure sustainability.

**Market linkages:**

Activity 2.3 Enhance institutional coordination and knowledge management capacities for climate-resilient agricultural production in the face of increasing climatic hazards

This activity will focus on strengthening institutional coordination, generation and sharing of knowledge regarding climate-resilient agricultural practices, water management, value-chains, marketing and other factors among key government departments; these will include Department of Economics and Markets, AGRA/IRD and CGIAR in collaboration with Department of Livestock and Veterinary Services to ensure that the lessons learned and best practices identified through IFIs, upscaling and Innovation Platforms are shared across departments. The activity will also focus on strengthening institutional coordination among key government departments regarding agricultural practices and water management. Specifically, it will upgrade KIGS data collection/gathering platforms and protocols across knowledge centers in participating agricultural colleges and research centers, engage technical expertise to support generation of climate-resilient agricultural technologies, strengthen the capacity of the Department of Livestock and Veterinary Services for climate-smart agriculture, and undertake impact evaluation and codification of best practices/lessons for systemic, evidence-based learning to scale up resilient agricultural practices.

At project level, the project's knowledge generation and learning activities will be grounded at the five Innovation Platforms (IPs) with support from each of three anchoring agricultural training colleges - Masvingo, Malolotja, and Ekurhuleni – and research stations (e.g. Mabote, Chikwanda and Chilimba stations). Together at national level experts, the IPs will provide a platform for knowledge generation and sharing among key stakeholders.

The project – with GoZ and CGIAR funding - will invest in strengthening five centers to serve as climate-resilient agricultural knowledge hubs. The centers will be equipped with state-of-the-art technology and resources including computerized smallholder assessments of on-the-ground experience with CRA initiatives and irrigation management, enabling student participation in practical experiments and research. The centers will also have a range of advanced technology tools and employing writers to draft readily accessible case studies and policy analyses. Together with the Innovation Platforms and beyond


---
**Page 41**
---

The project lifetime, these centers of excellence will support dissemination of lessons learned and best practices through the continuous monitoring and evaluation of climate change adaptation experiences. This material will be channeled through a national multi-stakeholder platform for knowledge management on climate-resilient agricultural systems and water resources management. The platform will facilitate exchange of information between project partners, disseminate practical lessons and practices, and learn from others to inform strategies that facilitate future scaling against AGRETA training platforms for new staff and extension workers.

At the national level, efficient institutional coordination and knowledge management is essential in facilitating system-level changes to enable sustained and transformational climate resilience and adaptation impacts lasting beyond the lifetime of project interventions. The project will support the development of a national multi-stakeholder platform for knowledge management between key institutions and stakeholders so that improved communication and more coordinated sharing of data and information can occur, leading to better decision-making on climate-resilient agricultural systems and water resources management in Zimbabwe. Also, the project seeks to strengthen the systematic documenting and sharing of information through adequate ICT and GIS systems, including the development of a national database for climate change adaptation experiences, generation of ongoing monitoring and evaluation data, development of knowledge management and information sharing guidelines and training on effective organizational knowledge management systems and advanced data analysis for climate risk-informed and resilient water and agricultural management.

In addition to the RfD, Section 2.1 will include impact evaluation to support evidence- and evidence-based learning. Lessons learned and best practices (referred to as lessons) will be documented in a national database for knowledge management not only support adaptive project management but also inform learning across national/sub-national/community levels within Zimbabwe and beyond, including policy makers and decision-makers.

This activity includes the following sub-activities:

2.3.1 Upgrade ICT/GIS data collection/sharing platforms and protocols for knowledge management on climate resilient agricultural systems and livelihoods across knowledge centers in participating agricultural colleges and research centers

2.3.2 Generation, certification and exchange of knowledge across agricultural colleges and research centers for climate-resilient agriculture.

2.3.3 Impact evaluation and codification of best practices/lessons for system-based evidence-based learning to scale-up resilient agricultural livelihoods.

Output 3: Improved access to weather, climate and hydrological information for climate-resilient agriculture

94. This Output addresses Barrier #8, above. To effectively address weather and climate-related risks through adaptive management strategies, farmers need timely and accurate information on rainfall, temperature, evaporation rates, irrigation and rainfall, extreme temperatures and evaporation, as well as the timing of these with respect to the crop growth cycle. This information is critical for decision-making in relation to water availability, crop selection, planting dates, sowing schedules. To address this need, water managers and agricultural extension officers (DINSA, AGRETA and MDC) require: i) the development of a national multi-stakeholder platform for knowledge management on climate-resilient agricultural systems at different timescales (weatherers, 1-2 days and seasons, 16 months); ii) the translation of weather/climate-related information into impacts on water availability for crops; iii) the translation of information into understandable language (colloquial and technical); iv) the dissemination of this information to farmers through appropriate channels (e.g. radio, SMS, mobile phones, etc.). In Zimbabwe this requires the development of systems to provide forecasts of water availability for irrigation (following catchment management practices), including rainfall-runoff models, soil moisture content, crop evapotranspiration and dam retention levels; weather forecasts, seasonal forecasts and water resource models.

95. This Output will support the establishment of a continuous, functional climate information system to optimize the resilience of agricultural systems and enhance decision-making through different sources (meteorological and hydrological), utilizing a combination of standard and low cost (particularly for O&M) technologies, as well as develop capacities to ensure the generation of fairly accessible weather and hydrological forecasts and information for water resource management, irrigation and dryland cropping. Weather/months/hydrological information will be appropriately packaged and combined with other sources of


---
**Page 42**
---

information related to household vulnerability/flood security by multi-institutional task teams (AGRITEX & MSID), and disseminated through mobile telecommunications and radio programs, with training conducted by both international and local universities. Further information will be provided on the use of satellite observations for monitoring vegetation stress and crop development, its materials on the use of water/weather forecasts and observations for irrigation scheduling by IMCs. This Output includes the following sub-activities:

Activity 3.1.1: Installation and operationalisation of weather/climate and hydrological observation networks

96. This activity addresses existing gaps in weather station coverage by installing 12 automatic weather stations, 10 automatic rainfall stations and 10 hydrological gauging stations at proposed irrigation sites that are not currently covered by the existing weather network. The new stations will be installed to provide a more comprehensive national weather and climate monitoring system and institutional capacities for hydro-meteorological data transmission and processing and train MSID, ZINWA, DRMS/AGRITEX officials, observers and other stakeholders in the use of these systems. This activity will also develop a national database using gridded observational datasets based on satellite observations, thus extending observations to areas without weather stations, as well as improving the quality of existing data through the application of advanced remote sensing techniques and statistical techniques. Targeted seasonal forecasts (based on ENSO/ODIO states) will be evaluated and utilized over the south western region. These quantitative outputs will form the foundations for developing the colored sectoral forecasts in activity 3.2. This activity will also support the development of data sharing protocols between MSID, ZINWA, DRMS/AGRITEX and other stakeholders on shared weather data for modeling and forecasting. It will further provide training for MSID, ZINWA and others (e.g. for school/college students) in the use of GCM or equipment.

This activity includes the following sub-activities:

3.1.1.1 Install 12 automatic weather stations to cover key agricultural zones and 10 automatic low-cost rainfall/weather stations to improve rainfall monitoring at the catchment level

3.1.1.2 Develop national-level seasonal forecasts for strategic points in the three catchments

3.1.1.3 Upgrade systems and institutional capacities for hydro-meteorological data transmission and processing to enable localised weather, climate and hydrological model-based forecast generation

Activity 3.1.2: Training, assessment and build institutional capacities (MSID and AGRITEX) on tailored climate and weather information products

This activity will scale up innovations, developed through the UNDP/GEF supported project, on targeted seasonal forecasts (based on Niño and the Indian Ocean Dipole) and climate information dissemination (utilizing SMS communications) to smallerholder farmers. The project will also develop a national-level water resource management system for smallholder farmers at the national level; ZINWA staff in the use of water resource models and ingesting input data from weather/climate observations and forecasts, as well as other sources such as rainfall-runoff models, soil moisture monitoring systems, remote sensing, etc. The project will also hold community meetings and local posters and bulletins. The project will also develop new tailored products to inform decision-making for food security and water resource management under the NEWL, and be used as part of the information dissemination network in the three southern catchments. This activity includes the following sub-activities:

3.1.2.1 Develop a national-level water resource management system for smallholder farmers at the national level

3.1.2.2 Train MSID, ZINWA, DRMS/AGRITEX officials, community observers (low-cost stations) in collecting data, operating and maintaining seasonal forecast training for MSID & ZINWA and DRMS/AGRITEX officials and observers from three catchments over two years)

### C.4 Background Information on Project/Programme Sponsor (Executing Entity)

The project sponsor is the Ministry of Lands, Agriculture, Water and Rural Resettlement (MLAWRR). The MLAWRR was set up to implement the Climate Change Policy and the National Climate Change Response Strategy (NCCRS) and to coordinate a sectoral response to climate change. MLAWRR has the mandate to receive climate change and related funding and channel it appropriately.

The Ministry of Lands, Agriculture, Water and Rural Resettlement is responsible for overseeing all land use planning and management operations. These include the Environment Management Agency (EMA), the Zimbabwe National Water Authority and the Parks and Wildlife Management Agency. The Meteorological Services Department, Forestry Commission and the Environment Management Agency also fall under the MLAWRR.

99. MLAWRR has coordinated a number of national climate change projects in the country through collaborative partnerships with the UNDP, FAO, WFP, GIZ, IUCN, CIDA, DFID, SDC, USAID, and others. These have contributed to the development of the NCCRS, the National Climate Policy, and the Coping with Drought and Climate Change and the Scaling Up


---
**Page 45**
---

Adaptation projects. Currently, the Ministry is implementing a UNDP funded project entitled Supported Enhanced Action for Low Carbon and Climate Resilient Development Pathway (SECA), which is expected to deliver results aligned with the NCCRS.

The Ministry has also been involved in the Global Facility for Climate-Smart Agriculture (GFCSA). As of March 2017, the Ministry had received US$35.4 million from the GFCSA, and as at December 2016, it had received US$89.5 million.

The Ministry is also involved in the Global Environment Facility (GEF) support project, the MUNAWIR has received GEF grants totaling USD 25,035,556, which leveraged USD104,313,100 in co-financing for 13 national projects. These include five projects in climate change, five in biodiversity, two in multi-use areas, and one in persistent organic pollutants (POPs). The total project cost is USD298,576,556, of which GEF contributed USD40,324,915, including USD21,821,348 in co-financing. These include seven projects in biodiversity, two in land degradation, five in international waters, one on climate change, one in persistent organic pollutants, and one multi-use area project.

101. Technical input: The Ministry anchors its strategic planning process through three key departments: the Meteorological Department (MD), the Zimbabwe Water Authority (ZWA), and the Environmental Management Authority (EMA). MD has a prime function of measurement and collection of accurate weather records. The Ministry maintains 47 Manual Synoptic Station (MSS) sites, which are manned by trained personnel to collect data on temperature, rainfall, humidity, wind speed, and direction. It also operates 20 voluntary Rainfall Stations. The MUNAWIR has plans to enter into partnerships set up to use 150 automatic weather stations and decontarate data collection. It has piloted the establishment of Climate user interface platforms for smallholder farmers to participate in climate change adaptation, co-operating with the California Department of Water Resources, a private sector firm, the UNDP and the Universities. Through its departments responsible for the environment, meteorological services, water management and climate change, the Ministry is able to provide a balanced approach to climate change mitigation through investments and catchment management. MUNAWIR has a strong collaborative relationship with the Ministry of Lands, Rural Rehabilitation and Resettlement (LRRR).

102. Management and Operational inputs: Under the proposed implementation arrangements outlined in section C.7 below, the Executing Entity (EE) for this project is the MUNAWIR. The MUNAWIR will have oversight of the project by co-chairing the Project Steering Committee with UNDP. The EE will be responsible for the overall management and coordination of the project on behalf of UNDP for managing the project, including the monitoring and evaluation of project interventions, achieving project outcomes, and for delivering outputs in line with the work plan and stated objectives. In addition to the above, as a co-executing entity under this UNDP, in agreement with the Government of Zimbabwe, will provide extensive implementation support (support to NMM) and oversight through the UNDP Country Office in Zimbabwe. MUNAWIR has implemented similar projects before, and external audits, including those by the World Bank, have been conducted.

91. To determine the capacity for the MUNAWIR to undertake this role in the project, an external capacity assessment of the Ministry of Environment, Water and Climate was conducted in March 2017. This is part of the requirements under the UNDP Harmonized Approach (HA) for assessing the capacity of the EE to manage the project. The assessment identified technical and operational shortfalls that needed to be addressed for effective implementation of this GCF project. Based on the assessment done on capacity, the Ministry has developed a work plan for the project which includes the establishment of an Asset Register and Inventory, Financial Reporting Monitoring and Procurement, the overall risk rating for the MUNAWIR was assessed as low. This indicates a low level of risk to the success of the Executing Entity in co-execution of the contract and work and a low likelihood of a potential negative impact on the IP's ability to execute the programme in accordance with the work plan and stated objectives.

103. Smallholder farmers' communities in Southern Zimbabwe live predominantly on communal lands. Settlements are mainly concentrated near rivers and in scattered villages inland where some water is available from natural pools and springs. Across this area, smallholder farmers grow a variety of crops including maize, sorghum, millet, beans, groundnuts, tobacco, and mixed production systems, livestock production systems and mixed crop-livestock production systems. The feasibility study provides a more detailed description of the agricultural landscape in Southern Zimbabwe. The agricultural landscape in the Southern Lowveld is mainly characterized by animal husbandry (small and big livestock) and some rain fed cultivation of maize, sorghum, pulses and sweet potatoes. In semi intensive farming regions such as Bvika and Zaka, smallholder farmers supplement their income through the production of tobacco, tea, coffee and other cash crops. The region is also characterized by high potential agricultural Eastern Highlands in Mancanland province, smallholder farmers in the Chipinge and Chimanimani districts intensively cultivate a variety of crops including maize, sorghum, millet, beans, groundnuts, tobacco, tea, coffee, fruits (e.g., mangoes), root crops, fruits, tea/coffee, tobacco). Several value chain studies indicate that there is significant demand and ready markets for a variety of agricultural products in the region. The study also indicates that there are opportunities to link smallholder farmers' production levels, unlock access to credit for inputs, develop farmers technical expertise, support access to buyers/markets, provide insurance and other services such as storage and transportation for smallholder farmers. (For further details on the agricultural landscape of Southern Zimbabwe please refer to the Agricultural Landscape Analysis under the Feasibility Study - Annexes IIIb and IIIc)

C.E. Regulation, Taxation and Insurance and Risk Management

104. The Zimbabwe National Water Act (ZINWA), a wholly Government owned entity tasked with managing the country's water resources provides technical advice on groundwater planning, development and management to the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources Development. ZINWA also has responsibility for the administration of water abstraction. Authorization and a valid permit for water use needs be sought from the relevant Catchment Council – for both ground and surface water. The Zimbabwe National Water Act (ZINWA) is administered by the Department of Water Affairs, with ZINWA being their Secretariat. Holders of a water permit, such as irrigation schemes, are charged with a water levy, paid into the national revenue fund which is mandated with the development of water resources in Zimbabwe under the Water Act. It is the responsibility of the relevant Catchment Council through the ZINWA to ensure that all permits are issued and monitored. The relevant Catchment Councils are responsible for issuing permits and monitoring compliance as required from Catchment Councils in their region.

105. Land is owned and administered by the government under the Communal Land Act 1982, according to which rural district councils have responsibility for land administration on Communal Land; and by communal land holders does not require a permit, for purposes of an irrigation scheme, such land is designated for such purpose in terms of a rural development plan approved by the Minister responsible for Communal Lands.

106. For any activities related to procurement of services through UNDP, taxes are exempted if required. Section 72 of the Convention on the Privileges and Immunities of the United Nations provides inter alia that the United Nations and its subsidiary agencies are exempt from taxation in respect of income derived by them or their employees from the performance of their functions and duties in respect of articles imported or exported for official use. If the services are procured directly by GoZ, then national legislation will apply, including any applicable VAT.

107. There are no specific insurance policies relevant for the project activities; however, insurance shall be applicable for all major project assets including project vehicles, ICT equipment and staff recruited for the project in accordance with UNDP rules and procedures. In terms of the Environment Impact Assessment Act, all activities being financed through the project, or in parallel with it, that are subject to conduct an Environmental Impact Assessment will do so.


---
**Page 47**
---

### C.7 Institutional / Implementation Arrangements

108.The project will be implemented under UNDP’s National Implementation Modality (NIM), according to the Standard Basic Assistance Agreement between UNDP and the Government of Zimbabwe, the Country Programme Document (CPD), and as policies and procedures set out in the UNDP Project Management Guidelines. The Project is a Direct Project. More information can be found at: <https://info.unido.org/global/pops/ppm/Pages/Defining-a-Project.aspx>. The National Executive Entity—also referred to as the Implementing Agency—is responsible for implementing the project, including the monitoring and evaluation of project interventions, achieving project outcomes, and for the overall management of the project. This includes ensuring compliance with UNDP’s policies, procedures, guidelines, and regulations, policies (including the NIM Guidelines). In legal terms, this is ensured through the national Government’s commitment to sign a Standard Basic Assistance Agreement (SBAA) with UNDP, which will be signed by the Executing Entity to govern the use of the funds (once the funds are secured). The Standard Basic Assistance Agreement (SBAA) was signed with the Government of Zimbabwe in May of 2019.

109.The project is implemented through the Ministry of Lands, Agriculture, Water and Rural Resettlement (MLAWRR) (the “Executing Entity”). MLAWRR, through the Permanent Secretary, is accountable to UNDP for managing the project, including the monitoring and evaluation of project interventions, achieving project outcomes, and for the overall management of the project. This includes ensuring compliance with UNDP’s policies, procedures, guidelines, support services (support to NIM), including procurement of goods and services, as agreed and costed in the letter of agreement between MLAWRR and UNDP. The Project is implemented from the UNDP Country Office in Zimbabwe and BPF/UNDP Global Environmental Finance Unit in Addis Ababa Regional Hub and Headquarters in New York.

110.UNDP provides a three-tier oversight and quality assurance role involving UNDP staff in Country Offices and at regional and headquarters levels. The quality assurance role supports the Project Steering Committee by carrying out objective and independent assessments of project performance, including progress towards achieving project outcomes as they are managed and completed. Project assurance must be independent of the Project Management function; the Project Steering Committee is responsible for ensuring that all project activities are carried out in accordance with UNDP’s policies and procedures. The quality assurance role will be provided by UNDP staff who have been covered by the Accredited Entity fee provided by the GCF. As an Accredited Entity to the GCF, UNDP is required to deliver GCF-specific oversight and quality assurance services including: (i) Day-to-day oversight supervision; (ii) Oversight of the project compliance with UNDP policies.

111.In addition, the Government of Zimbabwe may request UNDP to provide direct project services for this project. The UNDP and Government of Zimbabwe acknowledge and agree that those services are not mandatory and will be provided only upon Government request. Any such provision of direct project services would follow UNDP policies on the recovery of direct project costs relating to GCF funded projects.


---
**Page 48**
---

112.The Executing Entity will enter into or put in place arrangements and/or legal agreements with the following Responsible Parties™ for the implementation of certain outputs and/or activities: (i) The Department of Irrigation (“DoI”), (ii) AGRITEK, (iii) the Ministry of Environment, Climate, Tourism and Hospitality Industries (“MECTHI”) as the implementing agency of the project, and (iv) the Pakistan Council of Research in Water Resources (PCRWR) as the service provider for the Pakistan Climate Change Services for Agriculture (PCCSA) and will be engaged by UNDP as a service provider through a UN to UN agreement. The responsible parties will have specific roles and responsibilities with respect to the implementation of certain outputs and/or activities, while the MUNWIR will facilitate their collaboration for each activity. Each of the responsible parties will support the delivery of the specific project activities indicated below:

| Outputs | Activities | Responsible Party(ies) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1. | Activity T-1: Climate proofing irrigation infrastructure for enhanced water security in the face of climate change | DoI |
|  | Activity T-2: Field-based training and technology investments for farmers on rain-fed farming for climate-resilient water management | AGRITEK |
| 2. | Activity Z-1: Establish transformative multi-stakeholder innovation platforms for developing climate-resilient agriculture and markets | AGRITEK |
|  | Activity Z-2: Investments in inputs, technologies and field-based training to scale up the implementation of climate-resilient agricultural practices on rain-fed farming (including climate hazards (rainfed and irrigated farms)) | AGRITEK |
|  | Activity Z-3: Enhance institutional coordination and knowledge management capacities for climate-resilient agricultural production in the face of increasing climate hazards | AGRITEK |

<sup>10</sup>Due to recent changes in the Government of the Host Country, MISO is now a department under the new Ministry of Environment, Climate, Tourism and Hospitality Industries (“MECTHI”), while DoI and AGRITEK remain as departments under the same ministry. PCRWR remains an independent entity. On the other hand, DWNWA is a state-owned legal entity (parastatal) responsible for managing the country’s water resources.


---
**Page 49**
---

3. Activity 3.1: Installation and operationalization of weatherclimate and hydrological observation networks.

MSD/ZINWA/AGRITEK

Activity 3.2: Develop, disseminate and build institutional capacities (MSD and AGRITEK) for tailored climate and weather information products

MSD

Activity 3.3: Capacity building for farmers and local institutional staff on effective use of climate and weather information and products for resilient smallholder agriculture and agricultural planning

AGRITEK/MSD


12.2 The Department of Irrigation will be responsible for delivering activities under Output 1 including climate proofing of irrigation infrastructure and equipment in 21 irrigating schemes; training of 21 MLCs, support to O&M and networking of MLCs for learning. The department will AGRITEK, will be responsible for the agricultural extension service activities under Output 1 and 3, with emphasis on the use of climate information products. ZINWA will be responsible for the installation and maintenance of weather stations, ZINWA for hydrological station and together ZINWA and MSD will be responsible for stream gauging and water quality monitoring. The Department of Water Resources and Irrigation will be responsible for DSS related to innovation platforms, with the support of DSSR and collaboration with Department of Economics and Markets and DVS, including development of climate information products targeting smallholder farmers, use of crop and livestock specific strategies for production and market linkages and facilitation of partnerships with private sector.

14. Under the instructions and supervision of MAWIRRI, MSD and ZINWA will be jointly responsible for delivering activities under Output 2, which includes: establishment of a climate information service centre; development of climate information products targeting smallholder farmers; dissemination of climate information products to smallholder farmers through extension services and enhance and develop existing DRESS and AGRITEK knowledge centres. MSD will take responsibility for the installation and maintenance of weather stations, ZINWA for hydrological station and together ZINWA and MSD will be responsible for stream gauging and water quality monitoring. The Department of Water Resources and Irrigation will be responsible for DSS related to innovation platforms, with the support of DSSR and collaboration with Department of Economics and Markets and DVS, including development of climate information products targeting smallholder farmers, use of crop and livestock specific strategies for production and market linkages and facilitation of partnerships with private sector.

15. Given the potential of investments towards policy reform, the Department of Markets and Economics under MAWIRRI, as a Department responsible for Policy, will mainly take up a coordinating role around policy, participating in the project board and working group meetings.

16. The project proposed will be established and delivered through a project management unit, which will purely use the implementation and management structure of the current UNDP Zimbabwe Resilience Building Fund.


17. Overview of the Zimbabwe Resilience Building Fund: The ZRBF was established in May 2015 through an agreement between UNDP and the Government of Zimbabwe (GoZ) to provide a financial mechanism for the implementation of the Zimbabwe Resilience Building Fund. Currently three donors - DFID, EU and SIDA- have contributed to the ZRBF. The ZRBF is implemented as a UNDPS assisted project in partnership with GoZ and funded by the standard UNDP/GoZ agreement.


20 MSD will be responsible for the installation and operationalization of weatherclimate observation networks while ZINWA will be responsible for the installation and operationalization of hydrological observation networks.


---
**Page 50**
---

Between the Government of Zimbabwe, represented by Ministry of Lands, Agriculture, Water and Rural Resettlement (MLAWRR), and UNDP, guided by the Zimbabwe United Nations Development Assistance Framework (ZUNDAF) Action Plan and policy framework, a National Climate Change Adaptation Strategy (NCCAS) and Operational Plan (OP) have been developed. This is a framework mechanism that guides the implementation of resilience programmes and projects in Zimbabwe, enabling synergies to be harnessed between ZBF and other climate change initiatives.

118. Under the current Assisted NIM arrangement of ZBF, UNDP through the ZBF Management Unit, ensures management, good governance and fiduciary oversight of the ZBF. To perform this responsibility UNDP operates under the existing Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) between the Zimbabwe Government and UNDP. The MoU is reviewed annually and updated by a Steering Committee which is co-chaired by government (MLAWRR and donors EU, DFID and SIDA on a rotational basis). UNDP through its ZBF Management Unit provides strategic guidance to the project, makes high-level policy decisions, and guides the Executing Entity and responsible parties in the execution of the project. The ZBF Steering Committee is responsible for approving the annual work plan, monitoring progress towards targets, managing and overseeing the day-to-day work of the ZBF and prepares technical inputs for consideration of the ZBF Steering Committee.

119. Given the potential for synergies between ZBF and the proposed project and considering aid effectiveness principles, a coordinated management structure is desirable. Synergies can be found both at the strategic level (i.e. joint steering committee) as well as operational level (i.e. shared resources and common platforms). This will ensure that there are no duplications and substantive level (i.e. programmatic collaboration based on similar and complementary interventions to build resilience), hence, a coordinated management structure is proposed for both the ZBF and the proposed project. The proposed management arrangements to ensure a shared national resilience building approach. At the same time, the proposed management structure builds upon existing institutional arrangements in Zimbabwe and ensures appropriate accountability and decision-making per different contractual arrangements vis-à-vis the ZBF and the proposed project.

| **Operational Plan (OP)** | **Operational Plan (OP)** |

### Proposed project Steering Committee meetings may function as a national Zimbabwe Resilience Building Platform that includes both ZRBf and GCF project stakeholders. This platform will enable substantive synergies from the various initiatives in existence within the country to be harnessed for resilience building. The WFP-GCF project will contribute to this by setting up a shared national platform to maintain resilience thinking across GCF missions, departments and projects with time. Mechanisms for sharing results and monitoring of GCF project outputs will be established to ensure meeting all GCF requirements.

125.WFP will be a responsible entity and a member of the PSC. It can also engage to provide services for the project in the form of utilizing its experience within the PICSA components of Activity 3.2 (the coordination of the Executing Entity MAWIR). This responsibility is based on the ongoing collaboration of WFP with the University of Reading on PICSA, the need to take a coordinated approach across all GCF projects and the fact that WFP has already developed a climate information system proposal to the GCF relating to climate information systems in Zimbabwe, and WFP’s comparative advantage from its existing project experience. The PICSA activity will be executed by WFP (as a service provider) and will be implemented in countries, e.g., Malawi, and the two GCF projects; if approved, with above the implementation of the PICSA package over four years. In addition, both proposed projects support the strengthening of national climate information systems and will coordinate their efforts to avoid duplication of efforts. The PICSA activity is expected to be complementary rather than a duplication of efforts or activities between this proposal and the WFP-GCF project but rather complementarity since the two projects are in different countries.

The proposed GCF project day-to-day operational arrangements: The Executing Entity will designate a National Project Director responsible for the overall direction, strategic guidance and timely delivery of the Project. The project will set up an PMU, including a PMU Head of Unit (PMU HOU) to be responsible for the management and supervision responsibility of the execution and implementation of the GCF activities, under the guidance of the PSC. The PMU is set up and managed by UNDP, including recruitment and appointment of the PMU HOU. The PMU will be responsible for the overall direction, strategic guidance and will follow UNDP rules and regulations. Additional administrative and logistical support staff for processing of human resources, procurement and logistic aspects of the implementation of the project may be assigned by UNDP.

The proposed GCF project day-to-day operational arrangements: The PMU HOU will be the focal point for ensuring synergies and complementarities across the ZRBf and the proposed project. As the current ZRBf funding cycle runs until 2021, the ZRBf PMU Head of Unit will act as resilience advisor to the GCF project team and provide guidance on how to ensure that the ZRBf and the GCF projects are complementary in terms of activities and proposed GCF project investments and resilience approach. Based on workload estimates, the ZRBf PMU Head of Unit will be proportionally allocated time to support the GCF project. The PMU HOU will also provide guidance on how to ensure that synergies and complementarities between the ZRBf and the proposed project are maintained in terms of capabilities of the ZRBf and the proposed project PMU. The proposed management structure will reduce transaction costs for the planned activities.

The project coordinator will have day-to-day management and supervisory responsibility of the GCF proposed project team and will be supported by five thematic specialists in the PMMU responsible for Climate Resilient Irrigation Design and Operations, Climate Change Adaptation and Mitigation, Water Resources Management, Climate Change and Disaster Risk Reduction. Each output and activity and oversight for general maintenance will be provided from the UNDP Country Office gender specialist and focal person for climate change. The project coordinator will be responsible for ensuring accountability for the delivery of results specified in the project document, to the required standard of quality and within the specified constraints of time and cost. The annual work plan is prepared by the Project Coordinator and reviewed and approved by the PSC.

The ZRBf Project Coordinator will run the GCF project on a day-to-day basis with advice from the ZRBf PMU Head as resilience advisor and will have the responsibility to ensure complementarities between the two projects. The advantage of a shared national platform is that it will enable better coordination, sharing of resources, exchange of information, pooling of evidence building and better collaboration for resilience building. Likewise, a strong collaboration across the ZRBf and the proposed project will ensure that both projects are complementary in terms of activities and investments related to rural climate change adaptation and resilience building. The PICSA activity will be executed by WFP (as a service provider) and


---
**Page 53**
---

the related advisor for PICSA climate information services will be cost shared with WFP (two years for WFP and two years for the proposed project), as part of the delegation of responsibilities for the PICSA component of Activity 3.2 and the related inter-agency arrangements.

### 130 Funds Flow: Donor funding, including the GCF funding, will be received by the administrative agent (UNDP) on behalf of the Executing Entity (MAWAW) based on a standard letter of agreement signed between the donors and UNDP and an MDU letter of agreement (LOA) with the MAWAW. The funds will be disbursed to the goods and service providers as required, utilizing whereby transfers will be made directly from UNDP to the goods and service providers upon the request of the EE. For co-financing from the Government of the Host Country, the funds will flow through the MAWAW to the UNDP (as administrative agent of the Executing Entity) with channel such funding via a "Direct Payments" option whereby transfer will be made directly from the Government of the Host Country to the goods and service providers (Executing Entity). On the other hand, the Co-financing from the Government of the Host Country will flow to the MAWAW as budget allocations and used directly by such ministry to pay procured parties and/or transfer such funds to the relevant Responsible Parties.

# D.1 Value Added for GCF Involvement

## 132. In the absence of GCF engagement to complement or strengthen ongoing efforts and address key insufficiencies and gaps, Government of Zimbabwe (GoZ) will continue to face challenges in addressing climate-related risks and impacts on water and food security at scale across southern Zimbabwe. Given the indebtedness<sup>45</sup> of the country, the government is constrained to invest in adaptation measures that are critical to secure the resilience of the agricultural sector as well as the broader economy. The GoZ will continue to face challenges in financing climate change adaptation, which will foster scaling up of existing and additional efforts for transformative change and impact across southern Zimbabwe.

## GCF involvement is, therefore, critical<sup>46</sup>.

### 133. Smallholder farmers in southern Zimbabwe have largely maintained traditional approaches to managing water, soil, and crops for food production, which has contributed to considerable income losses, the productivity and viability of their agro-ecosystems have deteriorated over the years due to a number of factors, including overly intensive cultivation and land degradation<sup>47</sup>. The country is also prone to frequent and severe flooding events that are exacerbated by heavy rainfall and floods. Farmers have been constrained in adapting to hydro-meteorological hazards by their intensity and frequency, which leaves farmers unable to repair irrigation infrastructure and equipment held in common—in particular as they are caught in a cycle of debt<sup>48</sup>. The GoZ has invested heavily in irrigation infrastructure over the past decades, but this has not been sufficient in addressing the incremental costs of enhancing agro-ecosystem resilience to climate change.

### 134. Development investments over the past decades, particularly in relation to irrigation infrastructure, have suffered from inadequate maintenance and rehabilitation<sup>49</sup>. This is due to a combination of factors including the onset of heavy rains, landslides on damaged canals, dams and ponds with sedimentation of erosion of banks and stream beds. Current investments and projects are difficult to sustain in the long-term because they do not address the underlying causes of these problems, such as poor design<sup>50</sup>. The private sector has little incentive to invest given the risks and uncertainties associated with smallholder production, including drought, climate change and other barriers.

### 135. With the effects of climate change expected to increase over the coming years, the Government of Zimbabwe fully recognizes the significance to its country's food security of ensuring that vulnerable smallholder farmers have the means, skills, resources and access to information required to manage their resources in a climate-resilient manner<sup>51</sup>. While some government funds have been made available for irrigation rehabilitation<sup>52</sup>, the limited budget of the Government of Zimbabwe is limited and insufficient to move smallholder farmers to climate resilient and improved livelihoods. The debt crisis has also constrained the ability of the GoZ to invest in adaptation measures, including through more concessional financing and debt relief; Zimbabwe has little choice from emerging out of debt problems even in the long term<sup>53</sup>. The country's economy is heavily dependent on agriculture, which is a major source of employment for smallholder farmers but also negatively affects the country’s future economic growth prospects. The smallholder farmers in the project target areas live in poverty and are highly vulnerable to climate change impacts<sup>54</sup>. Smallholder farmers need access to climate-smart<sup>55</sup> resources are indispensable to address the incremental costs of climate-proofing community irrigation systems, promoting climate-resilient agricultural practices, diversifying income and managing climate risk by facilitating public private partnerships for climate change adaptation<sup>56</sup>. The GoZ has recognized that climate information is disseminated and disseminated to decisions and policy makers at all levels, from farmer to the national level.

### Leverage and combining public and private sector financing for community-level investments for adapting among smallholders


---
**Page 56**
---

136. GCF financing will galvanize government investment in building the resilience of vulnerable smallholder agroecosystems in southern Zimbabwe by mobilizing its institutional and financial support towards the proposed investments. This co-financing Standardized Investment Proposal (SIP) is designed to leverage additional resources from a variety of public, private, and civil society partners to provide comprehensive and cost-effective climate change adaptation services at scale.

137. GCF funding enables leveraging of additional resources to support adaptation investments that, together with community contributions, will help ensure sustained benefits from infrastructural resilience. This project will engage the private sector in public-private partnerships (PPPs) for the development of a resilient agroecosystem. These PPPs will include value chain actors such as integrated suppliers, buyers, credit institutions, relevant government agencies, and smallholder producer organizations in agrarian communities. The PPPs will also include other stakeholders such as input providers, extension services, commercialization goals, coordination of input provision and marketing, and use of climate information and climate-resilient agricultural advisories. These partnerships will ensure efficient coordination of project resources for sustained impact on the ground. GCF funding will support the development of a PPP framework that includes a set of enabling conditions to incentivize private sector investment in managing public infrastructure, incentives farmers to invest in soil and water management, and engage and sustain private sector investments.

138. GCF Financing will facilitate parallel and complementary financing from private sector and development partners for community level initiatives in southern Zimbabwe aimed at strengthening the sustainable rural livelihoods of smallholder households. This will be achieved by providing access to credit facilities for smallholder producers’ groups to access credit by linking them directly to lenders on multi-stakeholder Innovation Platforms.

Reaching the most vulnerable population in southern Zimbabwe

139. GCF funding will directly support the efforts of a significant, vulnerable population who, over the past decade, has experienced increasing vulnerability due to climate change and limited access to water availability. These smallholders have very limited market access and few alternate employment or income opportunities. Investing in local irrigation schemes can provide an opportunity for these households to diversify their livelihoods by increasing their participation in the production and sale of marketable climate-resilient crops, creation of complementary rural enterprises, and increased participation in off-farm activities. The GCF investment will also support the development of a multi-stakeholder segment of the population of Zimbabwe through this project which will preclude the need to provide extensive relief later in response to anticipated climate change impacts.

140. Without GCF funding, the Government of Zimbabwe will be unable to invest adequately in building the capacities of smallholders at scale to adapt and manage their rain-fed and irrigated agriculture and rural livelihoods in the face of increasing climate hazards. By restoring and revitalizing irrigation schemes for climate-resilient production, providing climate information for crop selection and management, and supporting the development of a multi-stakeholder platform for adaptation services, we will leveraging inter-institutional coordination and public-private partnerships for increased food security and income. GCF investment will be critical in helping the GoZ to strengthen its adaptive capacities.

### D.2. Exit Strategy


---
**Page 57**
---

143. The project exit strategy is premised on stakeholders' abilities to continue to implement project activities beyond project termination. This includes increased knowledge and technical skills, securing ongoing investment in resilience-enhancing agricultural practices, and access to markets for improved seeds, inputs, and products.

142. Key elements of the exit strategy, as described below, include: integrating traditional and modern agro-ecological methods and systems to achieve greater and more sustainable yields; building the capacity of small farmers and other local stakeholders for integrated, locally owned solutions; leveraging complementary investments by government and local communities for post-project maintenance and management activities; ensuring that project investments are sustained beyond the project's life through IMCs in maintenance and operations; and growing in private sector engagement through value chain, market and financial linkages.

The project proposal has been designed in close consultation with the Ministry of Lands, Agriculture, Water and Rural Re settlement (MLAWRR), particularly AGRITEK and the Department of Irrigation (DOI), Department of Research and Specialist Services (DRSS) and other relevant line departments. The consultations were also conducted at the provincial level with technical line Departments, as well as local governments, local and national NGOs, private enterprise, and men and women farmers of the three targeted provinces of southern Zimbabwe. These consultations and discussions (see Stakeholder Consultation Report) provided a solid foundation for the project approach and identified the corresponding integrated set of interventions that will be implemented in each province. The stakeholder consultation process ensures that project design ensures that project investments and results will be sustained beyond the project's duration through:

144. Integrating traditional knowledge and production practices with modern agro-ecological methods and systems to establish a resilient agricultural system that is climate-resilient, sustainable and economically viable. This approach focuses particularly women who constitute the majority of irrigation and dryland farmers; typically have a reserve of local contextual knowledge and experience in crop management and soil fertility; are often more innovative than men because they generally have very a clear idea of production constraints and opportunities and have adapted their production practices over time to overcome these challenges. However, this adaptation has not been documented or systematized. The generation and acquisition of indigenous knowledge cannot keep pace with rapidly emerging climate-driven obstacles and production problems. This project will use traditional knowledge of the local smallholder context as a point of departure for the establishment of an integrated agricultural system that is climate-resilient, sustainable and economically viable. It will integrate modern methods, climate-resilient agro-ecological science with traditional knowledge through a participatory action research methodology. The project will also ensure that the solutions are owned by smallholder communities and that they have the skills and knowledge to implement them. This is achieved by building on the portfolio of CRA packages designed during project preparation. Calibration of these packages will be done in collaboration with local farmers, who will then use their own local climate knowledge and expertise to support integration with traditional knowledge and continued development and dissemination of best practices.

145. Building the capacities of smallholders and other local stakeholders for integrated, locally owned solutions: by using a participatory action research methodology, local smallholders confirm the climate-driven production problems they currently encounter as well as their own adaptation strategies. This is followed by an analysis of these strategies to identify knowledge systematized, codified and evaluated in a smallholder process of learning-by-doing. This activity participatory methodology builds capacities for analysis and identification of solutions, ensures ownership of the project results and builds confidence among local stakeholders that they can effectively manage their own resources and livelihoods, especially in regard to climate-resilient solutions. This methodology is structured formally around Farmer Field Schools which is lead farmer-based approach where a group of farmers are trained by an expert who then applies them, and then replicate and adapt this experience in other smallholder communities through farmer-to-farmer exchanges.


---
**Page 58**
---

146. The project builds capacities of smallholders and institutional staff including through Farmer Field Schools and peer-to-peer learning for sustained impact beyond the project. Capacities are built for climate-adapted operations and maintenance of irrigation systems, water harvesting and storage, water use efficiency (Activity 2.1), crop diversification (Activity 2.2), improved agricultural production, and business development and organizational and financial management for production (Output 2), and on climate information services (CIS) to support smallholder adaptation to climate change (Activity 3). The project will also provide technical support to smallholders around the locally selected climate-driven production issues to overcome the sectorial and piecemeal approach to climate change adaptation. Smallholder capacity building will be provided through Farmer Field Schools (FFS) (Activity 2.1), and ZINWA will receive training in the use of water resource models (Activity 3.2). Dot, ZINWA and CCF will be staffed with trained personnel who can provide technical support to smallholders on climate change adaptation using a participatory system design approach (Activity 3.3). Training will also be provided to district and local level intermediaries: Agriculture Extension, MISP and IMC staff - in the interpretation and dissemination of climate and weather information products for crop/water management and crop irrigation.

147. Leveraging complementary investments by government institutions and smallholder communities. Government institutions and smallholder organizations in southern Zimbabwe are committed to the objective and outcomes of this project, as evidenced by their willingness to invest in the project. The project will leverage these commitments through mobilizing resources to support post-project implementation. Investments by these institutions include resources totaling USD 4.2 million for climate change adaptation activities (Activity 3) and USD 105,600 for irrigation scheme management and dissemination of climate-resilient agriculture packages and training of smallholders in Farmer Field Schools and support to extension officers on climate change adaptation. The project will also provide resources totaling USD 784,290 for agricultural advisors and climate and hydrological information for agriculture and water management planning (USD 750,400).

148. The project also leverages significant contributions from smallholders themselves mainly through Irrigation Management Committees (IMCs). Smallholder contributions will be made in cash and/or kind. In-kind contributions include the provision of inputs and practices and their extension to other smallholders will be implemented with community co-financing in cash and/or kind. Due to the depressive economic situation among many dryland smallholder farmers, a minimal cash contribution for project implementation is required. The project will require USD 600 per year from each IMC (12 IMCs) of which USD 500 will be used by the IMC to provide significant contributions in cash and in kind. Community contributions per year are estimated at USD 47,983 in total for the full period project (2015-2025). The project will also require a minimum annual contribution of USD 60 per month per IMC. With 21 schemes and 5,900 beneficiaries, there will be an estimated USD 2,482,740 in cash contributions for the full period project (2015-2025). The project will also require a minimum annual contribution of USD 360 per year from each IMC and USD 1,936,430 in kind contributions for the full period project (2015-2025) to target irrigation schemes.

149. Overall, with this co-financing - "in the game" - smallholders strengthen their commitment to and ownership of climate adaptation activities beyond the life of the project. These contributions manifest stakeholder commitments to sustain their investments beyond the lifetime of the project.

150. The project's close collaboration with ZIMFAR will also enable ongoing support by ZIMFAR, post-project, to generation and dissemination of climate information services (CIS) for smallholders through its existing marketing network. In this regard, ZIMFAR will continue to explore possible future non-grant options, including private sector investment.

151. Establishing Operations and Maintenance funds for each irrigation scheme and training in project and post-project O&M: To ensure continued support to the irrigation schemes beyond the life of the project, it is necessary for irrigation infrastructure and management systems to be established on demand. This is especially important considering that irrigation schemes are established in a communal setting i.e., one irrigation scheme for multiple farmers requiring close scheduling of irrigation of individual plots. An essential element of this project will therefore be the establishment of an operations and maintenance (O&M) fund for each scheme, with experience with O&M of the irrigation schemes they have supported in the past and an in-depth analysis of each scheme - that


---
**Page 59**
---

lays out the corresponding activities, costs and responsibilities, as well as the approximate capital required to maintain and operate irrigation schemes on an annual basis. This provides the participating Irrigation Management Committees with financial benchmarks for their operations and maintenance (O&M) plans. The project will also provide training workshops for each participating smallholder. As such, this project will work with each Irrigation Management Committee to establish its O&M fund and prepare a plan for its operation. The project will also support the development of an Operations & Maintenance Manual that builds their organizational capacities to plan and manage irrigation and water allocations, manage on-going capitalization of their O&M funds, and provide guidance in the implementation of the O&M plan. This manual will be based on the GCF’s O&M manuals, technical guidelines for water management, and SOPs, among other tools. The O&M plan is detailed in Annex XIII.

### 152. Operations and Maintenance of the hydro-meteorological infrastructure: Operations & Maintenance (O&M) of the installed hydro-meteorological infrastructure will be carried out by the project through a combination of regular funding from domestic resources (government budget allocations) and capacity building (human and financial planning) for regular/sustained O&M services. The project will provide technical assistance to the Irrigation Management Committees, the Ministry of Water and Sanitation (MWS), the Ministry of Infrastructure Development (MID) and ZWNA, helping them to negotiate future increases in allocated domestic financing and commitments, which in turn strengthens the long-term viability of established infrastructure. Maintenance and service contracts (for three years) will be bundled with the project’s O&M plan for each Irrigation Management Committee. The funds required for this will be provided by MID and ZWNA through their budget allocations. USD 145,000 in post-project O&M will be financed by MID and ZWNA; see G&I part A section 3.2.

### 153. Crowding in private sector engagement through value chain, market and financial linkages for sustained production and commercialization of climate-resilient crops: This project will bring together in Innovation Platforms all stakeholders in selected value chains to identify the key challenges that need to be addressed to ensure long-term sustainability of climate adaptation by smallholders. These Innovation Platforms will comprise representatives of all important stakeholders, including government authorities (e.g., MWS, MID and ZWNA), private sector actors (e.g., input suppliers, the water allocation authorities, local government authorities, research institutions and others, as feasible). Innovation Platforms will identify and analyze climate-resilient value chain objectives and develop medium to long term strategies and action plans to overcome identified challenges. The project will support the development of a common platform for all stakeholders in each value chain as a common goal for and in the best interest of all Innovation Platform members. It is expected that the relationships and processes established within these platforms will be sustained beyond the life of this project. In addition, it is expected that the establishment of Innovation Platforms micro-sized institutions will be positioned to support community-level enterprises and the production of high value climate-resilient crops. The project will also provide technical assistance for the development of a market linkages with growers, enabling access to finance through technical support to both smallholder borrowers and lenders (credit associations, banks, etc.) and providing feasibility to scale on the investments made by the project and sustain and scale their agricultural livelihoods beyond the project lifetime.

# E.1 Impact Potential

## Potential of the project/programme to contribute to the achievement of the Fund’s objectives and result areas

### 1.0 Livelihoods

The project will contribute to the following GCF GCF level impacts for adaptation: 1.0 “Increased resilience and enhanced livelihoods of the most vulnerable people, communities and regions” and 2.0 “Increased resilience of health and well-being, food and water security, and ecosystems.”

### Strategic framework and impact potential

The figure below illustrates how the project will contribute to the achievement of the Fund’s objectives and result areas through strengthening the adaptive capacities of vulnerable smallholder farmers, especially women, to climate change-induced impacts on their agricultural livelihoods. This is done by revitalization of irrigation schemes, upgraded water and soil moisture management and water use efficiency, climate-resilient agriculture, improved access to climate information and markets, and partnerships with public and private sector actors.

![Figure 7: Linkages fostering Impact Potential](image.png)

### Overall impact

Overall, the project will benefit a total of 320,122 direct and indirect beneficiaries in 15 districts and 137 priority wards in vulnerable areas (Mashonaland West and Vhembeland) of the provinces of Manicaland, Masvingo and Matabeleland South in southern Zimbabwe (about 24% of the total population of the three provinces), with 543,620 rural beneficiaries directly benefiting from the project interventions. The project will also benefit an additional 178,900 urban households through partnerships with local government as well as government and other partners. The interventions will provide indirect benefits to an estimated additional 1,758,500 people in the 249 remaining wards of the 15 districts by mainstreaming climate change concerns into planning and implementation of the project’s activities. These households will benefit from improved access to climate information, training on new technologies and knowledge and learning mechanisms. The project will also influence the government to transform its spending on its existing input supply schemes in a more climate-resilient manner.

- 5000 smallholder households (of which at least 30% are female-headed households) will directly benefit from the revitalization of 21 irrigation schemes in the targeted 15 Districts for a total of 29,500 people overall;


---
**Page 61**
---

- 23 Irrigation Management Committees will be trained in a) climate-adapted operations and maintenance of irrigation systems, b) project planning and monitoring methods c) organizational management and administration, conflict resolution and gender equality.
- 7,590 (6,900 dryland and 690 irrigated) lead farmers will be trained in 251 Farmer Field Schools on soil moisture management, climate change adaptation, water use efficiency, and gender equality. The project directly benefits female farmers per household/beneficiary; Farmer field schools will target to reach at least 1,375 female lead farmers, which should include at least 40% women.
- 69,000 dryland beneficiary farmers and 5,270 irrigation beneficiary farmers will receive training from selected lead farmers, including climate change adaptation, water use efficiency, and gender equality. The project targets to increase water use efficiency for a total of 371,350 people benefited. Lead Farmers are encouraged to target at least 50% female farmers – reaching out to at least 17,175 female farmers.
- Five farmer field schools will be established in each of the districts for select climate-resilient crop and livestock value chains, consisting of an estimated 25 members each, representing all value chain stakeholders – public and private. The project directly benefits women farmers by targeting at least 40% female farmers. The project will result in increased awareness of climate vulnerabilities of crops and livestock, improved climate risk management, collaboration among stakeholders, and the establishment of informal and formal partnerships for production and commercialization. Farmer representatives are requested to ensure 50% female representation.
- At least 4,130 women on dryland farms and in irrigation schemes across the 137 climate-vulnerable wards will receive women-targeted training under a financial empowerment training programme in business development and organizational management, conflict resolution, and gender equality. The project directly benefits women farmers by targeting at least 50% female farmers.
