farmers, for a total of 408,995 people through implementation of gender-equitable climate-resilient agricultural practices and water security management strategies and activities to respond to climate change and variability.

### Climate Change Impacts

The project will address the impacts of climate change on smallholder livelihoods in the priority districts. These livelihoods have suffered from declining productivity and increasing exposure to climate change impacts, primarily a high degree of variability in rainfall. Investments in assets, climate-resilient agricultural skills, multi-stakeholder partnerships, and institutional and farmer capacities for development will be made at the district level. The project will also support the establishment of 15 Catchment Councils, enhancing yielded enhanced incomes and resulting in reduced vulnerabilities to the impacts of climate change. Community-based interventions will be implemented through a participatory approach that ensures the sustainability of the project’s outputs and the sustainability of the impact beyond the project. These organizations will receive climate, weather and agricultural advisories prepared by AGRITEK staff at the national level as well as district-level extension officers. It is estimated that 543,630 people depend on agricultural livelihoods in the priority wards; they will benefit directly from timely climate and weather information services.

### Project Outputs

The project builds the technical capacities of MEWIC (particularly NURWA, MfD and Catchment Councils) and MAARR (particularly AGRITEK, DRSS & DOE) to assess climate risks and adaptation needs for smallholder livelihoods, agricultural practices and systems. The project will also build the capacity of 15 district-level Catchment Councils and 315 local level extension programs and policies. All AGRITEK staff at national, provincial and district level will be trained to support DSS in implementing climate-resilient agricultural practices and systems through Farmer Field Schools (FFS) supported by AGRITEK. The project will also build the capacity of 200 local level extension managers as well as 60 members from the Catchment and Sub-Catchment Councils will be trained in hydro-meteorological services, climate-resilient agricultural practices and systems through Farmer Field Schools (FFS) supported by AGRITEK. In total, the project will benefit at least 315 government staff directly (local district, provincial, and national) with capacities to implement gender-equitable, climate-resilient solutions for water security and sustainable agriculture.

### Reliable Water Supply

A reliable supply of water, together with farmer-based water management and governance structures (both women and men as leaders and decision makers), enables smallholders to plan and manage water resources effectively to offset climate risk. The project will support the development of a reliable water supply system for 500 households in each priority ward through scheduling and supply conflicts, and finance operations and maintenance of their irrigation systems. IMCL, representing 3,900 households, will be trained on water management practices and technologies, including rainwater harvesting, water storage and experience to an additional 60 IMCL beyond the 21 directly benefiting from this project.

### More Secure Access to Water

With more secure access to irrigation (Output 1.0), along with the adoption of climate-resilient agricultural practices (Output 3.0) and improved governance structures (Output 4.0), smallholder farmers will be better able to adaptively respond to evolving climate variability. The project will ensure that approximately 6,900 dryland leaf farmers and 630 irrigation leaf farmers and their households in the priority wards will benefit from climate-resilient agricultural practices and systems through Farmer Field Schools (FFS) supported by AGRITEK. FFS have proven to be important for awareness raising on climate change adaptation strategies and technologies, including climate-resilient agricultural practices and systems through practical partnerships with value chain stakeholders joined together in Innovation Platforms (Output 2). These platforms will also provide a forum for the exchange of information and ideas among farmers and other stakeholders.

### Multi-stakeholder Innovation Platforms

Multi-stakeholder Innovation Platforms (Output 2) will strengthen access to finance, and markets, based on value chain improvement strategies and practical partnerships developed by IP stakeholders. Training in business skills, particularly with

## women farmers, will ensure that climate-resilient products are marketed successfully. This will help farmers and their households to become more resilient to climate change. With greater and more sustainable income, farmers' households and communities can better cope with shocks and stresses from climate change.

In an environment of increasing variable rainfall, access to more accurate and dependable weather and climate information is critical for farmers to make informed decisions about when to plant or harvest crops. Climate advisories based on enhanced weather and climate information will permit smallholders to effectively plan for and manage climate risk (Output 3). Installation of solar-powered irrigation systems in the irrigated schemes will reduce reliance on diesel generators, which are often unreliable and expensive.

Farmers, in a participatory co-processing work with key government institutions, will acquire the technical capacities, tools and resources needed to adopt and implement climate-resilient agricultural practices. This will be achieved through training around climate-resilient agricultural production. With increased planning skills, farmers will be able to prepare their operations and respond more effectively to weather and climate variability.

### E.2.3. Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

The project supports measures to enhance collaboration and knowledge sharing on climate change adaptation between key institutions and stakeholders so that improved communication and more coordinated sharing of data and information can more easily be achieved in support of decision-making by smallholder farmers, service providers and other relevant decision makers. The project also seeks to strengthen the systematic documenting and sharing of information through ICT/OS systems to support climate change adaptation. This will include the development of a knowledge management system and information sharing guidelines, training in effective organizational knowledge management systems and advanced data analysis. The project will also seek to identify and promote best practices that can be used for the identification, certification and best practices that can promote resilient agricultural livelihoods across other areas of the country, as well as in the SADC region. 182. This project specifically supports the implementation of adaptation priorities identified in Zimbabwe’s Nationally Determined Contribution by directly promoting adapted crop and livestock development and climate smart agricultural practices; increased resilience to climate change impacts on livelihoods and food security; and reduced vulnerability to other risks of losses in crops, livestock and agricultural income. It also contributes to the implementation of the National Climate Change Action Plan (NCCAP) 2015-2030, which aims at: a) increasing access to climate information services and increased agriculture specialization according to the constraints and opportunities of Agro-Ecological Regions; The project also quantifies the impact of climate change on agricultural productivity in Zimbabwe using the Climate Change Vulnerability Index (CCVI 2018) with actions aimed at supporting ZimAsset priorities for a revitalizing agriculture and the agro-processing value chain; b) infrastructure development, particularly in relation to the water subsector; c) unlocking the potential of small and medium enterprises (SMEs), including women-owned SMEs, through the implementation of the National Strategy on Climate Policy (2017), which aims to achieve coordinated delivery of strategic responses to climate change among GIZ departments, government agencies, civil society organizations, international development partners, national NGOs, international donors and private sector actors. Responses to the agriculture and water sectors include: integration of climate change adaptation into agricultural extension services; promotion of climate-smart agriculture (CSA) practices in existing agriculture, including adequate assessment of irrigation potential and irrigation demand under climate change; and promotion of sustainable land-use systems in line with principles of Climate-resilient Land Use (CRLU).

183. This project will support the implementation of evidence-based decision making for planning, policy and investments in rain-fed and irrigated agriculture. The ZBRF has already supported the development of a national strategic resilience framework, as well as the revision of an agricultural extension policy and strategy. This project will support the implementation of this framework by: a) supporting the identification and validation of successful approaches to reducing climate vulnerabilities through climate-proofing of irrigation systems and smallerholder agro-enterprises; b) developing a decision-making tool to be deployed at multiple levels, including partners on the Innovation Platforms from the public and private sectors.

E.3 Sustainable Development Potential

### E.3.1 Environmental, social and economic co-benefits, including gender-sensitive development impact

#### Environmental co-benefits:

Smallerholder agriculture in northern Zimbabwe has become increasingly maladaptive with increasing climate change. With increasing temperatures, yields of staple crops are declining. Extreme weather events have resulted in massive erosion as the soil is unable to hold onto the soil until it is able to absorb it, resulting in soil transport to other areas and loss. In southern Zimbabwe, this has resulted in sediments choking stream channels and the progressive elevation of stream beds. Erosion also results in a reduction in water availability for irrigation and livestock watering. The majority of smallholders often feel forced to clear other land to take advantage of its initial fertility. Without human intervention, lands once covered with vegetation will be lost forever as they are unable to recover their soil structure, nutrient growth and the need for farmyard i.e., farmers may return to farm once eroded land before it has fully recovered its fertility.

This project will build on and strengthen current conservation agriculture practices to adapt agro-ecosystems to increasing climate change. This will be done by encouraging and supporting smallholders in the use of a range of soil conservation techniques such as contour ploughing, gully plugs, etc., through training in Farmer Field Schools. With control of water movement onto and across farm fields, soil fertility can recover with appropriate cropping and livestock systems. These will include the use of climate adapted crop varieties as part of an integrated crop-livestock system that will reduce soil erosion and improve soil structure and nutrient cycling to prevent or slow down soil moisture loss. Climate-resilient crop varieties will also be used in multi-cropping systems, as well as in integrated crop- livestock systems. This project will also include the use of appropriate livestock breeds that are adapted to the climate and additional organic inputs from livestock. With the increased stability in the agro-ecosystem, soil biodiversity will recover, and beneficial organisms such as earthworms will be encouraged. The project will also include the use of solar-powered pump and distribution. This project will support solar-powered pumping installations on revitalized irrigation schemes, financed by GCF, which will provide water for livestock watering and crop irrigation. The project will also include the use of a social fund that has taken the risks associated with both water provision and agricultural interventions into account; and the Environmental and Social Management Framework has been developed to both avoid and mitigate these risks, promote enabling regulatory frameworks and ensure transparency.

#### Social and gender co-benefits:

This project will strengthen community resilience through increased food security (higher yields), improved health and productivity of livestock and enhanced social cohesion. The project builds social capital by strengthening irrigation Management Committees through training that strengthens their capacities to function as productive community-based organizations, including climate-adapted operations and maintenance of irrigation systems, project planning and monitoring methods, and the development of a financial management system for the sustainable operation of G&M Social capital is also strengthened through FFS, as cohorts of lead farmer trainees work and learn together over two seasons. This will result in increased social cohesion within communities and improved gender equality by increasing farmer-farmer learning interactions. Multi-stakeholder Innovation Platforms enhance social capital by bringing a variety of value chain stakeholders together to share knowledge, establish commodity production and access to markets and implement action plans in pursuit of agreed goals and objectives.

This project will help transform existing gender norms around women’s capacity to manage soil, water, and biomass resources. The project will also include the use of appropriate livestock breeds that are adapted to the climate and additional organic inputs from livestock. This project recognizes the importance of addressing underlying gender norms and constraints


---
**Page 72**
---

### Economic co-benefits

The economic co-benefits of the project include potentially greater income, stronger assets, job and enterprise creation, and increased farm productivity from adoption of climate-resilient agricultural practices, improved water security, and value chain partnerships. Project support to smallholders for climate-resilient agriculture results in increased income and enables participation in value chains. The project will generate an estimated economic return, for the 10-year period, of US$48.4 million (over the life of the project). The project will increase economic empowerment of smallholders and their households and spur enterprise development among farmer organizations in the communities. The project's activities in support of value-chain strengthening include investments in infrastructure such as water harvesting systems, irrigation schemes, and storage facilities to withstand downswings and enable job creation across the value-chains. The investments in irrigation schemes also contribute to community resilience by reducing vulnerability to droughts and floods. The project will enhance inclusive engagement with smallholder enterprises, and improve the overall wealth and resilience of the beneficiary communities.

The project will yield indirect economic co-benefits at the macro-level derived from the contribution to livelihood and food security for smallholders in the region through increased agricultural productivity and improved water security. Adoption and application of climate-resilient agricultural practices should lead to higher yields and potentially incomes that may reduce the need for migration, which is a major driver of poverty in the region. The project will also contribute to the macro-economic stability by revitalization of irrigation schemes, enhanced soil moisture management, and efficient water use; the socio-economic status of the smallholder communities of southern Zimbabwe will be enhanced from strengthening of market-based partnerships and access to markets.

E.4. Needs of the Recipient

### Vulnerability and financing needs of the beneficiary country and population

#### E.4.1. Vulnerability

194. Zimbabwe continues to experience deteriorating economic circumstances. The country suffered its greatest annual decline in GDP in history after the financial crisis of 2008 and a prolonged period of hyperinflation.[5] This has resulted in deteriorating quality of life for most citizens, particularly those living below the poverty line, and is reflected in the vulnerability of the rural economy. The economy grew slightly between 2009 and 2011, mainly because of price stability due to the introduction of a multi-currency system in April 2009.[6] However, this growth was not sustained as inflation rose again from mid-2011 until early 2014.[7] The financial sector is characterized by low liquidity and non-performing loans.[8] Credit is increasingly difficult and expensive to obtain. The informal economy has grown in importance since the introduction of the multi-currency system, but the private sector is best described as fragmented, as it recovers from sharp economic decline, spurred on by a ban on fresh produce imports.[9] The majority of the rural population is not effectively integrated into formal value chains and participates in inefficient informal markets. This has led to increased vulnerability and limited capacity for impact mitigation and adaptation to climate changes of mass, mainly from Zambia.

195. An increase in the frequency and severity of extreme events, such as droughts, floods, and severe dry spells or floods, is causing significant damage to infrastructure and livelihoods and is limiting economic growth in the absence of adaptive measures in the agriculture sector. Annual GDP significantly declined in the drought years of 1983, 1987, 1992 and 1995[10] and is expected to further decline in 2016 due to a severe drought.[11] The agricultural sector accounts for about 40% of GDP. Between March 2016 and March 2017, GoZ provided food relief to nearly two million people affected by drought at an estimated cost of US$251 million[12]. The impact of the drought systemically destroys communities' assets and their capacities to build climate risk management into their livelihoods.

196. The vast majority of farmers practicing rain-fed agriculture in southern Zimbabwe are likely to experience significant deteriorations in crop yields, and mid-seas dry spells are projected to increase in frequency and intensity in southern and semi-arid regions.


#### E.4.2. Financing needs

areas under climate change10. Rainfall is one of the most critical factors determining the production of maize, the staple food crop in southern Zimbabwe. Increasing temperatures and rainfall variability, leading to reduced water availability, are causing mass migration from rural areas to urban centers. The number of people living below the poverty line has increased by a security of over 3.9 million people (or 700,000 households)11. Climate-induced water stress is the absence of irrigation technologies and infrastructure in many parts of Zimbabwe. This results in reduced crop yields and food insecurity for small farmers on stream-bank and in-stream cultivation in search of sufficient soil moisture. This increases in crisis rather than a long-term problem.

Yield reductions in crops, particularly cash crops, will significantly reduce household and national-level revenues, incomes and employment12. The financial losses incurred from cash crop yield reductions will substantially undermine rural livelihoods, as there is no other income source to fall back on. These include the loss of food security, access to health care, education etc.13. Increasing temperatures and variable precipitation are causing droughts that also pose significant and particular risks for women in Zimbabwe. Women are disproportionately affected by climate change. Zimbabwe's demographic data published by the National Statistical Agency shows that more women than men are poor and live in rural areas where livelihoods are intimately linked with natural resources. Women have less access to land, water, and other productive assets such as livestock, tools, seeds, information, finance, market, and cultural, social and economic roles equip women with the least capacity to cope or prepare for climate change impacts14. The lack of access to these resources means that women are more vulnerable than men in terms of resources which from important decision and policy-making institutions that govern the natural resources on which their livelihoods depend on. Governance and decision-making structures at all levels are still dominated by men. This is also observed in the livestock sector, where women have less access to land, water, tools, seeds, information, finance, markets and natural resources in Zimbabwe15. Further, the impacts of climate change will continue to widen the gap between and exacerbate existing inequalities in terms of gender, age, race, ethnicity, disability, and social class. Women are more vulnerable than men with respect to access to water supplies and fuel wood, especially in rural areas which increases the distances that women and girls have to walk to collect these resources. This is also observed in the livestock sector where women have less access to land on which they would have available to invest in themselves, and also increases security risks. Women are also particularly vulnerable to the shock of climate change because of their role as primary caregivers for children and the elderly16. The evidence suggests a shift in livelihood strategies towards tree farming (more resilient to climate variability), led to higher alcoholism and increase in


---
**Page 75**
---

domestic violence and abuse of women. The vulnerability of women to climate change in Zimbabwe is succinctly described in Table 5 of the Gender Analysis and Action Plan found in Annex III.

**200.** The project will invest in adaptive capacities of smallholder farmers in southern Zimbabwe, focusing on gender equal access to resources and services, and addressing the interlinked drivers and impacts on livelihoods and water security. Through investments in assets, skills, knowledge, and capacities for adaptive livelihood planning and implementation, the project will enhance the resilience of vulnerable households and communities to climate change. The project will also ensure that these benefits, Climate-risk-informed planning, innovation, and improved knowledge and learning will create an enabling environment for long-term adaptive capacities of the vulnerable and poor smallholder communities in southern Zimbabwe.

**201.** The agricultural sector in Zimbabwe accounts for about 20% of GDP[35], is the largest single source of export earnings (estimated at about 40%)[36]; contributes between 25 and 30% to formal employment[37]; and accounts for about 60% of raw


---
**Page 76**
---

materials for the country’s largely agro-based industries[10][11]. Agriculture is also the basis of the direct and indirect livelihoods of almost 70% of the population[12]. Increasing temperatures and rainfall variability, leading to increasing aridity of lands and reduced water availability, are expected to have a significant impact on agricultural productivity in Zimbabwe (ZimAFRICA[13]). Due to its heavy dependence on rainfall, a reduction in precipitation negatively affects GDP103. Exposure to climate change is also likely to reduce the effectiveness of development gains invested into the GIZ donors, and communities; as a result, generation of fiscal resources is weak.

2. The impact of limited fiscal capacity on the principle departments involved in building climate resilience: MSU, ZINWA and ADDA. These three departments are responsible for providing services that include weather monitoring, hydrological monitoring infrastructure to gain an accurate and timely picture of climate risks in agricultural livelihoods. The cost of site identification, installation and maintenance of these stations is high[14]. In addition, there is a lack of adequate financial capacity of these departments as it is needed funding to upgrade the gauging stations and process the information gathered (gathering both analysis and dissemination). AGRIEX seeks the financial capital to invest in training courses for Extension staff on climate change. The cost of this training is high[15]. In addition, there are limited resources available within a business. There is scope for improving training; as necessary baseline investments are in place: God has invested in developing Agriculture and Water Sector Development Plans (SWD) and the Zimbabwe National Climate Change Strategy. However, limited fiscal space; most training centres have devised means to self-sustain, reflecting the drive of GIZ institutions. However, there is a need for more investment in climate change training as well as capacity building on climate change strategies among extension staff and lead-farmers.

3. Zimbabwe’s agriculture and water sectors have very well-developed institutional structures to deliver services at every level; however, this structure has a lack of implementation of a coordinated response to climate change. Essentially the management of multiple stakeholders across the water and agricultural sector, whose aggregate activities, if effectively coordinated, could provide an effective response to climate change. The coordination is hampered by the existence of institutions, private companies, IMFs and farmer associations. A lack of coordination by government service providers contributes to inadequate service provision to smallholders. Existing resources, technical capacity and operational challenges in key departments are not sufficient for a comprehensive response to climate change[16]. This is further complicated by the fact that the coordination barrier is mirrored at subnational and district operational levels; where the same institutions are represented. Weak horizontal linkages between government service providers and other stakeholders also contribute to inadequate service delivery-departments. This is particularly pertinent in the climate information system, where a number of government departments, including MSU, ZINWA, ADDA, DZM, GIZ, etc., have overlapping responsibilities at different levels in the generation, processing, and dissemination of climate/weather products and agriculture advisories.

4. Changes in climate require community institutions’ arrangements to be adaptive and responsive; building climate risk management capacity is a key element for this. However, there are significant gaps between the amount of information available for decisions on crops and agricultural practices[17]. This does not equip farmers to adequately respond to climate change impacts. This presents a significant barrier at the community institutional level, and exacerbates problems such as poor management and

### Country Ownership

Beneficiary country (ies) ownership of, and capacity to implement, a funded project or programme

#### E.S.1 Existence of a national climate strategy and coherence with existing plans and policies, including NAMAs, NAPs, etc.

206. Zimbabwe's Nationally Determined Contribution: Zimbabwe’s Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) highlights the agriculture sector as the country's core vulnerability to climate change[1]. Adaptation in the agricultural sector is stated as a “national priority” and the Government of Zimbabwe has identified four key areas that it proposes how contributes to the Government of Zimbabwe’s priorities outlined in the NDC, as follows:

- Strengthening capacities to generate new forms of empirical knowledge, technologies (including conservation agriculture) and indigenous crop varieties that can help meet climate challenges
- Promoting the use of indigenous and scientific knowledge on drought tolerant crop types and varieties and indigenous natural resources for food security
- Developing framework for sustainable intensification and commercialization of agriculture at different scales across agro ecosystems
- Strengthening management of water resources and irrigation in the face of climate change by:
  - Promoting and supporting water harvesting as a climate change adaptation strategy.
  - Developing and implementing drought-resistant crop varieties and indigenous natural resources.
  - Strengthening and intensifying monitoring systems for hydro-meteorological parameters.
  - Promoting and supporting integrated water resource management.
  - Strengthening institutional capacity, research and extension for integrated water resources management.
  - Strengthening water and moisture conservation initiatives.
  - Strengthening the capacity of the national hydrological and hydroligical services to provide climate data timely.

The proposed project is prioritized for inclusion in Zimbabwe’s GCF Country Work Programme, currently under development.

207. Climate change considerations are increasingly mainstreamed into new policy in Zimbabwe, with the Renewable Energy Policy that is currently being finalized (see below) and several sectoral policies and strategies as examples: these include the National Climate Change Response Strategy (NCCRS), the National Water Policy, the National Science, Technology and Innovation Policy 2012, The Water Policy, the Zimbabwe Agricultural Policy and Investment Plan, the Irrigation Sector Development Strategy, and the Renewable Energy Policy.

208. While the National Climate Policy, approved in April 2017 (see below), provides a overall national framework for climate change, there are practical coordination challenges to delivering a coordinated response to climate change impacts.

National Climate Change Response Strategy: The National Climate Change Response Strategy (NCCRS) (2014) provides the strategic direction and policy framework for climate change action in Zimbabwe. It aims to achieve delivery of strategic responses to climate change adaptation into planning across sectors and institutional levels in a coordinated manner, through multi-stakeholder engagement and collaboration. The NCCRS is underpinned by five key pillars: improved water resources management; sustainable agriculture; strengthened capacity of agricultural support services; and increased agriculture specialization according to AERs. It also highlights the role of technology and strengthening mechanisms for commodity exchange, trading and marketing.

National Water Policy: Zimbabwe’s National Water Policy (2014) provides a strategic direction and policy framework for climate change action in Zimbabwe. It aims to achieve delivery of strategic responses to climate change among key departments and from them to guide effective implementation with key stakeholders, including research institutes, NGOs, private sector, communities and other relevant institutions. The policy is underpinned by five pillars: improved water resources management; sustainable agriculture; strengthened capacity of agricultural support services; increased agriculture specialization according to AERs; and adaptive capacity to climate change109. Responses in the sectors include: integration of climate change analyses into the planning process for all major development projects; establishment of a national climate change information system; assessment of irrigation potential and irrigation demand under climate change; and promotion of sustainable land-use systems in the water sector.

211. Renewable Energy Policy: Zimbabwe is developing a Renewable Energy Policy (REP) due to be presented for cabinet approval in 2018. The policy will advocate for a larger share of renewables in the national energy mix, incentives for renewable energy projects, and the development of a regulatory framework for the sector. It also aims to identify priority areas that are not yet reached by the grid in the near future (as mapped out in the Rural Energy Master Plan 2017) and may include solar home systems, biogas, small hydroelectric power plants, wind energy, biomass, and geothermal.

Environmental Management Act: The Environmental Management Act (EMA) of 2002 is a framework legislation that establishes coordinated legal and institutional mechanisms to improve national capacity for management of the environment across sectors110. It provides for the Environmental Management Agency to manage all environmental issues in the country, including those related to climate change. The EMA also provides for the establishment of an Environment Fund at central and district levels. It includes a provision for the establishment of an Environment Fund, used to provide environmental management services and support to communities and other stakeholders.

National Adaptation Plan: Zimbabwe is currently in the process of developing a National Adaptation Plan with readiness funding from the GCF. UNDP supports the stocktaking process assessing climate vulnerabilities and risks as well as adaptation


---
**Page 79**
---

options, led by the Environmental Management Agency, in cooperation with the MULWRR. A final stocktaking report is expected in the first quarter of 2018, which is expected to inform the development of the final NAP document, anticipated by the end of 2018.

### Zimbabwe Agricultural Investment Plan (ZAPI) (2013-2018)

This proposal is in line and contributes to the ZAPI (2013-2018), which is the shared national framework for coordinating public, private and development partners’ investment into the agricultural sector. The ZAPI aims at improving the productivity, profitability, sustainability, resilience, competitiveness and competitiveness of Zimbabwean agriculture that focuses on client and consumer needs through building capacities of farmers and other stakeholders in the value chain. The ZAPI is aligned with the UNDP’s strategic focus on climate change alignment. ZAPI’s ‘strategic thrust’ focuses on supporting farmers to become an integral part of domestic and export value chains.

#### E.5.2. Capacity of accredited entities and executing entities to deliver

**Experience and track record of the accredited entity**

ZAP, which is a UNDP-supported project, has been at the forefront of climate change grants for developing countries, with a current portfolio of US$14.18 billion in grant-financed mitigation and adaptation projects in over 140 countries, supported by co-financing of US$6.7 billion. ZAP has worked closely with UNDP’s global thematic areas (GTA) to support the development of climate change strategies and action plans closely with the GtC for the last four decades in supporting its development efforts. UNDP has collaborated with GoZ in various projects, including the Zimbabwe National Climate Change Strategy and Action Plan (NCCSAP), which was developed under the environmental management with technical and capacity building support. UNDP has focused on capacity building, policy and community interventions, targeting the poorest and most marginalized women, men, girls and boys. The UNDP Zimbabwe Country Office is currently implementing the NCCSAP, which is a joint project between the GoZ and UNDP, under which currently manages five programs with GoZ using resources committed by bilateral, multilateral and global funds such as the Global Environment Facility (GEF).

The proposed project is built on ZAP’s Zimbabwe’s ext track record in implementing climate change adaptation, social protection and disaster risk management work. This includes the UNDP-supported Coping with Drought Project in Chiredzi and Southern Districts, which was implemented by the Ministry of Water Resources, Irrigation and Energy (MoWRIE) and UNDP and managed by MULWRR in management arrangement with UNDP. UNDP has supported government in formulating key national policies such as the National Climate Change Response Strategic Plan, the National Climate Policy, the Renewable Energy Policy, the Climate Policy and others.

**Experience and track record of the Executing Entity: MULWRR**

MULWRR has coordinated a number of national climate change projects. The most recent is the Zimbabwe National Climate Change Strategy and Action Plan (NCCSAP), which is one of the national projects that the MULWRR has coordinated include the development of the NCCSAP, the National Climate Policy, and the Coping with Drought and Climate Change and the Scaling Up Adaptation projects. Currently, the Ministry is implementing a UNDP funded project in Chiredzi District, which is being implemented by the Ministry of Water Resources, Irrigation and Energy (MoWRIE), which is expected to deliver results aligned with the NCCSAP.

As part of the requirements under the UNHPRD Harmonized Approach to Cash Transfers (HACT) framework, a capacity assessment was conducted in 2017. The assessment identified operational shortfalls that need to be addressed for effective implementation of this GCF project. Based on the assessment done on capacity for Programme Management,

### E.S.3 Engagement with NGOs, civil society organizations and other relevant stakeholders

219 The Government of Zimbabwe, led by the NDA, has been very involved in the crafting of this proposal – both in terms of strategy decision making, feedback and validation through a series of consultations as well as through active participation in the fieldwork phase leading to the feasibility study and the proposal. The following has been undertaken since September 2015 in developing the proposed project:

- A series of consultations with government officials, donor organizations, inter-governmental organizations, private companies, research institutions, NGOs (for more details), and other stakeholders were carried out.
- Interviews with research institutions, NGOs and CWDs were carried out, with a focus on climate change adaptation and resilience.
- Key stakeholder engagement was conducted using the Stakeholder Engagement Annex X1B.2 for more details).
- Interviews with research institutions, NGOs and CWDs were carried out, with a focus on climate change adaptation and resilience.

220 The following key stakeholders were consulted:
- Climate change experts from government, civil society, the private sector and research institutions.
- The GEF (the Global Environment Facility) and other donors such as USAID, DFID, SIDA, CIDA, KfW, ADB, IDA, and others.
- Key NGOs that have been involved in climate change adaptation projects in Zimbabwe include Oxfam, SAFIRE, World Vision, SNV, CSEK, Practical Action and Agricultural Partnership Trust. Research institutions such as the University of Zimbabwe, the University of Pretoria, the University of South Africa were consulted along with donors such as USAID on major resilience building projects and ZIMF stakeholders, including MULAWIR, 2017 PRAI, CIDA, SIDA, KfW, ADB, IDA, DFID, CIDA, SNV, Practical Action, etc.

221 Interviews with senior government officials, including directors and senior members of departments at national headquarters, who provided further guidance on ideas for the proposal.

222 Meetings and validation workshops between the Think Tank established by the Ministry of Lands, Agriculture, Water and Rural Resettlement (MLAWR) and other key stakeholders such as climate change experts from government, civil society, the private sector and research institutions, to spearhead the process of identifying and developing projects for funding by the GEF. The Think Tank was established in 2015 with representatives from MLAWR, CWDs, SNV, Oxfam, SAFIRE, etc., in southern Zimbabwe, and the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) to present progress in project development and obtain feedback on the draft proposal. This process was repeated several times by the Think Tank for validation of ideas with GEF stakeholders. Key stakeholders included the Ministry of Lands, Agriculture and Rural Resettlement with participation namely from the DoI and AGRIATRS as well as representatives from MLAWR and ZINWA.

223 Interviews were conducted in 147 irrigation schemes and communities, of which 29 were irrigated farming communities, where consultations with farmers and other key stakeholders such as AGRIEXTENSION Officers and provincial engineers, were undertaken. Key informant information from farmers and CWDs were based on interviews with the leadership of the irrigation schemes and communities. The Think Tank was established in 2015 to provide guidance for the project. Farmers were encouraged to participate in the discussions, an approach which yielded important field-based information and evidence.

224 A total of 147 government officials, resource persons and NGOs were consulted from end 2015 to September 2017. Visits were made by the Think Tank to the Ministry of Lands, Agriculture, Water and Rural Resettlement (MLAWR), Department of Irrigation and Drainage, Department of Environment Management, Department of Forestry, Department of Land Reform, Agriculture and Rural Resettlement, as well as CRED and UNDP consultants. The irrigation schemes and communities were chosen based on the criteria set by MLAWR, based on their potential relevance to climate change adaptation and the adoption of climate-resilient agricultural practices and irrigation technologies, as well as receiving no or limited donor activity, to prevent duplication of efforts.

# E. Efficiency and Effectiveness

## Economic and, if appropriate, financial soundness of the project/programme

### Adequacy of the financial structure

GCF grants are critical for this project given the country's current economic situation. While some government funds have been allocated to addressing climate change, the current public expenditure budget of the Government of Zimbabwe is limited. This budget is insufficient to move smallholder farmers to climate resilient and improved livelihoods. According to an IMF report titled "Zimbabwe: Staff Report for the 2017 Article IV Consultation – Debt Sustainability Analysis," June 2017, the total public debt as a share of GDP was 44.8% of GDP, with 70% in arrears (overdue payments). Private external debt stood at 31% of its GDP as of end-2016, resulting in a total external debt-to-GDP ratio of 59%, one of the highest levels among developing countries. External debt ratios are expected to increase further due to the country's inability to service its debt and the lack of access to concessional financing. The government is unable to increase investments into climate resilient agriculture, which not only impacts farmers' income but also negatively


---
**Page 82**
---

229\. Financial analysis of proposed activities and investments under outputs 1 and 2 of this project over a 25-year period results in a positive Financial Net Present Value (FNPV) of USD 160 million and a positive Internal Rate of Return (IRR) of 20.1% which is greater than the discount rate of 8%. The financial returns are sensitive to changes in the assumptions made under outputs 1 and 2 and are computed to be financially viable and sustainable for the long-term. Sensitivity analysis of financial returns shows that the project can withstand a 50% increase or decrease in the assumed prices, as well as a 30% increase in costs. The financial returns still show positive if revenues decrease by 20% or costs increase by 20%. Activities under output 3 are more sensitive to changes in assumptions and therefore require further analysis.

230\. Given that public funding from such GCF are scarce and need to be used only if they bring additionality to the project, an assessment was conducted to complete the financial analysis under outputs 1 and 2 in the absence of proposed GCF funds. The results show that grants, which form more than 50% of the total cost of the project, are not financially viable as the only alternative to these smaller farmers is to borrow from local banks or Microfinance Institutions (MFI) for investing in irrigation systems and water storage infrastructure. These institutions charge interest rates ranging between 12% and 36%, at times as high as 20% per month, as reported by studies. In 2016, Zimbabwe’s Central Bank, the Reserve Bank of Zimbabwe (RBZ), introduced a micro-loan scheme for smallholder farmers with an interest rate of 8%. However, this loan is only available to those who have collateral and has a maximum term of five years. The interest rates charged by MFIs are still very high which still works out to 120% of interest per annum. To be on a conservative basis, we use a micro-loan interest rate of 60%. For example, if the cost of an irrigation system is USD 500 and the loan term is five years with a repayment period of six months, then the monthly repayment would be USD 17.50. If the FNPV value is negative, and IRR is less than WACC/hurdle rate (which is now computed using micro-loan interest rates of 60% for the local currency-denominated loans) and government co-financing which is assumed to be a grant to farmers), including that proposed under this project, then it would not be financially viable. The project will also bring additionality in terms of increased yields and brings additionally to investments under these proposed outputs 1 and 2\. However, the project enhances the cashflowiness of the smallholder farmers by providing them with access to credit through a formal financial institution such as banks or other intermediaries such as women savings and loan associations and MFIs to lower the risk of lending to the farmers and enable access to credit at more reasonable interest rates.

231\. The government of Zimbabwe and the smallholder communities of the vulnerable southern Agro-Ecological Regions have little capacity to meet the additional costs of adaptation. GCF grant resources are necessary to overcome a combination of financial, institutional and technical constraints that prevent smallholder farmers from accessing credit and other services building the resilience of our agro-ecosystems and livelihoods to increasing climate hazards. These include the inability of smallholder farmers to access credit due to lack of collateral; limited access to irrigation systems which increases water volumes and availability of water resources, increasing risks faced by smallholder farmers to the productivity and yield stability of their dryland agro-ecosystems, given increasing drought and mid rainy season dry spells; the inability of smallholders and government institutions to manage water resources in a sustainable manner due to lack of access to information on rainwater management and agricultural planning; and a lack of the coordinated institutional support required by smallholders to carry out the proposed activities under outputs 1 and 2.

232\. Grant funding is necessary to overcome the above barriers to climate-resilient rural livelihoods in southern Zimbabwe by giving key investments in a) climate-resilient irrigation systems of targeted communities; b) strengthening the technical capacities of farmers through training, extension services and access to information on rainwater management and agricultural planning; c) providing smallholder farmers with access to credit for investing in their agroecosystems and water resources for climate resilient crop production that is more diverse, more productive and more

3. In particular, the experience of the Climate Resilience Infrastructure Development Facility (CRIDF), a DFID-funded water infrastructure facility working with the Government of Zimbabwe to improve irrigation schemes, is relevant here. The CRIDF's Kufandara Irrigation Scheme Economic & Financial Analysis and page 88 of the Feasibility Study, later references CRIDF’s Kufandara Irrigation Scheme Economic & Financial Analysis.

1. At the same time, the project will build on, complement and consolidate investments of the ZBRIF and Scaling Up Adaptation (SUA) projects in Zimbabwe, which have already demonstrated the potential for providing technical support to market linkages, replicating and consolidating proven CRA practices, and scaling up and scaling out good practice through community-based irrigation management. The project will also build on the experience gained by CRIDF in developing a base case to ensure cost-effectiveness and impact of irrigation design and management/governance, climate smart agriculture and climate change adaptation.

### Cost-effectiveness

1. The financial and economic analysis accompanying this proposal cite the cost-effectiveness of Farmer Field Schools as a well-tested methodology for training farmers in agricultural innovations and a highly successful means of upscaling new practices to multiple farmers. This is because it provides an opportunity for farmers to learn from each other, share knowledge with neighboring farmers in their communities, ensures efficient uptake of climate resilient agricultural practices and systems at scale.
2. Organizational coordination and collaboration among the various stakeholders involved in the design, implementation and maintenance of rehabilitated irrigation infrastructure and technologies through improved community-based funding of O&M and training of local water users associations (WUAs) will ensure that the project is sustainable over time by involving representative stakeholders of a particular value chain to develop strategies and plans, as well as practical partnerships, increase collaboration among stakeholders and coordination thereby reducing information, investment, and other barriers to climate-resilient value chain development.

### Cost-effectiveness

1. More effective institutional coordination fostered by this project among the different government agencies and departments involved will enhance coherence of their services at field level based on a more integrated approach to generating knowledge gained from research and extension work and making it accessible and relevant to farmers, local staff and policy makers.
2. Detailed consultations and engagements with a multitude of stakeholders were carried out regarding traditional and more modern knowledge of agro-ecological conditions and constraints; climate change trends, patterns and expected impacts; poverty and vulnerability of rural communities; agricultural potential, including existing and promoting climate-resilient crops and

### E.6.3 Financial viability

238. Financial analysis of proposed activities and investments under outputs 1 and 2 of this project over a 25-year period results in a positive Financial Net Present Value (FNPV) of USD 160 Million and a positive Financial Internal Rate of Return (FIRR) of 20%. The financial viability is based on the assumption that all GCF co-financing will be available. Sensitivity analysis made under outputs 1 and 2 are computed to be financially viable and sustainable for the long-term. Sensitivity analysis of financial returns, revenues, costs, and direct investment revenues to project activities under output 3 are not included as estimated costs. The financial returns are still positive if revenues decrease by 20% or costs increase by 20%. Activities under output 4 have no expected financial returns, and direct investment revenues to the project's intended beneficiaries under this output is difficult to be quantified; and hence, activities/investments under this output have not been included in financial analysis.

### E.6.4 Application of best practices

241. A number of good practices and existing efforts from a desk study, site visits to 16 irrigation schemes and multiple consultations with stakeholders, including provincial engineers, AGRITEK Extension Officers and farming communities, finds that a climate-resilient 'restitution' design approach to existing irrigation schemes overcomes challenges faced by previous irrigation investments in Zimbabwe. The approach is based on the experience of CROPS' pilot projects in addressing climate risks. Best practice in designing climate-resilient irrigation, predominantly from the practical experience of CROPS' pilot projects is detailed below. It includes: (i) design of irrigation schemes to withstand extreme weather events such as droughts and flooding; climate-proofing techniques that protect irrigation and related infrastructure should be employed. Methods of climate proofing include: (a) designing for a 10-year flood event in detail at the feasibility study and irrigation sub-basin level.

Operations and Maintenance of Irrigation Schemes

241. The basic premise for O&M sustainability or continuation of irrigation schemes is that income from livestock/pastoral production, crop production, and/or other activities generated by CROPS' pilot projects should be costed into factored into the project budget for the first two years of the project, to allow time for MCA to put appropriate measures in place to eventually fulfill their upkeep responsibilities and operationalize their capacities to manage climate change. Another best practice from CROPS is to establish a maintenance fund that can be used by farmers to pay for the maintenance of irrigation infrastructure and maintenance of commonly used irrigation infrastructure. A key part of a scheme's Constitution, as is the case in CROPS' pilot projects, should include provisions for the establishment of an O&M fund that will be funded on a cost-recovery basis. While project budgets should build in costs to provide an initial buffer for this fund (as part of the O&M budget line), over


---
**Page 85**
---

time, community contributions, made possible by increased incomes from increased yields, will generate enough capital to pay for the upkeep of the irrigation infrastructure, including paying engineers to troubleshoot, repairing faulty equipment, etc. The evidence is presented in a case study on the scaling up of climate-smart agriculture practices in the Upper Tana River Basin Study Farmer Field Schools and adoption by smallholders of Climate-resilient Agriculture practices.

242. Farmer Field Schools are a tried and tested methodology in Zimbabwe to support farmers to increase productivity of their crops and livestock, and to adopt climate-smart agricultural practices. In the Scaling Up Adaptation project, the UNDP/GIZ supported Scaling Up Adaptation project and DRRB-supported projects reveals that building the capacity of farmers to diversify into climate resilient crops and livestock is a key factor in increasing resilience and improving livelihoods while achieving sustainable development results. Farmers are exposed to a range of climate-resilient cropping options, and farmer-managed demonstrations are the best way to introduce new technologies and practices. The Scaling Up Adaptation project has demonstrated that there are no silver bullet solutions for technologies that will not be easily adopted by farmers, because of farmers’ constraining circumstances. These experiences are presented in the feasibility Study Chapter 5, Section 5.2.7 on the evidence base of CSA and DRRB crops and practices, the project drew on the evidence from practical experience at AGRITECH GIZRISI, the CGIAR research institutions such as CIMMYT and ICRAF, the Scaling Up Adaptation project, BDRRB consortia, VUNA and the recently finalized CSA manual developed in a partnership between MURARR, CTCN and Green Impact Trust to identify best practices for CSA.

Innovation Platforms

243. A combination of Farmer Field Schools and Innovation Platforms is considered by AGRITECH staff at national, provincial and district levels as an effective way to support the scaling up of climate-smart agriculture (CSA) in Zimbabwe. Innovation platforms are inserted in the broader context of value chain development involving production, technical assistance, input supply, crop or livestock purchase, processors, micro-credit, research findings, and other factors. Innovation Platforms bring together representatives from all these actors to share information on CSA practices and technologies, which can be adopted more easily, plan more strategically and collaborate more effectively through formal and informal partnerships. Examples of these agreements include the Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) between farmers, input suppliers, processors, micro-credit providers, buyers, micro-credit providers), or participatory innovation development of production practices (farmers, TAs agencies, research institutions, processors, buyers, micro-credit providers). The role of Innovation Platforms in supporting CSA and the importance for value chain development, facilitation of market linkages and the usefulness of Innovation Platforms as a space for learning and access to information on climate-smart agricultural practices and technologies was also identified in the feasibility Study as a highly effective means of linking smallholder farmers with input and output markets, ensuring a stable and profitable income base and access to much needed finance and input at the start of a season.

Value chain information

244. The project draws on the successful experiences of the Participatory Integrated Climate Services for Agriculture approach (PICSA), as well as the expertise of the University of Reading in providing capacity building to roll out the approach. PICSA is an innovative approach that combines climate-smart agricultural practices, weather and climate services, and locally relevant crops, livestock and livelihood options, with the use of participatory tools to aid their decision making. The existing partnership and the lessons learned from the UNDP/GIZ/GRIM Scaling Up Adaptation project on Climate User Information Platforms (CUIPs) in Zimbabwe are also being used as a basis for this approach. The PICSA approach is innovative because it documents the importance of: a) providing basic climate infrastructure in the form of weather stations; b) building capacity of extension officers and other key stakeholders to use the information provided by these stations, c) developing tools that can be usable by farmers (which includes exploring the use of software as well as the development of different approaches to seasonal forecasts).

# F.1 Economic and Financial Analysis

## Economic analysis

The economic feasibility of the project was determined using funds flow from small-scale farmers who will benefit from the project. Financial and gross margin analyses were conducted to assess the financial returns of the project interventions for the project lifetime.

### 246. Analysis of the project was carried out in accordance with the Guidelines for the Economic Analysis of Projects of the United Nations Development Program (UNDP) 2015).

The economic desirability of the investments was determined by computing the economic net present value (ENPV), internal rate of return (IRR), and cost-benefit ratio (CBA) at a discount rate (as recommended in UNDP 2015).

### 246. Discounted fund flow periods vary between 13 and 25 years.

The analysis is based on assumptions at the project sites and data from different agencies in the country. The climate information and climate resilient outputs of the projects are assumed to have a lifespan of 25 years, with an average discount rate of 20%.

### 248. Total cost of the project amounts to USD 47.62 million including co-financing by the government of Zimbabwe.

This excludes the operating and maintenance costs for the interventions over the lifetime of the project. The cost-benefit analysis shows that the total cost of the project is USD 10,538,900 with a benefit-cost ratio (BCR) of 4.62 and an internal rate of return (IRR) of 20%.

### 248. The economic internal rate of return is 20%, which exceeds 10%. Though the internal rate of return is 20% for the base case, there are some assumptions that may not be realistic in the future.

The project will have a positive impact on agricultural production output and it is reasonable to assume that in the future without the project scenario, expected net revenues will reduce because of climate change. The project will prevent all or some of that projected climate change impact on agricultural productivity. Therefore, the economic internal rate of return (IRR) for this project is 20%, which is higher than the discount rate.

### 249. There are sensitive test cases were examined: (i) total cost increased by 20%; and (ii) total benefits decreased by 20%; and (iii) total costs reduced by 15% with a benefit-cost ratio of 3.86, internal rate of return of 17%, and economic feasibility and EIR remains above the minimum threshold.

Results are presented in the Economic Analysis Report in Annex XII.


## Financial analysis

### 250. Financial analysis of the proposed project was carried out in accordance with the "Financial and Economic Analysis of Project Interventions" (Annex I) and "Guidelines for the Economic Analysis of Projects of the United Nations Development Program (UNDP) 2015".

Detailed financial data has been conducted for outputs 1 and 3. Activities under output 1 are more of public good nature, and discounting is not appropriate. The intended beneficiaries under this output are difficult to quantify, and hence activities/inputs under output 3 have not been included in financial analysis.

Financial analysis over a 25-year period results in positive economic returns for the project (ENPV = USD 10,538,900). This is higher than the discount rate of 20% which is higher than WACC/hurdle rate (which is 0% on this case due to GCF grant).

### 250. Investments proposed to be made under output 3 are still uncertain.

The financial analysis conducted above was performed primarily to assess the impact of lower-than-assumed revenues or higher-than-estimated costs. The financial analysis shows that ENPV increases by 70% on costs increase by 20%.

### 252. In the case of output 1, which covers nearly 93% of the project cost, the only alternative to these smallholder farmers is to borrow from local Banks or Microfinance Institutions (MFIs) for investing in irrigation infrastructure and climate resilient inputs and market linkage.

Micro-loans in Zimbabwe carry very high interest


---
**Page 88**
---

rates. In 2016, Zimbabwe’s Central Bank, the Reserve Bank of Zimbabwe (RBZ), issued a directive to Banks/NIFs to restrict interest rates on micro-loans to a maximum of 10% per month, which still works out to 120% rate of interest per annum. To be on a commercially viable basis, the interest rate should not exceed 36% per annum. The RBZ directive is applicable for loans that are typically for 2 years [4]. Under these financing terms for capital costs of outputs and 1 and 2, NPV value is negative, and FIRR is way below the required threshold of 10%. This implies that the proposed activities under output 1 and 2 will not be financially viable (which is assumed to be a grant to farmers), indicating that proposed activities under output 1 and 2 are not financially viable if they are financed by microfinance loans. Hence, GO funding brings additionality to investments under these proposed outputs of Level I.

### F.2. Technical Evaluation

#### 253. Identification and selection of technical solutions to mitigate climate change risks to smallholder agriculture in southern Zimbabwe began with: a) consultations with a large number of local stakeholders to capture indigenous knowledge of climate risks and adaptation strategies; b) review of existing literature on climate change impacts, vulnerability assessments, and practical perspectives and economic potential of proposed solutions; c) consultations with technical staff of key government agencies (e.g., the Ministry of Lands, Agriculture, Water and Rural Resettlement (MLAWRR), Department of Research and Specialist Services (DRSSS), the Meteorological Service Department (MDS)), and other relevant government agencies, institutions and technical experts in Zimbabwe; d) consultations with local farmers and extension officers to identify climate risks faced by smallholder farmers of the three targeted provinces of southern Zimbabwe. These consultations and discussions, combined with the information gathered from literature review, were used as a basis for developing a list of potential solutions for inclusion into the project approach as well as a corresponding integrated set of interventions. Building on these foundations, the project design process ensures that project investments and intervention results are technically robust and will be sustained beyond project duration.

The project approach has been designed in close consultation with the Ministry of Lands, Agriculture, Water and Rural Resettlement (MLAWRR) particularly AGRITECH and the Department of Irrigation (DOI), Department of Research and Specialist Services (DRSSS), the Meteorological Service Department (MDS), and other relevant government agencies, institutions and technical experts in Zimbabwe. The project approach was also informed by consultations with local farmers and extension officers to identify climate risks faced by smallholder farmers of the three targeted provinces of southern Zimbabwe.

The findings of each sub-assessment were validated in presentations to the Think Tank and through continuous engagement with key stakeholders including MLAWRR, DOI, DRSSS, MDS, AGRITECH, CIRASAT, CGIAR, ICRISAT, WFP, FAO, and research institutes including CIIMMYT. These engagements included workshops, field visits, and discussions on the project approach as well as a corresponding integrated set of interventions.

Interventions were developed based on lessons learned from practical experience on the ground, namely on strategies for Promotion of Climate-resilient Agriculture (CRA), CRA methodologies, market development for smallholder farmers and climate information systems. Key CGIAR’s consulted included CIIMMYT, ICRISAT, CCAFS, and CCAFS-Water. Other relevant government agencies, institutions and technical experts from other institutions consulted included ICRI SAT and CIMMYT. Intergovernmental organizations such as WFP, FAO and IFAD were consulted on specific areas such as USAID on major resilience building projects and ZRBF stakeholders, including MAWRR, SRFI PMU and ZRBF donors.


---
**Page 89**
---

257. A total of 147 government officials, resource persons and NGOs were consulted from end 2015 to September 2017. Visiting teams to communities were composed of officials from the Climate Change Management Department and Department of Water Resources Development (DWRD) of the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources Protection (MENRP), the Department of Irrigation and Drainage (IDG) and Department of Agricultural, Technical and Extension Services (AGRITECH) of the Ministry of Lands, Agriculture and Rural Resettlement (MLARR). The project team also collaborated with the Zimbabwe Agricultural Research Institute (ZARI), in collaboration with AGRITECH, based on the climate vulnerability assessment and potential for successful adoption of climate-resilient agricultural practices and irrigation technologies.

258. In total, 104 farmers were trained by the project to 35 farming communities, of which 29 were irrigated farming communitas, where consultations with farmers and other key stakeholders, such as AGRITECH Extension Officers and provincial engineers, were undertaken. Farmers participated in the discussion, which yielded important field-based information and evidence.

259. An Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS) assessment (see Section F.1.) has been rigorously carried out to confirm best design and implementation practices to promote climate-resilient agricultural practices and cropping systems, climate information systems, and other elements of the project in the four priority ecological regions I, II, III and V of southern Zimbabwe.

260. All major components of this project – irrigation system revitalisation, climate-resilient agriculture, farmer field schools, innovation platforms and climate information systems - have been designed based on extensive experiences and proven models under previous projects in the same geographical areas (see Annexes A to D). The project will be implemented across four ecological regions IV and V of southern Zimbabwe. For further information on the technical feasibility of these elements, please see the environmental assessment of the project.

### F.3 Environmental, Social Assessment and Mitigation Considerations

261. A Social and Environmental Screening assessment was undertaken to identify and address any potential social and environmental risks that could arise from project activities. Based on the findings of this assessment, an Environmental and Social Management Action Plan (ESMAP) has been developed for the project (see Annexes VIII).

The overall social and environmental risk category for this project is moderate. Specific project risks are listed in Section G below, together with appropriate mitigation measures.

#### Environment

262. The project is likely to have some short-term, small-scale environmental impacts during implementation, but will ultimately have considerable long-term benefits (see Section E.1.).

The envisaged project has three inter-related outputs:

* **Output 1:** Increased access to water for agriculture through climate-resilient irrigation systems and water resource management
* **Output 2:** Scaling up climate-resilient agricultural production and diversification through increased access to climate-resilient inputs, practices, and markets.
* **Output 3:** Improved access to weather, climate and hydrological information for climate-resilient agriculture.

263. Through delivery of the outputs, communities in Southern Zimbabwe will be better equipped to adapt to increasingly harsh climatic conditions by reducing their vulnerability to droughts and floods, while also reducing their demands on the natural environment. Benefits will include increased food security through higher yields, improved weather information and


---
**Page 90**
---

### Early warnings, improved management of water resources, more efficient irrigation practices, improved agricultural outputs through better crop selection and management, improved access to markets.

#### Environmental impacts associated with the project are considered acceptable due to the nature of the interventions, while social benefits will be maximized by ensuring that all communities benefit from the project. The project will also promote inclusion, particularly of women and vulnerable groups. Key considerations include minimizing environmental and social risks, including those related to biodiversity, CDM, but include social inclusion and consultation, sedimentation and erosion control, and health and safety for workers and community.

#### 265. Physical impacts will be primarily associated with construction and installation of the pump, such as barricades, river gauging stations, etc., and the operation of the pump itself. The project design takes into consideration that the infrastructure will be designed to minimize long-term impacts e.g. pumps will be placed out of river beds above flood levels – this not only protects the pumps in times of floods, but also reduces the sedimentation rate, river flow and potential contamination or waterway blockage. In addition, the project design takes into consideration that the pump (SMP) will be installed in a satisfactorily managed.

#### 266. Furthermore, the project makes provision for a compliant register along with a two-tiered Grievance Redress Mechanism consistent with the UNDP’s Stakeholder Response Mechanism: Overview and Guidance (2014) and World Bank Group Safeguards Policies. The Grievance Redress Mechanism established goals and objectives along with eligibility requirements to make a complaint, including that complaints must be made in good faith and not maliciously or vexatiously. It is important to note more importantly, that arbitration will be followed in trying to achieve mutually acceptable resolutions for all parties. The Grievance Redress Mechanism also provides for the establishment of an Office of Anticipation and Integration (OAI) within UNDP. The OAI’s role is not disambiguated by bringing complaints to the attention of CDDA and UNDP. Finally, the Grievance Redress Mechanism will be reviewed on a regular basis with input from the OAI, CDDA, and Environmental Compliance Unit within the Office of Anticipation and Integration within the UNDP should they have any concerns as to corruption, unethical behavior or where they believe their complaint or grievance has not been adequately addressed.

#### 267. Project interventions will be undertaken in areas of Southern Zimbabwe where communities are particularly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change. These communities are largely rural and often have constrained or marginal livelihood opportunities. The project has been designed to address gender inequality and social inclusion concerns, including gender inequality in landholding, access to resources such as land permits and deeply rooted, yet it is estimated that 70% of smallholder farmers are women.

#### 268. The gender analysis undertaken at the project design phase acts as an entry point for gender mainstreaming throughout implementation. It has identified a number of key gender inequalities in agriculture which are currently being implemented, and national statistics, where available. The project design takes into consideration a number of key gender implications including: women’s access to land; women’s access to credit and other financial services; the gendered division of labor; women’s access to and control over environmental resources; and identification of gender equality gaps.

#### Based on the Gender Assessment, the project implementation proposes a number of actions to strengthen gender equality from the outset:

- Develop specific strategies to include and target female farmers, in both male and female-headed households for interventions to ensure equal gender participation;
- Enhance women’s access to credit and other financial services to enable female farmers to understand climate change information and use this to inform farming practices and crop/vegetable choices.


---
**Page 91**
---

c) Build capacities of both male and female farmers in climate smart agriculture production, with a particular focus on providing extension services to both husband and wife, taking into account women's daily routines and promoting both genders' participation.

d) Build capacity of male and female farmers in farming as a business and value addition for irrigation schemes as well as dry land farming.
- Develop initiatives to build capacities of female farmers, in particular, in leadership and marketing skills;
- Advocacy and awareness should be adjusted to most effectively reflect gender-specific differences;
- Address the issue of women's access to resources, which is critical since they are key partners in the co-development of climate information messaging so that they are also reached effectively.
- Access and use gender expertise in the roll-out of the project, including through expert consultancy services, and the leverage of existing networks and partnerships.

e) Inclusion of all stakeholders involved in the project to develop awareness raising/training aimed at drawing attention to the implications of climate change on agriculture.

269. During project implementation qualitative assessments will be conducted on the gender-specific benefits that can be directly associated with the project. This will be incorporated into the annual Project Implementation Report, Mid-term Report, and Final Evaluation Report.
- The Gender Action Plan (GAP) and the Gender Monitoring and Reporting Framework gender quality are included in the Gender Action Plan. The complete Gender Assessment and Action Plan can be found in Annex Xic.

The NIM Guidelines were developed and adopted by UNDP, and are fully compliant with UNDP's procurement and financial management rules and regulations.
- The National Implementing Modality (NIM), also referred to as the National Executing Entity in UNDP terminology, is required to implement the project in compliance with UNDP rules and regulations, policies and procedures (including the NIM Guidelines). In legal terms, this is ensured through the national Government's signature of the UNDP Standard Basic Assistance Agreement (SBA) and the UNDP Financial Rules and Regulations. The National Implementing Modality (NIM), as well as other UNDP documents, are legally binding on the executing entity. All parties involved in the project must comply with these documents. Failure to do so may result in legal action against the executing entity. Both of these documents require compliance. Prior to signature of the project document, all national Executing Entity like MIUAWRR


---
**Page 92**
---

need to have undergone a Harmonized Approach to Cash Transfer (HACT), assessment by UNDP to assess capacities to implement the project. During implementation, UNDP will provide oversight and quality assurance in accordance with its policies and procedures. The HACT assessment will be conducted at least once during the project period and may include additional assessments as agreed with the GCF. This may include, but is not limited to monitoring missions, spot checks, facilitation and participation in project board meetings, quarterly or annual implementation reviews, and audits at project level or at Executing Entity level on the resources received from UNDP.

275. The Harmonized Approach to Cash Transfer (HACT) framework consists of four processes: (1) macro assessments; (2) micro assessments; (3) project monitoring and evaluation; and (4) audit and quality assurance. These processes are supported by regular on-site reviews (spot checks), programme monitoring, scheduled audits and special audits. During micro-assessment, there can be weaknesses identified for which actions are required to address the gaps. When a spot check finds that the gaps are not addressed in time, the project board will be informed of this situation. The results from the spot check and the action plan by the Executing Entity will have to be reviewed if necessary. All details are available here: https://undg.org/wp-content/uploads/2018/03/HACT-Handbook.pdf

276. The project will be audited in accordance with UNDP policies and procedures on audits, informed by and together with any specific requirements agreed in the AMA currently being negotiated with the GCF. According to the current audit policies, UNDP will perform an internal audit of the project at least once during its implementation period. This audit may include plans, on the basis of the Executing Entity's risk rating and UNDP's guidelines. A scheduled audit is used to determine whether the funds have been spent in accordance with their intended purpose and in accordance with the work plan. A scheduled audit can consist of a financial audit or an internal control audit.

277. All GCF resources will be provided to the Executing Entity, less any agreed cost recovery amount. Under UNDP's national implementation guidelines, the Executing Entity is responsible for ensuring that all funds are used only for the implementation of agreed and approved program activities, in accordance with UNDP standard policies and the NIM Guidelines. The Executing Entity reports back expenditure via a financial report on quarterly basis to UNDP. Any additional requirements will be as agreed between UNDP and GCF and when it is agreed.
