# G.1 Risk Assessment Summary

## 297. Effective project implementation faces technical, operational, institutional, and social and environmental risks (please see annex Vb for a detailed EIS). These risks are associated with the construction of irrigation infrastructure, installation and operation of hydrological and meteorological monitoring stations. Construction and operation of renovated or new infrastructure will take place in rural areas that are often difficult to access. Effective and efficient operation of new irrigation equipment and infrastructure is dependent on the availability of trained personnel. The project will provide training for smallholders whose formal education may be incomplete. Institutional weaknesses may impede timely completion of administrative procedures, such as land registration and acquisition, and delays in obtaining permits from government agencies and stakeholders can risk delays in project implementation and inefficiencies throughout the project cycle.

## 298. Limited awareness and preparation of communities to involve themselves in the project can impede effective participation in project planning, design, construction, operation, maintenance, and monitoring and evaluation activities. Farmers' willingness to participate in training and planning meetings may be a challenge and risk delay in project implementation. Value chain development will be hampered by lack of information dissemination platforms, thereby putting at risk the development of market linkages for climate-resilient value chain development

### 298a. Climate shocks – extended droughts, extreme heatwaves or widespread flooding - if strong enough, could conceivably damage or destroy irrigation infrastructure and other project components. The project will be exposed to both local environmental and social risks related to sedimentation control, organizational function (IMCs), financial management, etc., that will affect smallholder communities in the project area. These are detailed in Section F.3., as well as added in the table below.

### 298b. This project is also exposed to climate-related risks such as extreme heatwaves and droughts. The project will involve local awareness raising and technical and organizational capacity building for communities and government authorities to ensure appropriate design and operation of irrigation infrastructure and hydro-meteorological monitoring stations. Smallholders will receive training on how to plan and manage their water resources, including crops as well as continuous technical assistance from the DO and AGIRTE. The project will implement tested O&M models for community irrigation and water management with short duration (1-2 years) and long-term (5+ years) maintenance plans that are cost-effective and sustainable for sustained O&M.

### 298c. The local community members operating the hydro-meteorological monitoring stations will receive special training and ongoing guidance on their activities. Developing SOPs for information sharing and data sharing for these stations and other weather information systems will ensure that reliable seasonal forecasts and flood information can be provided to smallholders.

### 298d. Institutional coordination will attenuate the risk of delay and inefficiencies caused by a dispersion of activities among institutions, as well as the lack of institutional capacity. The project will implement an institutional framework for close collaboration consisting of a CGI Project Steering Committee coordinating closely with the Zimbabwe Irrigation Development Authority (ZIDA), the Department of Agriculture (DO) and the Agricultural Research Institute (ARI). Strong collaboration across the project PMU and the ZRBF PMU. The Innovation Platforms for climate-resilient value chains will also bring together all relevant stakeholders to ensure that appropriate institutional mechanisms are intended precisely to ensure seamless coordination among institutions and organizations at the district level.

### 298e. The risk of low commitment to adoption of climate-resilient agricultural practices is mitigated through an inclusive multi-stakeholder approach, as demonstrated in Farmer Field Schools (FSS). This approach sensitizes smallholders to the climate risks in a BAI scenario and builds ownership for sustainable and climate-smart solutions. The potential risk of climate shocks is mitigated through use of reliable forecasts for planning and operation of the infrastructure as well as in operations and maintenance plans. The project will also implement an O&M plan (see Annex Vb) that has been formulated to address any environmental and social risks arising from the project

# G.2. Risk Factors and Mitigation Measures

## Selected Risk Factor 1

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Revitalization and climate-proofing of irrigation infrastructure could experience delays given logistical and operational challenges of construction in relatively remote areas. | Technical and operational | Medium (5-120% of project value) | Medium |

### Mitigation Measure(s)

This risk will be mitigated by building on the sub-assessments, studies and consultations during project preparation; clear, formal delineation of responsibilities and division of labor among collaborating institutions; rigorous vetting of building companies; and intensive planning beginning with a detailed construction plan. The project team will also ensure that all necessary equipment is available in advance, and sufficient seasoned staff will be employed to ensure efficiency in irrigation revitalization and climate-proofing. These measures maintain this risk at Medium.

## Selected Risk Factor 2

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Farmers may be reluctant to adopt climate-resilient agricultural practices due to perceived risk associated with application of new techniques and inputs. | Technical and operational | Medium (5-120% of project value) | Medium |

### Mitigation Measure(s)

Participatory action-research methodologies used in Farmer Field Schools will build on smallholder identified needs and priorities, as well as their traditional knowledge and practices. Smallholder organizations will be established at the village level to ensure that women and men – and women – will be identified for FFS training to take place over a course of at least two years. Smallholder organizations will be represented in the project steering committee and other decision-making bodies, ensuring that they have an equal voice with other stakeholders to ensure climate-resilient crop and livestock production and commercialization. Given a long history of institutional support to smallholders at both national and local level, farmers have often been trained by AGROTEK staff and are accustomed to the FFS modality.

These measures maintain this risk at Medium.

## Selected Risk Factor 3

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Elite farmers could capture the benefits of partnerships with the private sector. | Social and environmental | Medium (5-120% of project value) | Medium |

### Mitigation Measure(s)

Partnership agreements with the private sector – leveraged through multi-stakeholder Innovation Platforms – will involve smallholder organizations in decision-making processes, ensuring that they have an equal voice with other stakeholders. The project team will offer technical training to all community smallholders, provide timely technical assistance to smallholder organizations in regard to


---
**Page 95**
---

financial and post-harvest management, and ensure equal access to key inputs, including water and climate-resilient crop varieties. These measures are rated as Medium.

## Selected Risk Factor

### Description
Irrigation Management Committees do not participate in revitalization of irrigation schemes and feel no responsibility for Operations and Maintenance.

### Risk category
Technical and operational

### Level of impact
Medium (5-120% of project value)

### Probability of risk occurring
Low

#### Mitigation Measure(s)
Confirmation of the willingness of ICMs to participate in revitalization of irrigation schemes and assume responsibility for GEM will be a key criterion for selection. The project partners will work with ICMs to ensure that they are adequately trained and equipped, including provision of ICM members' attendants and equipment. Given the essential importance of financial resources for the success of this activity, the project partners will also provide training for ICM staff. Given the essential importance of irrigation to rural livelihoods, the probability of risk of ICM dismissal is considered low.

## Selected Risk Factor 5

### Description
Extreme weather events result in widespread erosion and administration of irrigation infrastructure.

### Risk category
Social and environmental

### Level of impact
Medium (5-120% of project value)

### Probability of risk occurring
Medium

#### Mitigation Measure(s)
Design of irrigation infrastructure will be effected taking into account the worst case scenario based on analysis of climate models as well as climate vulnerabilities of each selected irrigation scheme. While extreme weather events are expected to continue to occur and present high risks to irrigation infrastructure, the project partners will work with ICMs to ensure that they have adequate resources in place for dealing with this occurring as medium. In addition, the project partners will work to mobilize resources and engage in partnerships for improved distribution management of a irrigation measure.

## Selected Risk Factor 6

### Description
Private sector entrepreneurs decline to participate on innovation platforms due to perceived high potential costs of risk and effort to outweigh potential benefits.

### Risk category
Technical and operational

### Level of impact
Medium (5-120% of project value)

#### Mitigation Measure(s)
Innovation platforms are designed to reach common around the development of specific climate-resilient crop or livestock value chains by involving all stakeholders, from production through to wholesale. Private sector entrepreneurs stand to gain from stable markets for inputs and outputs, as well as improved access to credit facilities. The project partners will work with private sector entrepreneurs to build strong relationships with smallholders to access adequate amounts of produce for market. In addition, successful experiences from other projects


---
**Page 96**
---

Selected Risk Factor 7

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Increase in community conflict and Gender-based Violence (GBV) due to the high competition for resources and targeting women as equal beneficiaries of project interventions. | Social and environmental | Medium (0.1-20% of project value) | Medium |

Mitigation Measure(s)

In designing project activities, gender equity was a primary concern with the aim of strengthening opportunities for women in agricultural production and access to markets. The project will ensure that women are included as equal beneficiaries of project interventions and members in regards to "appropriate work for women" as well as exposure to GBV, which will be addressed through ongoing consultations, community awareness raising activities, and capacity building workshops. The project will also ensure that the gender equity policy is integrated into all components established, which will allow beneficial women to report any incidences of social conflict arising from their involvement in project activities.

Each component will have a focal person responsible for ensuring that the gender equity policy is adhered to. The Gender Equity Focal Person (GEP) will have an explicit gender focal point to ensure that analyses of lessons and project experience, as well as discussions of potential policy reforms or changes are conducted with women in mind. The GEPs will also be responsible for providing feedback on the implementation of the GEPs to drive these effective gender equality and gender empowerment methodologies – e.g., OFWAM implemented GEI/UNDP Scaling Up Adaptation project, which has been successful in addressing gender equity issues through the establishment of women’s groups. To address violence against women, the project will incorporate men and women dialogues on GBV. The FFS present appropriate platforms to establish fora for awareness and dialogue on GBV among targeted beneficiaries.

Selected Risk Factor 8

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Lack of community ownership and buy-in to irrigation scheme regulation | Social and environmental | Medium (0.1-20% of project value) | Low |

Mitigation Measure(s)

The social support for irrigation scheme revitalization was assessed during pre-project preparation. Given the underlying value of water availability and productivity, it is expected that communities will be willing to invest in their irrigation schemes. However, given the fact that many schemes represent a public good, communities have organized Irrigation Management Committees (IMCs) to operate and maintain their systems; however, IMCs are often weak or non-existent due to lack of resources, poor governance structures, and other institutional weaknesses that have impaired their ability to adequately manage, resources, resolve conflicts, or plan for the future. This project will strengthen IMCs through participatory development of IMC constitutions delineating roles and responsibilities of participating organizations.


---
**Page 97**
---

## Selected Risk Factor 9

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Seasonal crop price fluctuations are a potential threat to smallholder farmers' livelihoods. A sudden and significant fall in prices at an inappropriate time could result in significant economic losses, especially for those who have not adopted resilience-enhancing inputs and technologies. Continuous assessment of the market situation is required so that farmers can be protected from economic losses due to negative price fluctuations. | Social and environmental | Medium (5-10% of project value) | Medium |

### Mitigation Measure(s)

This risk will be mitigated by including monitoring and early warning on price fluctuations as part of regular Innovation Platform discussions in tandem with training in farming as a business for AGIRIX staff and farmers. The Innovation Platforms will draw on the ZMIF and MURRIFI inputs of price forecasting and market information systems for monitoring crop price fluctuations to support price planning. These measures together reduce the probability of the risk at medium.

## Selected Risk Factor 10

### Description

Women are restricted in participating fully in agricultural production, product linkages and value chain development, although they represent a significant proportion of farmers. Women are playing increasingly important roles in agriculture and outperforming men on many aspects of farm management. However, appropriate work for women, they are often relegated to specific areas of the farm such as "women's work", and they do not enjoy the same access to resources as men do.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

Restrictions on women's participation in agriculture will be explicitly addressed by this project. Lack of knowledge and technical skills in agricultural production and marketing, coupled with lack of access to inputs and markets are major constraints for women farmers. The project includes using of flexible times and the use of female trainers. Male household members will also receive awareness raising as to the economic and social benefits of involving women in agriculture. Women's participation is explicitly represented on the Innovation Platforms (IP), and, where specific value chains for fruits and animals deemed to be "women's work" fit a gender perspective, these are included within the IP. The project will engage with private sector representatives and other stakeholders including buyers, processors, exporters, etc., to ensure that women farmers have access to markets and inputs. Women's representatives will target women's product groups for potential partnerships with private sector entities, including buyers, processors, exporters, etc. The project will collect gender-disaggregated data on the effectiveness of interventions and apply lessons learned from the project and other projects in the region to ensure that future projects are designed to address the barriers faced by women farmers so that beneficiary concerns and perspectives are incorporated over subsequent years of the project. These measures keep the risk at Medium.

# H.1. Logic Framework.

## H.1.1. Paradigm Shift Objectives and Impacts at the Fund Level

| Paradigm shift objective | The project shifts the prevailing paradigm away from a focus on short-term, sectoral, production-oriented responses towards an integrated approach, in which all actors in specific climate-resilient value chains – producers, input providers, traders, processors, financial institutions, and consumers – are involved. This will lead to increased access to finance, information, and market access for climate-resilient crops, thus providing the incentive to maintain climate-resilient agricultural practices and sustain climate-resilient smallholder production from increased access to water for agriculture through climate-resilient irrigation systems, improved seed varieties, and diversification. |

### 2.1 Field-based training and technology innovations for building rain-fed resilience in water management

This activity will increase the climate vulnerability of smallholder farmers to extreme weather events by increasing their capacity to adapt and employ land use practices that harness rainfall. As part of the completion of YFS, participating farmers will implement climate-resilient water resource management techniques on 10 ha each year for two years (for total farmers each) under the supervision of AGATEK extension workers.

The project invests in building the capacity of smallholder farmers to adapt and apply climate resilient water resource management techniques and practices. These include rainwater harvesting, mulching, conservation tillage, agroforestry, rainwater harvesting, and others.

Lead Farmers at Farmer Field Schools will build their awareness and understanding on climate-resilient water resource management techniques, the role of organic matter in retaining and recycling moisture, etc.

Lead Farmers will apply this new knowledge and skills to implementation of climate-resilient water resource management plots as well as working directly with an additional 50,000 farmers. Lead Farmers will be trained on climate-resilient water resource management techniques and practices for rain-fed farming. Farmers are expected to contribute 30-50% of the land.

To support scale-up and replication, AGATEK staff will provide support and supervise each lead farmer in training additional farmers. Farmer-to-farmer learning will be supported through field visits and on-site technical support.


### 2.1.3 Participatory workshops and demonstrations

The project invests in building climate smart agricultural production by empowering smallholders to contribute their own knowledge, skills, and experiences (KSE) into the Innovation Platforms (IPs) composed of the public and private stakeholders that will be established at the district level. These stakeholders include key the actors and entities involved in agriculture, including government agencies, extension assistance agencies, and research institutions, as well as farmers.

Five multi-stakeholder Innovation Platforms will be formed across 15 Districts. Each Innovation Platform will be coordinated and


### 2.1.1 Technical assistance, training and capacity building

The project invests in linking IPs across the three provinces to facilitate exchange visits, dissemination of climate adaptation technologies and practices, and other activities at the district level at Catchment and Sub-Catchment levels and other activities.

This activity will lead training of 500 farmers per year in 230 Farmer Field Schools sessions on water management techniques and practices. This will be done through quarterly workshops (quarterly workshops across 15 districts and one workshop per District) through to annual meetings (one meeting per District). The project invests in building the capacity of smallholder farmers to adapt and apply climate resilient water resource management techniques and practices for rain-fed farming.

The project invests in building the capacity of smallholder farmers to implement climate-resilient water resource management techniques and practices. These include rainwater harvesting, mulching, conservation tillage, agroforestry, rainwater harvesting, and others.

## Facilitated by assigned AGUSTR and DRESS staff, with the assistance of relevant national agricultural research institutions and other international research organizations. It will meet quarterly during the first year and biannually thereafter to ensure that all partners respect each crop and market linkages and cataloging market realities within.

The constitution of an Innovation Platform together analyze the strengths and weaknesses of the selected crops in relation with current production and market linkage of a specific crop associated with the main findings of the feasibility study as a starting point. Based on the crop specific SWOT analysis, each Innovation Platform will be responsible for developing a strategy to address the identified challenges and opportunities within the context of the selected crop. The objectives, outcomes and outputs of such innovation platforms will be to increase the resilience of smallholder agricultural production of a specific climate-resilient crop (identified during phase one) by addressing the identified constraints from an ecological, social and economic criteria and evolving the market mechanisms for the selected crops.

Each Innovation Platform will bring practical partnerships between actors in the value chain. The platform will identify and address concrete obstacles to smallholder production and access to markets for the specific climate-resilient crop. It will also address issues of adequate and timely supply of inputs, credit availability, and other enabling factors that are critical to the success of smallholders and/or contractual agreements, as identified in each innovation platform. The feasibility study will be used by each Innovation Platform to identify key actors and stakeholders who can be connected to lead them in partnership with DRESS or AGUSTR.

Access to finance for smallholders will be facilitated as a function of ongoing engagement between the Innovation Platforms and financial institutions. As financing requirements become clear for production and marketing, the Innovation Platforms will work closely with banks and other financial intermediaries to identify and engage potential partners. Such engagement may take the form of contracts or agreements that will be provided to farmer production groups in demand at the final stage of the feasibility study. In addition, financial intermediaries interested in engaging with producers’ groups may also have access to information on the specific climate-resilient crop benefits and other aspects of investing in CCA.

Innovation Platforms will also provide technical assistance “horizontal” of Farmer Field Schools i.e., the specific skills, practices and techniques that are required for the production of a specific climate-resilient crop. Given that farmer plots are usually very small, it is expected that the Innovation Platform will work with cooperatives and other producers’ groups to ensure that all farmers in the project area have access not only agricultural production but also fair market and value chain opportunities.

The value add sub-assessment in the feasibility study identified several key areas of opportunity for innovation platforms.

more effective market linkages. These can be found in the Value Chain Sub-Assessment of the Feasibility Study, Annex III.

This activity will support government extension services – using a demand-driven, climate-resilient agricultural practices (Based on climate change vulnerability assessments) to smallholder farms in southern Ethiopia. Smallholder farmers, particularly women, are trained at national and district levels in the districts of southern Ethiopia. This training is followed by field testing and adaptation of package of climate resilient practices and technologies to their specific farming systems. The aim is to increase access to food security in the face of increasing climatic hazards.

As such, this activity invests in training of trainers for Farmer Field Schools (FFS) and other extension services, as well as testing and adaptation of CBA packages on centralised FFS plots and demonstration farms. This will be done through a demand-driven approach to experience other than that of IFAD, GIZ/AGT, CIMPAT and other partners, have led to the development of climate resilient agricultural practices (CRAs) for smallholder farmers in Ethiopia. These CRAs are tested and adapted to different smaller scale agro-ecological zones and farming systems. The CRAs will be disseminated through FFS and other extension services. In addition, there will be two sessions per FFS activities will incorporate CBA packages and where applicable, private enterprises.

CRA packages identified through consultations during proposed project implementation period. These include climate-resilient farming systems and agro-ecological principles to fit under actual climatic conditions in Ethiopia. The CRAs will include the use of improved varieties, appropriate tillage techniques, incorporation of tree species into crop rotations, intercropping, establishment of integrated crop-soil systems, as well as water-efficient practices such as drip irrigation.

Lead Farmers will implement CBA practices and cropping systems on their own plots and share these with other farmers in their area either individually or collectively to concentrated locations in their respective communities. This will be done through the use of inputs and technologies to implement CRAs practices on these plots so that all farmers can benefit from the improved yields and reduced required inputs.

The training session gained during the field testing and disseminated plot FFS is shared with smallholders in the community and district level extension services. The training sessions are usually, but not always, organized by AGT/ED. Lead farmers will work with district and local government officials to ensure that they extend their innovation into extended practices group of 15 farmers each such as through workshops and on-farm demonstrations. This will be done for a total of 97,500 smallholder households, equal to 375,000 people in the project area.

- 100 FFS organized for Small and Medium scale farmers.

2.3: Enhance coordination among key government departments for resilient agricultural production in the face of climate change

This activity will support the improved coordination and strategic planning on scaling up of climate-resilient agricultural practices among five key government departments, namely AGREIT and DRIIS, Department of Agriculture and Livestock Services, Department of Irrigation and Department of Economics and Markets. The project will also strengthen existing information systems and knowledge sharing protocols for effective implementation of climate change adaptation strategies in the future-scaling up of climate-resilient agricultural practices.

2.3.1 Upgrade ICT/US data collection and analysis systems and protocols for knowledge management and research centers to support national and regional research and development efforts.

2.3.2 Generation of climate resilient knowledge across key government departments and research centers for climate change adaptation strategies.

2.3.3 Impact evaluation and dissemination of best practices/factors for scaling up of climate-resilient agricultural practices, including factors decision making and our scaling of practices.

The focus will be on ensuring that the lessons learned, and best practices are shared among key government departments to scale-up and shared across departments at the national level in a comprehensive manner. This will support evidence-based decision-making and our scaling of practices.


2.3.4 Train MSDL, ZIMNA, DRIIS, AGREIT and other relevant stakeholders on collecting, analyzing and maintaining equipment


2.3.5 Upgrade ICT/US data collection and analysis systems and protocols for knowledge management and research centers to support national and regional research and development efforts.

2.3.6 Communication and automated weather station installation

2.3.7 Establishment of 10 regional level exchange programmes for sharing of best practices and learning opportunities.

2.3.8 Establishment of hubstations across three key government departments and DRIIS research centers to facilitate knowledge sharing among 15 districts.

2.3.9 Evidence-based study and monitoring conducted

2.3.10 Evidence based publication on climate change adaptation recommendations published

### M.2. Arrangements for Monitoring, Reporting and Evaluation

#### 287. The UNDP Country Office will support the Project Manager as needed, including through annual supervision missions.

The UNDP Country Office is responsible for: a) complying with project-level monitoring and evaluation requirements; b) providing additional M&E capacity building and troubleshooting support as required; c) approving the project’s M&E plan, quality assurance plan, and troubleshooting support will be provided by the UNDP Regional Technical Advisor as needed. The project target groups and stakeholders including the NDA Focal Point will be involved as much as possible in project monitoring.

#### 288. A project inception workshop will be held after the UNDP project document is signed by all relevant parties to: a) re-orient project stakeholders on the project strategy and discuss any changes that influence project implementation; b) discuss the project’s M&E plan, quality assurance plan, and troubleshooting support as needed; c) review the results framework, re-assess baselines as needed, and discuss reporting, monitoring and evaluation roles and responsibilities of all parties involved in the project; d) agree on the arrangements for the annual audit; e) plan and schedule Project Board meetings and finalize the first year annual work plan. The UNDP Country Office will coordinate the inception workshop with the NDA Focal Point and other stakeholders. The project inception report will be cleared by the UNDP Country Office and the UNDP Regional Technical Advisor, and will be approved by the Project Board.

#### 289. A project implementation report will be prepared for each year of project implementation. The Project Manager, the UNDP Country Office, and the UNDP Regional Technical Advisor will provide objective input to the annual PIR. The Project Manager will ensure that the indicators included in the project results framework are monitored annually well in advance of the PIR submission deadline as per the M&E plan. The UNDP Country Office will coordinate with the NDA Focal Point, other stakeholders and the Project Board and other stakeholders. The UNDP Country Office will coordinate the input of the NDA Focal Point and other stakeholders to the annual PIR. The UNDP Regional Technical Advisor will provide objective input on behalf of the UNDP. The final project PIR along with the terminal evaluation report and corresponding management response, will serve as the final project report and will be submitted in accordance with UNDP guidelines for quarterly reports that are produced by the project manager.

#### 290. An independent mid-term review process will be undertaken and the findings and responses contained in the management response section of the MTR will be used to inform the Project Board’s decision on whether or not to continue funding the project. The terms of reference, the review process and the final MTR report will follow the standard templates and guidance available on the UNDP Evaluation Resource Center (ERC) website: <http://www.undp.org/erc>. The MTR will be prepared by the UNDP Regional Technical Advisor, and will be approved by the Project Board. The final MTR report will be available in English. An independent terminal evaluation (TE) will take place no later than three months prior to operational closure of the project. The terms of reference for the TE will follow the standard templates and guidance available on the UNDP Evaluation Resource Center. The final TE report will be cleared by the UNDP Country Office and the UNDP Regional Technical Advisor, and will be approved by the Project Board. The final terminal evaluation report in English and the management response to the public under Evaluation Resource Center (ERC) <http://www.undp.org/erc>


---
**Page 112**
---

MTR and TE will be carried out by an independent evaluator. The evaluation report prepared by the independent evaluator is then quality assessed and rated by the UNDP Independent Evaluation Office.

291. The project will also include a return visit at MTR. Records for this project for up to seven years after project financial closure in order to support ex-post evaluations of the project’s impact on climate change adaptation, which will be included in the UNDP project document.

292. A key element of MTR for this project is the field reports and gender-sensitive impact surveys that will be conducted by PMU (Gender Officer, and AGRITEK field level extension officers) throughout the project to monitor progress of implementation and progress towards the expected outputs and outcomes. To monitor and verify the irrigation systems revitalization and adoption of climate-resilient agricultural practices, a baseline survey on the current status of irrigation (DOI) through district offices. Installations are captured in the community irrigation system database of DOI that will be reviewed for completeness and accuracy. The progress towards the expected outputs and outcomes such as increased cropping intensities will also be reviewed for progress on reach of agricultural practices and improved productivities. For data and information management, a baseline survey on the current status of irrigation systems revitalization and adoption of climate-resilient agricultural practices will be conducted by PMU (Gender Officer, and AGRITEK field level extension officers) throughout the project. Household surveys will also be conducted through AGRITEK and DUDP/DVS to monitor the progress and capture the impact of the project. These will include sections with questions dedicated to understanding the social and economic implications of climate change adaptation on households in terms of food security, income generation, and livelihoods. Climate-related early warnings, and forecasts will be monitored through field surveys conducted by MUD, AGRITEK and DUDP/DVS (for communities), and UNDP/UNFCCC.

293. While the Zimbabwe CO, through its ongoing presence in the country, will continue to engage with the GoZ, there is no provision for human and financial resources to undertake formal reporting post-completion of the project. Information, where available, will be communicated to the GCF Secretariat as feasible.

Re: Letter of No Objection for Funding proposal for the GCF by the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) entitled "Building climate resilience of vulnerable agricultural livelihoods in southern Zimbabwe".

We refer to the programme and proposal on Building climate resilience of vulnerable agricultural livelihoods in southern Zimbabwe, as contained within the funding proposal submitted by the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) to us on 17 August 2018.

The undersigned is the duly authorized representative of the Ministry of Environment, Water and Climate Change, the National Designated Authority/focal point of Zimbabwe.

Pursuant to GCF decision B.08(1), the content of which we acknowledge to have reviewed, we hereby communicate our no objection to the programme as included in the funding proposal.

By communicating our no objection, it is implied that:

(a) The programme and its no objection to the programme as included in the funding proposal;
(b) All programmes as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with Zimbabwe's national priorities, strategies and plans;
(c) In accordance with the GCF’s environmental and social safeguards, the programme as included in the funding proposal complies with all relevant international, regional and national regulations.

We also confirm that our national process for ascertaining no objection to the project as included in the funding proposal has been duly followed.

We also confirm that our no objection applies to all projects or activities to be implemented within the scope of the programme.

We acknowledge that this letter will be made publicly available on the GCF website.

Secretariat’s assessment of FP127

Proposal name: Building Climate Resilience of Vulnerable Agricultural Livelihoods in Southern Zimbabwe

Accredited entity: United Nations Development Programme (UNDP)

Country: Zimbabwe

Project/programme size: Small


## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

### 1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| Integrated approach to address climate change, involving climate-resilient agricultural practices, improved seed supply and management and climate advisories, which meets the needs of smallholder farmers and strengthens the agricultural value chain. | High climate impact potential with a large number of beneficiaries and with mitigation co-benefits. |
| Solid operation and maintenance plan with a financial commitment from the Government of Zimbabwe for the construction and commissioning for hydro-meteorological infrastructures. |  |

2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions as outlined in annexure A and addendum B, titled "List of proposed conditions and recommendations".

## Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### 2.1 Project background

3. Southern Zimbabwe has experienced increasing temperatures since the 1950s with a decline in total annual precipitation and an increase in mid-season dry spells coupled with extreme weather events in the form of droughts and floods. These changes in climate have reduced agricultural yields, increased food insecurity, and impacted the livelihoods of smallholder farmers in this region.

4. The project proposes to address these observed and projected climate impacts and build the resilience of vulnerable agricultural livelihoods in Southern Zimbabwe. The project will be implemented for seven years, with a requested GCF grant of USD


---
**Page 119**
---

26.6 million co-financing from the Government of Zimbabwe of USD 14.2 million and re- financing from the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) of USD 12 million.

5. The project will directly benefit around 543,620 people in 15 vulnerable districts of southern Zimbabwe from revitalized and climate-proofed irrigation schemes and increased water resources management. This includes 87,900 farmers, 10,000 households, 100 health clinics and schools (FFS) and climate information. An additional 1,758,500 people across the 15 districts will indirectly benefit from improved access to water resources and climate-resilient agricultural advisory and institutional capacity development.

6. The project is structured into three outputs:

(a) Increased access to water for agriculture through climate-resilient irrigation systems and water resource management

(b) Scaled up climate-resilient agricultural production and diversification through increased access to improved inputs, practices and markets,

(c) Improved access to weather, climate and hydrological information for climate-resilient agriculture (CRA).

### 2.2 Component-by-component analysis

Output 1: Increased access to water for agriculture through climate-resilient irrigation schemes and field-level water harvesting for rained lands to increase water efficiency and agricultural productivity, while reducing the risk of soil erosion and improving land management techniques and water management techniques. Overall, this output will ensure reliable access to water by farmers and their capacity to manage increasing climate risks through proven adaptation techniques in agriculture.

8. The output will not simply rehabilitate the irrigation infrastructures, but will also climate-proof them. This is achieved through a combination of approaches. The approach has been adopted from the Climate Resilient Infrastructure Development Facility, funded by the UK Department for International Development (DFID) and the governments of Great Britain and Northern Ireland, in the country. The proposed climate-proofing techniques - river or watercourse bank reinforcement, bioengineering, gullions, rip-rap protections and others – will be used to protect irrigation infrastructure from erosion caused by extreme events and floods.

9. The irrigation schemes will be supplied by existing dammed water and river flows. This will involve river-bed sand abstraction. Water will flow by gravity, siltumping or a combination of the two. The feasibility study confirms that no net negative water balance will result from this abstraction. In addition, the project proposes to use an alternative energy source (AES) was advised to phase out the use fossil fuels in water pumping. As a result, pump sets will be replaced by solar-powered pumps and diesel generators with a total of 630 ha, which is a shift away from conventional diesel-powered and grid connection. As such, there are no additional greenhouse gas emissions and there is an increase in greenhouse gas emissions and that mitigation co-benefits are generated.

18. The proposal sounds a water operation and maintenance (O&M) and financial budget of the project. The O&M budget will be shared between the Zimbabwe Water Authority as part of the Government of Zimbabwe and the communities. The cost estimation for O&M during and after the project has been provided, as well as the expected financial contributions


---
**Page 120**
---

from the government and communities. This commitment should be monitored to ensure that the established assets are adequately maintained.

Output 2: Scalable up climate-resilient agricultural production and diversification through increased access to climate-resilient inputs, practices and markets (total cost: USD 22.8 million; GCF cost: USD 9.4 million; GCF share: 41%)

- With output 1 ensuring reliable access to water resources by smallholder farmers, output 2 focuses on delivering CRA to these farmers. The output will provide CRA packages to 60,000 lead farmers in each district through a multi-stakeholder climate innovation platforms in each target district to provide technical assistance and capacity-building for the adoption of climate-resilient practices in the agricultural system including farming suppliers, processors and credit providers.

- Proposed inputs and techniques for CRA - storage structures, irrigation, appropriate crop varieties, soil conservation, targeted fertilization, agroforestry and post-harvest storage and management - are well-known measures recognized for their adaptation benefits to climate change.

- The CRA packages are expected to incentivize local farmers to adopt resilient techniques. In terms of beneficiaries, the packages will directly benefit 6,960 lead farmers, who represent a significant proportion (15%) of all direct beneficiaries in each district. Since one package cannot be extended to the entire direct beneficiaries, the proponent should develop a conversion plan for the remaining farmers and other stakeholders within the agricultural system.

- The proposed multi-stakeholder climate innovation platforms will enable dialogues and knowledge exchange among various stakeholders involved in agricultural value chains. While this can be a good initial step to bring in stakeholders, it is important for the project to draw concrete lessons from these platforms, including how to catalyze private sector financing for innovative and sustainable value chain development.

Output 3: Improved access to weather, climate and hydrological information for climate-resilient agricultural production (total cost: USD 1.5 million; GCF cost: USD 0.6 million; GCF share: 40%)

- The output will establish 12 automatic weather stations, 10 automatic rainfall stations and 10 hydrological gauging stations at proposed irrigation sites that are not currently covered by the existing meteorological and hydro-meteorological observational network in the agricultural system. It will deliver institutional capacity-building for relevant national and local staff to develop a climate-resilient agricultural production system.

- The feasibility study highlights the current gaps in the number of weather and hydrological stations, as well as the limited forecasting capabilities of the hydro-meteorological staff at regional level. This output aims to fill these gaps by establishing an improved observational network, such as focused on smaller regions. It is expected that the installations will be carried out with the collaboration of the Ministry of Agriculture, Water and Rural Resettlement (MILACWRR), and all the climate-related institutions in the country at both national and regional level.

Output 4: Project management (total cost: USD 25 million; GCF cost: USD 1.4 million; GCF share: 6%)

- The last component is the project management cost for the implementation of the project. The GCF portion of the project management cost is around 4.7 per cent of the total requested GCF funding, and is compliant with the GCF policy on fees.

### Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: High

- The proposal aims to reach a sizeable proportion of the population, with **545,629** beneficiaries directly benefiting from the climate-proofed irrigation schemes, climate-resilient agricultural practices, and improved access to inputs such as seeds, fertilizers, pesticides, FFSs, and credit facilities. In addition, 1,758,500 people are indirectly benefited by receiving agro-climate advisories and technical assistance from the Department of Agricultural, Technical and Extension Services (DASTES) and other stakeholders.
- The project will contribute to climate proofing of existing community-level irrigation schemes and introduce low-cost water harvesting technologies for improved water resource management.

#### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: Medium-High

- The project will catalyse an entire paradigm shift by supporting smallholder farmers in shifting away from conventional agriculture, which aggregates soil moisture and is highly dependent on rainwater, towards a more climate-resilient agricultural system using improved techniques.
- The shift of the agricultural system to one with a more reliable supply of water is achieved through the introduction of low-cost water harvesting technologies for irrigation, which will be used to shift the government's existing agricultural input scheme to one that allows crop diversification and increased resilience. The sustainability and scaling up of this shift will depend on further investment after project completion.
- The project also has good potential for scaling up and replication in other regions of the country through further financing. Project beneficiaries represent about 24 per cent of the


---
**Page 122**
---

population of the 15 target districts, and this can be further scaled up to other districts.

However, the proposal could have presented a more detailed plan and quantitative estimation on the replication potential of the proposed interventions.

25. The proposal provides good potential for knowledge-sharing and learning through the introduction of new technologies, platforms, and various capacity-building activities through FFS. It is expected to contribute to the creation of an enabling environment for the development of climate-resilient agricultural systems in Zimbabwean platforms, but the scope and extent of the actual market stimulation depends on the participation of stakeholders.

26. The proposal provides a solid post-project O&M plan with an estimated budget. A significant part of the cost is expected to be covered by beneficiary communities, such as a community-based savings group. It is important that the proposed infrastructure and assets are properly operated and maintained after project completion by enabling full government and community ownership of the assets.

### 3.3 Sustainable development potential
**Scale: High**

27. The proposal provides sound justification for its potential to generate positive environmental, social and economic externalities. The proposed CCA packages include generally well-recognized climate-smart agricultural practices such as agroforestry and agroforestry. No harmful environmental impacts are associated with the selected seeds in the O&M plan. The proposed water supply system is also considered environmentally friendly and avoids negative environmental externalities.

28. The proposed water supply and CCA packages will generate a significant increase in farmers' income, which is assessed as substantial. The potential for social benefits is also assessed as high; diversified agricultural production with climate information services will improve the livelihoods of smallholder farmers and their families so that it will be better prepared and equipped for current and future climate change impacts.

29. Gender considerations have been included in the project design with the aim of an inclusive approach to CCA. The proposal includes a gender action plan, which notes that gender equality participation will be achieved in all project activities. A detailed gender assessment and action plan was developed and submitted as an annex.

### 3.4 Needs of the recipient
**Scale: High**

30. The vulnerability of the country and the target beneficiaries and their adaptive needs are well described within this proposal, which conveys the scale and intensity of the country's exposure to climate risks. The impacts of climate change, particularly droughts on agricultural production, are noted as a major challenge for smallholder farmers, including women, and acknowledged that their high dependence on rain-fed agriculture, which is greatly affected by rainfall variability and increasing temperatures, is noted.

31. Substantial financial constraints of the government are acknowledged. Zimbabwe's public debt is 45 per cent of its gross domestic product (GDP), which is considered a major constraint to development. The International Monetary Fund classifies the country's status as "external debt distress."

32. The proposal explains the needs of the recipient based on the degree of economic dependence and vulnerability, and the capacity of the government and other institutions involved in climate resilience - Meteorological Service Department, Zimbabwe National Water Authority and the Department of Agricultural, Technical and Extension Services - have

### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### Environmental and social safeguards

The AF has undertaken a social and environmental screening to identify and address any potential social and environmental risks and impacts that could arise from project activities and has classified them for social and environmental risk category as follows (which equates to the GCF’s Category 4 in its Environmental and Social Framework):

- The project includes activities which are likely to have some short-term, small-scale environmental and social impacts but these will be mitigated or offset by expected long-term widespread environmental benefits.

The AF has developed an environmental and social management framework (ESMF) for the project. The ESMF incorporates a legal and institutional framework that covers environmental and social issues, as well as the legislation, policies and regulations as regards the environment and social aspects of the project. It also includes monitoring and reporting on environmental and social indicators, including climate, ecology, surface and groundwater, noise-and-light pollution, air quality, soil erosion, biodiversity, land use change, and archaeological and cultural heritage. It also provides guidance on the management of social issues and risks.

The ESMF is prepared on the assumption that the interventions will not require the displacement of people; that none of the interventions will be conducted in protected areas or sensitive ecosystems; and that there are no planned changes to land use, nor any procedures that will be undertaken during all stages of the subjects/and; and that there will be no release of hazardous substances into the environment.

The project also indicates that no land acquisition or resettlement will occur as part of the project. Moreover, the project is not expected to undertake activities in areas declared as cultural heritage sites.

The ESMF identified key potential project risks and impacts and outlined possible management measures for each risk and impact. The ESMF includes a description of the monitoring and reporting protocol. The project’s environmental and social impacts are mainly due to construction activities and to the installation of equipment, such as barrages, river gravel extraction, and the implementation of water supply schemes, including community-level irrigation schemes, including a water delivery and storage infrastructure and more efficient use of water resources. These include the rehabilitation of existing embankment bank reinforcement, bioengineering, gabions, rip-rap/geotextiles, structure anchoring, use of sediments for construction purposes, and the creation of new embankments, as well as the remooting and restating of water channels. The water-efficient irrigation technologies also include the installation of a water delivery infrastructure.

The ESMF provides for social inclusion and consultation, as well as the incorporation of sediment and erosion control strategies, as well as considering health and safety for workers and the environment. A detailed environmental management plan (EMP) will be developed with specific work plans will be provided, including incident reporting as well as periodic environmental monitoring. The EMP will also include a description of how risks are to be identified. Emergency management measures that comply with the occupational, health and safety requirements and regulations will also need to be incorporated into the ESMF. The ESMF is planned to be implemented in collaboration with the project team for its implementation, training of staff for delivery organization and continuing update.

The ESMF includes a general description of the project’s management structure including the general management structure and responsibilities and project delivery and administration during project implementation and operation, as well as guidance on public

### Gender policy

The GCF has provided a gender assessment and a project-level gender action plan, thus complying with the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy and Gender Action Plan.

The gender assessment is based on a desk review and stakeholder consultations. The desk review was conducted to assess Zimbabwe’s progress towards achieving gender equality and women's empowerment initiatives in Zimbabwe. The assessment indicates that Zimbabwe is a signatory to the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) and has made significant progress since 2015, with regard to the existence of favourable gender policies and responsive environmental and natural resource management strategies. However, the assessment also recognizes the need for improvement in terms of gender responsiveness of environmental and natural resource management strategies and/or climate change adaptation measures. The project-level gender action plan is a strategic document that supports the achievement of gender balance in economic development endeavours and the equal participation of women and men in decision-making processes.

The Ministry of Environment, Water Resources and Climate Change (MoEWRC), in collaboration with other ministries (the Ministry of Agriculture, Mechanization and Irrigation Development, the Ministry of Local Government, Public Works and National Housing, the Ministry of Higher and Tertiary Education, Science and Technology Development, and the Ministry of Finance and Economic Development), developed gender-responsive budgets in line with gender responsive budget principles.


---
**Page 126**
---

The assessment indicates that women are more vulnerable to climate change impacts in the country where the project site is located: women-headed households are more vulnerable and face higher levels of poverty (72 per cent) than other households (58 per cent). Women face challenges accessing land, water, and credit for agricultural production. They also have a negative perception that they are not capable. Furthermore, the existing patriarchal system presents barriers to women in gaining access to resources such as land, water, and credit from formal financial institutions. It is difficult for women to access and own land, which is an important resource. About 70 per cent of agricultural households in the country are headed by women, but they have limited education and poor access to farming areas; despite playing a key role in the sector, women have much less access than men to inputs such as seeds, fertilizers, and pesticides. Women-headed households are more likely to be poor, and are more dependent on natural resources that are vulnerable to climate change impacts. Women also face challenges accessing information about property rights and inheritance, and their designated social and economic roles render them more susceptible to gender-based violence.

The assessment highlights the importance of women’s empowerment in adapting to climate change. Women have limited access to irrigation systems (only 20 per cent), which limits their access to water resources. Women are also less likely than men to be involved in decision-making about agricultural production, but not larger livestock such as cattle, which generate higher income. Despite these realities, the assessment highlights that women’s empowerment is critical for climate change adaptation and resilience.

The stakeholder engagement with communities highlighted similar issues that are consistent across the country: limited access to irrigation assets; lack of access to land tenure, gender and mechanization governance of irrigation schemes, weak marketing arrangements, the need for improved agricultural extension services, and the need for farmers, both women and men, to be educated on climate smart agriculture.

The assessment also draws lessons from projects implemented in the country in the areas of women’s empowerment and gender equality. These include: increasing access to irrigation assets; introducing women to improve agronomic practices; developing the capacity of women to manage water resources; and providing training for women to address women's empowerment and gender-based violence issues – the latter being one of the biggest challenges faced by women in rural areas, which can lead to domestic violence or even sexual or physical violence at some point. The project, by targeting and providing equal opportunities, will aim to address the underlying causes of gender inequality. It will facilitate equal access for men and women to land tenure rights, credit facilities, extension positions and access to finance. The interventions, particularly those to be conducted through the FFS, will also contribute towards increasing the number of women farmers who adopt climate smart technologies. The reduction in time constraints will be monitored and reported on a regular basis.

The project’s grievance mechanism will be gender-sensitive to allow both men and women to freely report any grievances or incidences of societal conflict arising from their involvement with the project, including those related to access to resources and changing social norms that may be brought about by the project. Violence against women as a potential barrier to women’s empowerment is also addressed through interventions on violence. The FFS present appropriate platforms to establish forums for awareness-raising and dialogue on gender-based violence among targeted groups.

The gender action plan will be included in both the project-level proper action plan and the funding proposal. The gender action plan articulates various activities that are in line with the findings of the assessment, with corresponding indicators and targets for all activities

# Risks

## Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

55. GCF is requested to provide a grant of USD 29.4 million to strengthen the resilience of agricultural livelihoods for the vulnerable communities. The project will provide smallholder farmers with access to climate information and support them in adopting sustainable agricultural practices and utilize climate information. The Government of Zimbabwe is providing co-financing by way of a grant of USD 14 million and in-kind contribution of USD 5.7 million.

## Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current programme (medium risk)

The AE has been working closely with the Government of Zimbabwe for the last four decades. The AE is currently managing five programmes with the government with an average annual delivery of USD 10 million. This project is built on UNDP Zimbabwe's experience in implementing projects under the Global Environment Facility Small Grants Programme, Building Fund, financed by the European Union, DFID, the Swedish International Development Cooperation Agency and UNDP.

The AE has also coordinated a number of climate change projects in partnership with the AE as well as other international agencies. The institutional capacity assessment by the All India Management Association (AIMA) rated the AE's "project modality" being utilized for this project whereby no payments are routed through the EE.

## Programme-specific execution risks (medium risk)

58. Macroeconomic situation in the country: as stated in the funding proposal, the country has experienced a prolonged period of hyperinflation and the financial sector is characterized by low liquidity and high interest rates. The country's currency is the US dollar with the Zimbabwean dollar as the sole legal tender, but inflation of the local currency reached over 300 per cent in 2016. This situation may lower the environmental and/or economic benefits from the project's proposed investment.

59. Logistical and operational challenges of construction in relatively remote areas: The AE has identified logistical and operational challenges for the project. The project will impact 137 wards in 15 Districts of southern Zimbabwe. The project will invest in revitalization and climate change adaptation projects, which are geographically spread out. The AE plans to mitigate the logistic risks by building on the sub-assessment, studies and consultations during project preparation, and iterative planning beginning at project inception.

# Power source of the irrigation infrastructure

The project will support irrigation schemes with solar panels up to 30 kVA per scheme. According to the feasibility study, for a scheme with more than 30 ha and within 3 km from a reliable grid power source, a grid connection would be recommended. The feasibility study also took into consideration availability of steady power supply while finalizing the irrigation schemes.

## Farmers' ability to pay for O&M funds: smallholder farmers are required to contribute to the GCF portfolio risk (low risk)

### Compliance (medium risk)
The AE has submitted a RACT assessment as part of the supporting documentation for the project. That RACT report identifies a number of weaknesses within the internal control systems of the Ministry of Agriculture. In response to and in recognition of those weaknesses, the AE is recommending that the GCF not approve any future transactions with this counterparty, so as to avoid those weak areas. The AE will be applying its own internal controls when evaluating future projects for this counterparty. The AE has also completed a GCF accreditation process. Based on this information from the AE, Compliance is satisfied that the AE has demonstrated sufficient awareness of the risks and vulnerabilities within the project and has proposed appropriate mitigations to address those risks. These include preventing money-laundering and countering the financing of terrorism and other integrity and accountability issues. The AE has also provided a detailed risk assessment for this project accordingly.

Compliance assigns a medium risk to this project based on the information provided.

This includes but is not limited to monitoring missions, spot checks, facilitation and participation in project board meetings, quarterly progress and annual implementation reviews, and audits at the project level or at a higher level.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of FP127

## Proposal name: Building Climate Resilience of Vulnerable Agricultural Livelihoods in Southern Zimbabwe

### Accredited entity: United Nations Development Programme (UNDP)

### Project/programme size: Small

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel†

### 1. Impact potential Scale: High

#### 1.1 Adaptation impact

##### 1.1.1 Adaptation impact

The project targets the rural population of the Save, Runde and Mzingwane catchment areas of the Zimbabwean southern provinces of Manicaland, Masvingo and Matabeleland South, respectively. These are vulnerable to climate change impacts such as droughts and floods. The same climate risks affecting agricultural production are flooding and drought events.

The proposal aims at countering these effects by enhancing the climate resilience of agriculture through three components: (1) rehabilitation and upgrading of existing irrigation infrastructure and enhanced water resource management; (2) promotion of climate-resilient agricultural practices, including improved crop varieties and livestock breeds; and (3) improvement of hydrometeorological and weather information generation and use.

Component 1 comprises the revitalization and climate proofing of 16 existing irrigation schemes. This will be achieved through rehabilitation of infrastructure such as river bank reinforcement, bioengineering techniques, gabions, geotextiles, structure anchoring, use of erosion control blankets, and construction of check dams. The project also involves the creation and remooting of existing water channels. These activities, which would cover an irrigated area of approximately 10,500 ha, will be implemented in four districts with a total population representing almost 380,000 people. A total of 630 ha of the irrigated area covered by the project will be provided with solar power equipment for pumping.

The project proposes to strengthen and enhance institutional capacities, each Irrigation Management Committee (IMC) would be trained in organizational management, conflict resolution, climate-adapted operation and maintenance (O&M), and the monitoring, establishment and enforcement of water use rules. The proposal states that GCF resources would be used for the climate-proofing aspects of the irrigation schemes as well as for the climate-related costs associated with the project implementation costs. In this regard, climate-related costs were estimated to be as high as 78 per cent of the total costs.* Questioned about the reason for including abstraction works as climate-related

*Assessment based on the funding proposal with date of submission 4 December 2019 and its annexes.
† Funding proposal submitted by UNDP, dated 30 October 2019, page 144.

Each Irrigation Management Committee (IMC) is responsible for the management of an irrigation scheme, including operation and maintenance.


---
**Page 132**
---

(costs which represent 36 per cent of the total costs of the irrigation scheme), the proponent answered:

(a) Since the nature and scale of investment in the abstraction works enables continuous water availability, including during dry periods (as it involves activities such as drilling in non-renewable groundwater aquifers), there is a need to consider additional mitigation measures required due to climate change/variability, which leads to frequent drying of rivers and other surface water sources. In view of this, the proponent has included in its irrigation schemes, there has been an attempt in table A29 to define what could be financed under component 1. This is not intended as a comprehensive list but rather for the government to address baseline issues. Therefore, while it is true that abstraction works are not directly related to climate change, it has been deemed reasonable to treat this investment largely addressing climate change.

5. Component 1 also targets rain-fed farmlands by providing field training to 4,900 lead farmers in each of the 26 farmer field schools (FFS) and 300 extension officers through running learning workshops and on-site assistance activities to promote farmer-to-farmer knowledge sharing and exchange. The project would also provide training for climate-resilient water resources management techniques in farmer field schools. Training would be provided through Z8 such schools.

a. In conjunction with capacity-building activity, component 2 would use the farmer field schools to design, adapt and implement CRB packages on rain-fed and irrigated farms. Capacity building under this component is expected to benefit approximately 410,000 people who would train 155 staff of the Department of Agriculture, Technical and Extension Services (AGRITECH), who would then lead train farmers (6,900 for rain-fed land and 630 for irrigated land) with a total of 7,530 farmer field schools. Assuming five people per household, it is estimated that approximately 410,000 people will be trained.

b. The training of 155 national, provincial, district- and ward-level AGRITECH staff under activity 2.2.1 of component 2 would help to significantly strengthen the technical capacities of this personnel.

6. Activity 2.2 of component 2 includes the procurement of inputs and technologies to implement climate-resilient farming practices in rain-fed and irrigated farms through farmer field schools. The project would allocate USD 1,000 for each 5 ha demonstration plot, one for each farmer field school, and an average of USD 150 to each lead farmer for the procurement of seeds, chemical fertilizers, irrigation equipment, etc., as well as other inputs required in matching investments, and, for this reason, the selection criteria would be based on, among other requirements, the availability of water resources.

7. In combination with farmer field schools, component 2 would establish and operationalize five multi-stakeholder innovation platforms. These platforms would facilitate interaction between farmers, extension officers, researchers, private sector, government authorities, government service providers, financial institutions and non-governmental organizations. The project would also provide training to the stakeholders on a regular base and to foster linkages between value chain actors, allowing for market development.

8. Component 3 is exclusively focused on improving climate information availability and use. A total of USD 105,000 will be allocated to this component in order to establish networks by installing 12 automatic weather stations, 10 automatic rainfall stations and 10 hydrological gauging stations at irrigation sites not currently covered by the existing network.

### Paradigm shift potential

#### Potential for knowledge and learning
- Activity 2.3 of component 2 is focused on the generation and sharing of knowledge, lessons learned and best practices through farmer field schools and innovation platforms.
- The project would upgrade data collection and sharing platforms and support knowledge creation, circulation, and exchange between participating agricultural colleges and research centres.

1. The general monitoring and evaluation plan is adequately described in the proposal. At the local level, the following indicators will be used to monitor progress: surveys of lead farmers, Department of Irrigation (DoI) staff on satisfaction with irrigation schemes and surveys of CBA beneficiaries using scorecards.

#### Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment
- The project includes several elements to enable longer-term sustainability, the most important being the establishment of farmer field schools in all three provinces, present in all three components, with targets ranging from farmers to institutional staff at the local and national level. These will be used for training on a wide range of topics including the adoption of efficient water management techniques, CRA production, business development and organization, marketing strategies, value chain analysis, market access, generation and use of climate information for risk-informed agricultural planning.

1. Another contribution to sustainability beyond project lifespan comes from the activities related to the establishment of farmer field schools in all three provinces with key value actors, which would promote the participation of the private sector, enable the provision of additional services and products, and provide a platform for knowledge sharing.
- The establishment of a national-level stakeholder innovation platforms would serve as a nexus for promoting partnerships across value chain actors and generate and disseminate knowledge of CRA.

1. The project’s expected outputs (OpEx) for the first year, in order to allow farmers to learn the new farming systems and acquire financial services, include the implementation of demonstration plots and pilot-scale demonstration schemes implemented by the Climate-Resilient Infrastructure Development Facility pilot project, funded by the United Kingdom of Great Britain and Northern Ireland, such as the climate-resilient maize variety.

The independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP), the proponent noted that “in the event of failure due to unforeseen circumstances, the government is committed to cover any costs or shortfalls to ensure the success of the schemes.”^1

### Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies

20. Although the project does not include activities directly related to advancing the regulatory framework for climate change adaptation in public institutions and serve to enable the mainstreaming of CBA practices and climate considerations into national and local agriculture policies and development plans.

#### Scalability and replicability

21. The strong focus on capacity building and knowledge management, structured through training workshops, field visits, farmer field schools and innovation platforms, gives the project a high upscaling potential within the targeted woredas and high replication potential in other sectors.

22. Given that neighboring African countries face similar climate change effects and present comparable socioeconomic situations and institutional and knowledge barriers, the present proposal would have high replication potential in the region.

### Sustainable development potential

#### Environmental co-benefits

23. The implementation of agro-forestry practices, water resource management and bioengineering methods for soil conservation would bring important environmental benefits, including enhanced biodiversity, reduced soil erosion and consequent river siltation, improved infiltration of rainwater, improved water quality and availability, and, in general, enhanced ecosystem services.

24. The installation of solar power equipment to provide energy for pumping in part of the irrigation schemes would help to reduce carbon emissions. However, this reduction would be insignificant.^2

#### Social co-benefits

25. The greatest social and health benefits reside in the increased food security and consequent improvement in the health of the population that would result from higher yields and diversified production once CBA practices are applied. This would also reduce public health expenses.

#### Economic co-benefits

26. By increasing the agricultural production capacity of local farmers and developing existing markets for their produce, the project would generate additional income for the benefited households, and potentially create new jobs and enterprises.

27. The economic analysis estimates the benefits from investments in irrigation schemes as the profit coming from winter crops, which are virtually impossible to produce without

irrigation due to insufficient precipitation during the dry winter season.13 The calculations use the average profit from tomatoes and sugar beans, assuming that half of the irrigated surface would be cultivated with tomatoes and half with sugar beans.14 The selection of these two crops is based on their profitability in Zimbabwe, their relatively short growing cycles, and shorter cropping cycles.15 Tomato is the most profitable crop, with estimated profits of approximately USD 600/ha per year, followed by sugar beans at approximately USD 370/ha per year. ### Needs of the recipient

#### Scale: High

##### 1.4 Vulnerability of the country, vulnerable groups and gender aspects

###### 1.4.1 Human hazards developed by the Zimbabwe Resilience Building Fund show that southern Zimbabwe is highly prone to extreme climate events, and it presents high relative rates of crop pests and invasive species.

###### 1.4.2 Economic and social development

The area targeted by the project is home to 3.9 million people, and 45 per cent of the national rural population. It is one of the poorest regions of the country, with poverty reaching approximately 70 per cent in some areas. The provinces of Matabeleland South and Manicaland were among the three provinces with the highest proportion of food-insecure households.

During the period 2011–2015 Zimbabwe suffered a severe decline in its economic growth, decreasing from 11.9 per cent in 2011 to 1.5 per cent in 2015. The agriculture sector played an important role in this decline due to the poor performance of the gross national product and is greatly affected by rain patterns and flooding events.

The financial sector is underdeveloped, characterised by low liquidity and non-performing loans. In particular, the rural population has very limited access to financial services.
