1.5. Country ownership Scale: High

1.5.1 Alignment with national climate strategy and policies

The adaptation of the agriculture sector to climate change is stated as a national priority in Zimbabwe's nationally determined contribution. The project is also fully aligned with the National Climate Change Response Strategy, the National Climate Policy, the National Renewable Energy Action Plan, the National Adaptation Plan (2013–2018), and the Zimbabwe Agricultural Investment Plan (2013-2018).

1.5.1 Capacity of accredited entities or executing entities to deliver

The United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) has experience in climate-related programmes and projects, including those implemented over the last four decades in various programmes and projects in the areas of disaster risk management, climate change, poverty reduction, governance, and environmental management with technical and capacity-building support.

UNDP carried out a capacity assessment for the Ministry of Lands, Agriculture, Climate Water and Rural Resettlement (MLACWR) to identify strengths and weaknesses in managerial and operational capacity and weaknesses in the areas of programme management, organizational structure, human resources, information systems, project planning and inventory, financial reporting and monitoring and procurement; the overall risk rating was assessed as low. The executing entity has implemented several climate change projects in the country including those funded by the Global Environment Facility (GEF), the United Nations Children's Fund and the United Nations Environment Programme.

The design phase of the project included consultations with the Ministry of Lands, Agriculture, Climate Water and Rural Resettlement, the Department of Irrigation, the Department of Research and Specialist Services, the Meteorological Services Department, local governments, non-governmental organizations and farmers of the three targeted provinces. 1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: High

### 1.6.1 Financial adequacy

44 In general, the proposed financial structure seems adequate to achieve project objectives. However, the cost estimation process for the revitalization of existing irrigation schemes is not clear. The table presented in Annex II (page 120) shows that the per hectare cost is based on costs of a representative scheme per hand to calculate an average cost per hand. Yet while the four representative hands have per-hectare costs of USD 2,500 to 8,900, the table presented in Annex II (page 121) shows that the total cost for the revitalization is USD 374,600. It is not clear how the per-hand costing was then applied to estimate the cost of each individual scheme.

45 The same applies to the cost estimation process used by the project team for the irrigation scheme (excluding solar costs) of USD 27,570, and the Nyakhome scheme (USD 168,390). It is not clear how these costs were estimated. Therefore, it remains unclear whether the adequacy of the budget allocation for this activity without a more detailed description of each scheme.

46 The capacity-building scheme consisting of training of trainers, where AGRIEXT staff would train lead farmers in farmer field schools, who then train neighbouring farmers in their communities, appears to be efficient means for increasing the implementation of OBI practices.

47 Given the public service nature of project activities, and the limited resources of the beneficiaries, it can be expected that private sector and/or private investors will not participate. On the contrary, one of the objectives of developing the innovation platforms is to create in private investment.

### 1.6.2 Financial viability

48 Additional income generated from winter crops represents 96 per cent of total project income, as estimated in the economic and financial analysis. As explained in paragraph 27 of this assessment, without concrete financial aid included in the project budget, it is unlikely that the project will be able to generate sufficient income to cover its costs. The project assumes that land from year 2, as assumed on the economic and financial analyses. Therefore, the calculated net annual return on investment (ROI) for the project is not realistic because it does not reflect the first years of project implementation. For this reason, the internal rate of return and net present value presented in the proposal are not considered to be a realistic representation of the most likely scenario.

### 1.6.3 Best practices

49 The project plans to integrate modern, climate-resilient agro-ecological practices with traditional knowledge. Building on the portfolio of OBA packages designed during project preparation phase, the project will work closely with local farmers and other stakeholders in rural areas for the identification and prioritization of climate-related issues and solutions.


---
**Page 139**
---

The designs of both irrigation and CSA components took into account key lessons learned in previous designs, and are described in detail in each specific feasibility study.10

However, concerning irrigation, the proposal did not include a detailed description of the 21 irrigation schemes, and the provided descriptions are too general and vague to enable assessment of their technical viability or cost-effectiveness. Regarding climate resilience, the proposal included no information on how the project would address water availability.

Regarding climate-resilient crops, the value chain feasibility study argues that cassava was not selected owing to the lack of a developed market demand. CSA packages also fail to include any mention of the potential for growing drought-tolerant sorghum and millet varieties11 and tropical climate moderated by altitude provide ideal conditions for this low-input crop, which has been shown to be more productive than maize in many parts of southern Africa, and is one of the most drought-tolerant crops in the world. Capable of growing on marginal land, it can be harvested at any time during the dry season, and only needs to be harvested when needed, thereby serving as a reserve food when other crops fail.

For these reasons, which give cassava an unparalleled potential for enhancing food security, it is strongly recommended that the project include sorghum and millet in its CSA package. The lack of developed market demand could be addressed by taking advantage of the communication and capacity-building activities included in the project.


## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The independent TAP recommends this funding proposal for approval.

# Gender documentation for FP127

## Annex XII: Gender Assessment and Action Plan
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL

### Building Climate Resilience of vulnerable agricultural livelihoods in Mzingwane, Runde and Save river basins in southern Zimbabwe

#### I. Introduction

The proposed project supports the Government of Zimbabwe to strengthen the climate resilience of vulnerable agricultural livelihoods within the Mzingwane, Runde and Save river basins in southern Zimbabwe. The direct beneficiaries are estimated at 543,000 people living in V, VI, VII, VIII, IX, X, XI, XII, XIII, XIV and V of the provinces of Manicaland, Masvingo and Matabeleland South in southern Zimbabwe, who will benefit from climate change adaptation interventions. These include improved access to water resources through infrastructure and rehabilitation, and improved access to risk and financial mechanisms in Zimbabwe. It is expected that this project will contribute towards achieving the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) related to climate information systems and weather information. In addition, it is expected that the total rural population within the project area will benefit from increased access to early warning systems from climate information, weather and agricultural advisories as well as from the learnings from this project.

This gender assessment aims to provide an overview of the gender situation in Zimbabwe, with a specific focus on the Mzingwane, Runde and Save river basins. The assessment will identify gender issues that are relevant to the project, and examining potential impacts of climate change on women and men within these areas. This information is used as part of the project plan which is annexed to this report. The assessment is based on available data from studies conducted by the Government of Zimbabwe, donor agencies, and multilateral development banks, including the following:

- A Gender Analysis for the National Climate Change Policy (NCCP) (2013-2017); Zimbabwe Comprehensive Agriculture Policy Framework (2015-2035); Zimbabwe’s National Climate Change Response Strategy 2015; National Environmental Policy and Strategic Plan for Water Resources Development.
- National level consultative interviews with strategic stakeholders that include Ministry of Agriculture, Land Reform and Rural Development, Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources, Gender Commission, UN agencies, the private sector and development partners.
- Field visits to communities in Mzingwane, Runde and Save river basins to assess gender equality and stakeholder conditions, especially with female farmers;
- Lessons learned from studies and assessments from past studies and assessments on gender in Zimbabwe undertaken by the Government of Zimbabwe, UN agencies, development partners, civil society organizations, and multilateral development banks; and Past and ongoing projects have also been reviewed.
- Conducting stakeholder consultations and engaging women affected by the project and appropriate stakeholders;
- Reviewing and incorporating findings from other sub-assessments conducted for this proposal including the Gender Assessment for the National Climate Change Policy (NCCP) (2013-2017); and financial analysis which are based on consultations with stakeholders and also provide some relevant insights, and

*Government of Zimbabwe and UNDP (2015) Technical Pre-feasibility Study: Building Climate Resilience of Vulnerable Agricultural livelihoods in Mzingwane, Runde and Save river basins in southern Zimbabwe.*

## Gender equality and social inclusion in Zimbabwe

Building climate resilience of smallholder agriculture in Zimbabwe’s river basins requires women as key players, given that they form 70% of the rural population and also contribute towards 20% of food production. This proposed project will provide an opportunity for women to lead and contribute to addressing climate change adaptation challenges.

The Zimbabwe National Gender Policy (NGP) (2013–2017) recognizes the limited gender considerations in policy frameworks on the management and protection of environment and natural resources. The Policy acknowledges that women are disproportionately affected by environmental degradation, with a focus on gender-sensitive perspectives. The Gender and Environment Theme of the NGP’s objective is to increase gender-responsive climate change adaptation and mitigation initiatives.

In 2013, the Government formulated and put in place a five-year economic development plan known as the Medium Term Strategic Framework (MTSFF) with a vision: Towards an Empowered Society and the Growing Economy. The blueprint has four clusters, namely Food Security and Nutrition, Social Services and Poverty Eradication, Infrastructure and Industrialization, and Environment and Natural Resources Management. It aims to address the aforementioned sectors.

The Food Security and Nutrition Cluster aims to strengthen the climate and disaster resilience of smallholder farmers in Zimbabwe by promoting sustainable land management practices; promote drought-resistant, high yielding and heat tolerant varieties and promote bio fuels and renewable energy.

The Rio Convention (1992) noted that without gender equality, key areas of poverty reduction, environmental sustainability and long-term economic development would not be attained. Due to their different levels of participation in the economy, women have a limited knowledge of natural resources, yet their contribution is often unrecognized. Ensuring climate-resilient agriculture production requires equal access for men and women farmers to land, credit, inputs, extension services, markets, and information.

Vulnerability of women to climate change is higher than that of their male counterparts in Sub-Saharan Africa, particularly in Zimbabwe as it is orchestrated by and related to the intersection of biological, socio-economic, and political factors. Women are more vulnerable because they have less access to land, education, wealth, reliance on natural resources, health status, and access to productive resources as well as assets for income generation. These factors make women more vulnerable than men in terms of their vulnerability to climate change.

The project envisages that, if barriers to climate change resilience – lack of technical and financial capacity to invest in climate resilient agriculture production and to participate in climate resilient agricultural extension services – are eliminated, rural smallholder farmers, particularly women, can become resilient. The project will be implemented through a participatory approach with the aim of institutional coordination; it is expected that the benefits of the project will be manifested through enhanced food production and climate-proofed income generation capacity, strengthened access to markets, improved livelihoods, adoption of sustainable agricultural production practices, as well as adaptive management of high risk systems in a sustainable manner.

# Annex XII (c) Gender Assessment and Action Plan GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL

There is potential for communities to take the initiative and act on adapting to climate change, such that both men and women equitably play critical roles in reducing their vulnerabilities and build their resilience to potential new risks. In the past, communities utilized indigenous knowledge systems (IKS) to cope with climate variability and change. However, these IKS are not always sufficient or effective when compared against the challenges posed by climate change.

The project will be implemented in southern Zimbabwe covering three river basins: The Save, Umvumbe and Muzi. These rivers flow through Mashonaland West and Matabeleland South. Targeting districts for this study in each province are in Manicaland province (Buhera, Chimanimani and Chigodonga Districts), Matabeleland North Province (Gwanda, Marondera, Mutare, Musina, Nyanga, Rusape and Victoria Falls Districts) and Matabeleland South Province (Bulawayo, Gokwe, Masvingo, Mutoko, Umtali and Zvimba Districts). The districts were selected based on the following criteria: 1) Proximity to the river basins; 2) High poverty levels; 3) High vulnerability to climate change as affected by the 2015/16 El Niño season; 30% were from Southern Zimbabwe. The high incidence of poverty in these selected districts renders inhabitants susceptible and little able to cope with the impacts of climate change.

## Gender Inequality Indices

Through the years, several indices have been developed to quantify the concept of gender inequality. This section will provide an overview of the following indexes as a measure to gender inequality in Zimbabwe:

* **Gender Inequality Index (GII)**
* **Gender Development Index (GDI)**
* **Gender Parity Index (GPI)**
* **Social Institutions and Gender Index (SIGI)**

### Gender Inequality Index (GII)

The Gender Inequality Index (GII) reflects gender-based inequalities based on three dimensions: 1) Reproductive Health, 2) Empowerment and 3) Economic Activity. Reproductive health is measured by maternal mortality ratio, adolescent birth rate, proportion of births attended by skilled personnel held by females and attainment in secondary and higher education by sex, while economic activity is measured by female participation rates in the labor force, female employment rates, female entrepreneurship and female representation in parliament. Empowerment development due to inequalities between male and female achievements in the abovementioned GII dimensions. Zimbabwe has a GII of 0.584 (2016) and ranks 112 out of 153 countries assessed.

### Gender Development Index (GDI)

The Gender Development Index (GDI) is a relatively new measure introduced in 2014 by the Human Development Report Office (HDRo) that measures gender inequalities in achievement in three basic dimensions of human development, namely health (measured by female and male life expectancy at birth), education (measured by female and male adult literacy rates and female and male secondary completion for adults aged 25 years and older) and command over economic resources (measured by female and male

### Social Institutions and Gender Index (SIGI)

The Organization for Economic Cooperation and Development (OECD) developed the Social Institutions and Gender Index (SIGI). The SIGI is a composite index that measures gender inequality in social institutions, which are defined as laws, policies, practices, and social norms. The study grouped into five sub-indexes: discriminatory family code, restricted physical integrity, son bias, restricted resources and assets, and restricted civil liberties to measure the discrimination against women in social institutions. The SIGI is measured on a scale of 0 (low gender inequality) to 1 (high gender inequality). High and Very High. Countries in the SIGI Very Low Category indicate good progress in progressing towards equality between men and women in social institutions, while countries in the Very High category are not making significant progress toward gender equality. The 2014 SIGI value for Zimbabwe is 0.5932 and is categorized in the Medium SIGI Category.

### Poverty

Zimbabwe is a low-income country, with an estimated 72% of the population living in chronic poverty.[1] Women are more likely to be poor than men, experiencing higher levels of poverty compared to their counterparts, whilst they also remain marginalized in terms of access to productive resources and in labour force participation. These other factors contribute to higher poverty headcount for women, in comparison with men.

Poverty is higher in women-headed households (72%), in comparison to male-headed households (58%).[2] The determinants that validate the ability to exit out of poverty are more favourable in Zimbabwe, as women-headed households have a lower level of education and access to productive resources than their male counterparts. At the tertiary level, which is a key to their empowerment and poverty reduction. In regard to estimated earned annual income per capita, women earn 50% less than men (Z$128,000 compared with Z$243,000), with Zimbabwe ranking 34 out of 144 nations globally with a value score of 0.685, slightly above the global average of 0.679.[3] In Zimbabwe, statistics show that women are more vulnerable to gender-based violence than men; 26% of female-headed households live under the Total Consumption Poverty Line.[4] Table 1 shows the poverty status of women in terms of the Special Human Needs Index (SHNI) and the Vulnerability Index (VI). Women have a greater share than their male counterparts in terms of the following: lack of access to food security, water and sanitation, health care services, education (52%) and Protection (90%), all denoting how they form a large portion of the impoverished in Zimbabwe.

# Annex XII (c) Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL

The Government of Zimbabwe is working towards poverty eradication, especially for women who bear the brunt and is a signatory to various conventions and protocols aiming to achieve poverty reduction. The Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action (1995) and the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development set strategic objectives, calling on state parties to eradicate women's poverty.[28] At the national level, the 2013 Constitution of Zimbabwe guarantees equal rights between men and women in all spheres of economic development with the National Gender Policy (2013-2017) echoing the same sentiments.

## Health and reproductive health

Zimbabwe has prioritized women-related health issues, and access to health through various initiatives, policies and strategies. Notable interventions include the following:

- **resuscitation of primary health care facilities in rural areas and low income urban areas;**
- **the introduction of a national family planning policy framework;**
- **the Prevention of Mother to Child Transmission (PMCT) programme;**
- **the National Breast Cancer (NBC) Programme for cervical cancer 2013-2017, and a supportive health policy framework.[29]**

Given the role women play in health service provision at all levels, most of the policies are pro-women and child-friendly. The maternal mortality rate has decreased from 586 per 100,000 live births (in 2013) to 75 deaths per 100,000 live births in 2014 compared to 75 deaths per 100,000 live births in 2013. Zimbabwe has the highest maternal mortality rate in the world at 470/100,000 live births in 2013.[30] The statistics on maternal mortality rates indicate great improvement in the health sector and a reduction of women dying during pregnancy or childbirth (probability of dying before the first birth) was 58 deaths per 1000 in 2014 compared to 75 deaths per 1000 in 2013. The number of women who died from complications related to pregnancy and childbirth has also reduced significantly, from 69% (in 2013) to 58% (in 2014). Zimbabwe has a life expectancy of 52 (females) and 46 (males) [indicating a female-to-male ratio of 1.15]. The country has also made progress in reducing the number of women who are overweight.[31] However, despite these achievements, the World Bank report notes that the economic challenges facing the country have affected women's access to quality health services and supportive social services.

Increased climate change and rainfall variability is likely to result in floods, which would lead to the distribution of waterborne diseases such as chikungunya, typhoid, guinea worm, dysentery, diarrhoea and malaria. The impact of climate change will also be felt by women who are responsible for fetching water from distant sources due to climate change owing to the traditional set-up of Zimbabwean society which designates the roles of provision of food, water, clothing and fuel to women and children. The impact of climate variability will also imply more droughts and greater hardships for women and children who will have to walk longer distances to fetch water.[32]

Zimbabwe occasionally suffers from water-related diseases. One notable example is the cholera outbreak of 2008 that claimed over 4,300 lives.

### Education

In 1980, soon after gaining independence, Zimbabwe adopted a policy of free primary education for all by abolishing school fees, leading to an exponential increase in enrollments. The country is on course to achieving gender parity in primary and secondary education while lagging behind in the tertiary sectors. Factors contributing to this disparity include poor infrastructure, lack of qualified teachers, and cultural practices like initiation practices requiring girls to be out of school for extended periods, security concerns and social norms that discourage female participation.

Zimbabwe is ranked 96th of 144 countries, with a score of 0.573, well above the global score average of 0.508, which places Zimbabwe in the top half of the Global Gender Gap rankings as of 2016. Table 5 shows gender enrollment, literacy levels and Global Gender Gap ranking of Zimbabwe in 2016. The country's high female literacy rate is a significant factor contributing to its ranking among the country first out of 144 countries. Enrolment at tertiary level education, on the other hand, negatively affects the country’s ranking ability.

In view of the difficulties faced by women and girls in accessing tertiary education, the University of Zimbabwe has introduced a policy to provide free accommodation for female students at its Harare campus to ease the situation of gender imbalance at tertiary level education. To date, all tertiary institutions have embraced the AA Policy initiative. Zimbabwe legislates uniform affirmative action measures to increase the number of women and girls in higher education. The government also provides financial assistance and a stipend for ensuring girl stay in schools. The country launched the National Action Plan of Zimbabwe in 2006, a 2009 review of the Educational Act, which made the Act gender-responsive in terms of gender equality.

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL

Zimbabwe is a signatory to the Southern African Development Community (SADC) Protocol on Gender and Development of 2008, which calls for equal representation of men and women in politics. The country is also a signatory to the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action adopted by the United Nations at the Beijing Platform for Action which call for the realization of equality between women and men through women’s equal access to, and equal opportunities in political and public life improving their status in these domains. Women are entitled to equal participation with men in all spheres of society including full participation in politics and decision making.

Women make up more than half of Zimbabwe’s Parliament with 124 women having been sworn in as Members of Parliament (MPs) since 2013. Following the 2008 General election, women's representation in Parliament more than doubled from 17% to 35% in the 2013 elections. This increase in women’s participation is attributed to the introduction of a quota system for women’s representation (Section [ii]), which provided for an additional 60 women members, six from each of the 10 provinces, elected under a First Past The Post (FPTP) system. The quota system was introduced by the Electoral Act No.25 of 2008 and calls for both males and females to be equally represented in all Institutions and Agencies at every level. Emulation of this model has been encouraged by the Government of Zimbabwe with a commitment to increase women’s representation in parliament to at least 30%, which is considered the minimum for collective effectiveness.

As part of this progress, women's representation in local government, both urban and rural, decreased from 19% from previous elections to 16% following the 2013 general election. It is argued that this was due to absence of a quota system at the local level. The Government of Zimbabwe has introduced a quota system at local government level using the First-Past-The-Post (FPTP) system which does not favour the representation of women candidates because it allows the candidate with a majority of votes to take all. This is in contrast to the proportional representation system used for parliamentary elections, which recognises the ratios of voters by all candidates (Used in the Parliamentary and Senate Representation.)

Gender and Employment

In Zimbabwe, customary law promotes the perception of women’s inferiority and creates a psychological basis for their discrimination in employment. Traditional and social practices and attitudes contribute largely to the underrepresentation of women in employment. More women than men continue to be marginalised in employment.

While the country is competitive in labour force participation, there are notable gender gaps in the areas of leadership and professional positions. Women make up only 35% of all managers and senior officials in formal employment in Zimbabwe, with 21.1% of males and only 19.0% of females involved in the labour force. Women also hold a lower share (47%) than men (68%) in professional and technical positions, with a female to male ratio of 0.71, whilst in part-time employment women comprise 65% and men 47%, with a favourable female to male ratio of 1.37. The ability of Zimbabwean women to rise to positions of leadership remains low as only 29% of the country’s parliamentarians are women compared to 71% for men, while women hold just 59% of professional working roles.

Zimbabwe as a government has crafted various policies and strategies to help close the gender gap in economic participation and opportunity. Post-2004, the Government of Zimbabwe pursued a number of gender responsive economic policies and programmes. Zimbabwe has ratified 26 International Labour Conventions (ILO) including ILO Convention No. 189 on Domestic Workers; ILO Convention No. 111 – no discrimination in employment; con. 39 – for formal labour; con. 105 – abolition of forced and compulsory labour; con. 29 – equal remuneration; con. 107 – abolition of child labour; con. 182 – elimination of worst forms of child labour; con. 169 – indigenous peoples’ rights; con. 114 – no discrimination in employment; con. 151 – for agricultural workers and con. 183 – on the SADC Protocol on Gender and Development of 2008, recommends state parties to amend and enact policies for gender equal access to employment, including equal pay for work equal work, eradication of discrimination against women in all areas of economic activity, and elimination of any form of discrimination. The Public Service Regulation prohibits discrimination on the basis of gender at all stages of employment from recruitment to retirement. ### Access to resources

The patriarchal nature of Zimbabwe's society presents women from having formal financial options and from accumulating assets and productive resources. This limits women’s ability to offer assets as collateral, and hence access credit. Women are also less likely than men to own livestock or other productive assets, which in some communities, constituting their ability to make independent decisions or contractual arrangements. Women's share of the agricultural land is very low, and this has continued despite legal framework put forward to date. In rural areas, women’s access to land and other productive resources in Zimbabwe, is entirely determined by men, as it is governed by the patriarchal system.

The ownership of land and other productive resources is a key indicator of women's empowerment in Zimbabwe. Women own only 10% of all land in Zimbabwe, which represents less than one-third of total population and 80% of those in farming areas. Despite playing a key role in the agricultural sector, women do not have equal access to land (which is a critical productive asset), as their male counterparts do, in both communal and commercial farms. Women are also less likely to own livestock or other productive assets. A recent survey showed that 40% of female landholders, compared to only 41% male landholders, reported that they had no access to land. Furthermore, statistics on the ownership of properties show that more than half (52%) of women in Zimbabwe do not have any property title or certificate which inhibits women in accessing loans and credit facilities which are pivotal to their economic development.

The Government of Zimbabwe has taken action to bridge these gaps, considering considerable improvement in the area. The Women's Fund was established through the Treasury, the Women’s Fund and Gender Responsive Budgeting (GRB). There has been significant progress on gender equality in agriculture, with the establishment of women's agricultural service development agencies and government institutions. The UNDP/IFC Scaling Up Joint Operation project for example highlights the successes from village savings and credit associations which are enabling women access financial services.

Other projects that provide lessons are mentioned in section 4.1 of the Inclusive risk and financial analysis. The Small Enterprise Development Corporation (SEDEC) is a good example, as it has been able to increase its female clients from less than 30% of its products and services to women entrepreneurs, having funded women's projects since 1986. However, despite these improvements, women still face significant challenges in accessing fixed assets which most women do not have. As observed in the inclusive risk and financial analysis, there is need

to sensitise the formal intermediaries on inclusive credit and loan schemes particularly for the benefit of women smallholder farmers. The Government of Zimbabwe's institutions and Ministries have made significant progress in addressing gender imbalances in agriculture.

However, there is still a need to develop land reform policies that ensure women's access to land.[16]

In Zimbabwe, the issue of Gender Based Violence (GBV), often labelled as a 'pandemic' by Gender Experts, is rooted in the historically unequal power relations between men and women.[17] The history of violence against women has been one of male dominance and control over female sexuality, reproduction, and bodies. Women are more likely to be victims of GBV than men. Types of GBV include: sexual torture, exploitation, humiliation, assaults, molestation, domestic violence, incest, involuntary prostitution, torture, and rape. Female genital mutilation and other harmful traditional practices are also forms of GBV. The impact of GBV on women's health, fertility, and mortality, are forms of GBV that cannot be overlooked.

Zimbabwe is a signatory to international regional and national gender protocols and conventions which emphasize the need for States parties to prevent and protect women against GBV.[18] The CEDAW commits states parties to prevent and protect women against GBV, including against domestic violence. Article 20 of the SAICG Gender Protocol on Violence Against Women (VAW) requires States Parties to take legislative measures to eliminate all forms of GBV in accordance with international human rights law, legislation and government programmes.[19] In line with international protocols and conventions, the 2013 Constitution of Zimbabwe recognises women's right to life, liberty and security of person as well as the right to personal security. Section 53 of the Zimbabwe Constitution includes the right to be free from torture and degrading treatment including GBV, while Section 25 of the same constitution (Protection of the Family) provides for the protection of children against all forms of violence.

In Zimbabwe, 47% of women and 35% of girls under 18 have experienced either sexual or physical violence at some point in time.[20] Thirty-7%, of women in Zimbabwe reported experiencing non-marital rape in their lifetime. The prevalence of early marriage is also high with 69% of women aged 45–49 reporting that early marriage is common in Zimbabwe, with women aged 15–19 years comprising 20% of the marriages which occurred over a five-year period.[21] Early marriage has been shown to be associated with GBV and poor health outcomes for girls. The prevalence of early marriage in Zimbabwe is higher than the global average at 26%. This shows a gender gap in terms of human development, as women are deprived of their developmental time through early marriages. The National Gender Based Violence Strategy (2010-2015) aimed at reducing GBV in Zimbabwe by 20% by 2015. Zimbabwe has enacted laws and policies to eradicate GBV, which includes inter alia, the Domestic Violence Act (2013), the Child Protection Act (2014) and the National Gender Based Violence Strategy (2010-2015). Zimbabwe National HIV and AIDS Strategic Plan II (2011–2015) and the Zimbabwe National Action Plan on GBV (2016).

The country has to date adopted the 365 Days of Action Campaign Initiatives to keep GBV in public discourse every year, which includes traditional, religious and community leaders as major actors in addressing GBV. The Ministry of Women Affairs, Gender and Community Development’s women empowerment programmes are mostly in tandem enabling women to be able to access justice through the Victim Friendly Unit (VFU) of the Police (Victim Protection, Participation and Programmes) Campaign to GBV which is informed by the Africa Unite to End Violence Against Women.

Several extensive studies were conducted to acquire comprehensive knowledge on the extent and prevalence of GBV in the country. These are the 2010-11 Zimbabwe Demographic Health Survey (ZDHS), the National HIV Prevalence, Incidence, Behavior and Communication Survey (NPHCBS) and the UN-VIPF Baseline conducted in 2013. GBV monthly statistics are also provided by the Victims Friendly Unit of the Police based on reported cases, although marred by widespread underreporting.

The government set up an inter-Ministerial Cabinet Committee on Rape and developed a National action plan to address rape and GBV in Zimbabwe (National Action Plan on GBV 2016-2021) in line with the provisions of the Beijing Platform for Action relating to the provision of shelters for the survivors of gender-based violence, the establishment of national mechanisms for the prevention and prosecution of cases of Child Sexual Abuse. This response has also created the Victim Friendly courts, which protect vulnerable witnesses in abuse related cases.

## IV Mechanisms to address gender inequality in Zimbabwe - legal and administrative framework

The Government of Zimbabwe 2011 Constitution is hailed for a very strong gender equality position. Gender equality is enshrined in Article 34 of the Constitution and Section 17 provides more focused highlights of gender equality. The Constitution Preaches “[a]ll citizens commits themselves to upholding and defending fundamental human rights and freedoms.” Gender Equality (g) is one of the Founding Values

and Principles of the Constitution. Under the same section (i) (iii) the Constitution recognises the rights of women, the elderly, youth and children.

Section 17 is dedicated to Gender Equality and outlines the following:

(1) The State must promote full gender balance in Zimbabwean society, and in particular:
(a) The State must promote the full participation of women in all spheres of Zimbabwean society on an equal basis with men.
(b) The State must take all measures, including legislative measures, needed to ensure that:
(i) both genders are equally represented at all institutions and agencies of government at every level; and,
(ii) women constitute at least half the membership of all commissions and other elective and appointed governmental bodies established by or under this Constitution or any Act of Parliament.
(c) The State and all institutions and agencies of Government at every level must take practical measures to ensure that women have access to resources, including land, on the basis of equality with men.
(d) The State must take positive measures to rectify gender discrimination and imbalances resulting from past practices and policies.

Section 16 "Equality and Non-discrimination" highlights that all persons are equal before the law, and women and men have the right to equal treatment, including the right to equal opportunities in political, economic, social and cultural spheres. The section highlights non-discrimination on grounds of race, colour, tribe, place of birth or origin, language, religion, marital status, gender, disability, age, pregnancy etc.

The Ministry of Women Affairs - Gender and Community Development (MINWAGCD), also known as the National Gender Commission and the Zimbabwe Gender Commission (ZGC), was established in accordance with the Zimbabwe Gender Equality Act (Chapter 10:31), and acts as the key institution responsible for working towards gender equality in Zimbabwe.

# Zimbabwe’s National Climate Change Response Strategy (2015)

## Gender, People Living with HIV and AIDS, and other Vulnerable Groups

Mainstream climate change policies for the vulnerable groups with their active participation.

i. Strengthening the capacity of the vulnerable groups.
ii. Enhancement of early warning system on droughts, floods, and cyclones to ensure a coordinated approach in providing them with emergency services.
iii. Mainstream gender within environmental policy, planning, and monitoring activities by mobilizing men and women’s programmes aimed at achieving equitable and sustainable use of resources.

## National Environmental Policy and Strategies (2009)

### Guideline 24: Vulnerable groups within society need special provisions that guarantee equitable access to natural resources.
i. Provision of water for drinking and cooking, as well as other vulnerable groups to environmental resources and livelihood opportunities.
ii. Mainstream gender within environmental policy, planning, and monitoring activities by mobilizing men and women’s programmes aimed at achieving equitable and sustainable use of resources.

## Gender issues in response to threats of water scarcity to agricultural livelihoods of vulnerable smallholders

In Zimbabwe, more women than men are poor and live in rural areas where livelihoods are intimately linked with the exploitation of natural resources that are extremely vulnerable to climate change. Gender differences in property rights, access to information, and cultural, social and economic roles make women more susceptible to water scarcity. Women’s limited access to land, coupled with their greater dependence on water supplies and fuel wood accessibility, which increases the distances women need to go to secure such resources, makes them particularly vulnerable to climate change. This is further exacerbated by Female Child Headed Households lacking access to irrigation systems. Climate variability exacerbates incidences of droughts and floods, leading to food insecurity and malnutrition. Women are limited in their access to medical services and their reproductive role, including caring for the sick. Zimbabwe has a history of HIV/AIDS which is associated with a high risk of health problems; women being the most affected as care givers.

The Torke Murkosi Dam bursting caused by unusually heavy rainfall and excessive flooding led to the displacement of 12,500 people in the Murehwa District. Of these displaced persons, 1,234 households were rated high risk while 8,000 households were downrated as medium risk. The same flood is reported to have caused a total of 67 deaths and 90 injuries. The flood affected 5,000 people with various illnesses, namely, diarrhoea (37.5%), cough (44.4%) and fever (11.7%). Severe acute malnutrition was found to be above the national average of 8.6%. Environmental conditions in the camp were noted to be poor due to lack of sanitation facilities and water supply. Women are not only victims of environmental change, women seem to be shown not just as climate change victims or adaptation beneficiaries but as being crucial agents of change. The role of women is increasingly recognized for their contributions made by women as part of their daily lives (through climate-smart agriculture in the face of increasing risk) and through disaster recovery and preparation. By integrating these skills into project design and

# Annex XIII (c) Gender Assessment and Action Plan GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL

## Gender and Irrigation

### Land Rights:
In the old irrigation schemes, women have limited land rights and only access land through their husbands or fathers. This is in contrast to new irrigation schemes where women have almost equal (40-50% ownership) to land through quarter systems, gender sensitive irrigation constitutions and by-laws. Hence, the project will work towards gender equality in land tenure.

### Participation in Decision Making:
In the majority of cases, there is gender parity in the membership of Irrigation Management Committees (IMCs), with women holding 50% representation. However, contribution by women to strategic decisions making in these committees is questionable and this needs to be addressed. The project will work towards ensuring that issues are addressed. Nonetheless, indications from the assessment are that irrigation Management Committees are dominated by men who have more experience in irrigation management than females.

The proposed project builds on this by supporting female leadership development programmes and achieving gender equality in decision making processes.

### Gender Responsive Technologies:
In the old irrigation schemes, women use gender responsive technologies using surface irrigation (which uses gradient for water flow and less lifting of heavy water containers). In contrast, new irrigation schemes have gender responsive technologies using surface irrigation (use of manual labour), however, experience in initial establishment and maintenance is lacking. 

### NGO Supported Irrigation Schemes:
The majority of NGOs supported schemes, whose support base are mainly women smallholder farmers, are reported to have inadequate irrigation planning designs often characterised by poor water management systems and lack of gender responsive irrigation systems. Through Component I, this project is addressing issues such as climate-proofing irrigation infrastructure through training Irrigation Management Committees (IMCs) on efficient water usage.

The project will facilitate exchange visits on best practices for maximizing water availability on rain fed farmland.


## Gender and Dryland Farming

### Land Ownership:
In dry land farming it is largely owned by men as it is based on customary tenure. Women have limited ownership by men. Stakeholder consultations indicated that only 32% of women have customary land rights on communal lands. On irrigation schemes, traditionally plots were held in the name of the head of household (often men). When he passes on, the entire plot is lost and although the widow might remain as a tenant, she has no title.

Access to irrigation by un-married adult girl children is not guaranteed. Recently, as pointed earlier, women have been given land rights through quarter systems. However, there are still many recognising widowed women as plot owners. This project will conduct gender analysis and will build on the existing quarter systems to ensure that women have access to irrigation plots to integrate gender-specific considerations in irrigation constitutions.

### Gender and Livestock Production

## Livestock Ownership: A survey conducted in five districts of Zimbabwe (Chimanimani, Mangwe, Gokwe, Mazoe and Gokwe North) showed that ownership of cattle is higher among males (54.3%) than females (45.7%). Despite having more control over livestock, women have less decision-making power over cattle but have more control over goats and chicken rearing.

## Livestock Ownership by Sex: Women are more likely to own smaller livestock such as chickens and goats in the five districts surveyed. Women also tend to keep smaller livestock often fetch very low prices as they are commonly traded informally between households, whereas men dominate larger livestock such as cattle which are sold through formal supply chains. Men dominate livestock markets, with women mostly involved in marketing of smaller livestock. Female farmers acquire fewer livestock/productive assets than their male counterparts.

Women in leadership: The GRIAM Scaling Up adaptation project has demonstrated the benefits of including women’s empowerment particularly in irrigation management and Climate Smart Villages, the introduction of an element of irrigation maintenance fund and village savings and lending schemes (VSALs) that enhance women smallholder farmers access to finance and productive assets. VSALs and other village based microfinance initiatives have also been introduced by the project which has facilitated organized easier linkages to formal financial intermediaries.

The gender action learning systems (GALS) approach that positions women as the drivers of change for revitalization of agriculture has successfully been implemented in FAO and IRAD projects. Activities have made farming more resilient to climate change by emphasizing the development of rainwater harvesting infrastructure and improved drainage and irrigation systems, as well as the introduction of improved seed and agronomic practices, through Farmer Field Schools.

# Gender Analysis and Recommendations

The gender analysis undertaken at the outset and design of this project acts as an entry point for gender mainstreaming throughout implementation. Stakeholder consultations took place in Harare and Zaka (Five Paraguru) Districts, Zimbabwe. The stakeholders included representatives from government departments, the National Gender Machinery and the Zimbabwe Gender Commission, civil society and both male and female farmers in the selected field sites. Results from the consultations are detailed in the Stakeholder engagement section of this document as well as in the Project Report and Stakeholder Action Plan annexes included in full as an additional annex as part of the proposal.

The gender analysis, through stakeholder engagement and consultation, enabled:

- Assessment of gender-related impacts of climate change and variability for irrigation, dry land farming and livestock production.
- Identification of lessons and good practice for mainstreaming gender to inform design project components.
- Engagement, development and input into the design of the proposed project;
- Development of a Gender Action Plan (GAP) for the project, which will establish a baseline in which to measure improvements and identify areas of focus; and
- Establishment of recommendations to incorporate into the Gender Action Plan and Budget.

The recommendations below seek to address the specific issues and difficulties that women face in responding to immediate threats of water scarcity to vulnerable agricultural livelihoods and to building climate resilience. The following table provides an overview of the gender interventions included in the recommendations include: Project Design and Implementation, Stakeholder Engagement and Monitoring & Evaluation.


## Project design

The project design and implementation will take into consideration the following gender interventions:

- Achieve increases of % of women’s membership in Irrigation Management Committees (IMCs) and build their capacity as female farmers through leadership training programmes.
- Promote women's decision-making skills by allocating a % of women in change of smallholder enterprises.

# Annex XIII (c) Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL

### Contribution to Operations and Maintenance Fund:
- Facilitate women-to-women farmers workshops and hands-on training on managing rainwater and soil moisture.
- Women and men trained in CSA through FFS and adopting and implementing best practices.
- Women and men trained in climate-resilient irrigation, dry land farming, and land use that enhances the climate resilience and productivity of various value chains (e.g., Sesame and horticulture).
- Assist and integrate women's roles in selected value chains as part of specific crop management plans.

### Gender Mainstreaming Approach:
- Ensure gender equality and inclusion throughout all project phases.
- Appear and integrate gender-specific considerations in farming systems and agro-ecological principles, CSA packages, guided by the 50% of women farmers adopting CSA packages.
- Facilitate the development of a gender action plan that includes specific actions to address gender considerations in CSA best practices and learning methodologies to promote knowledge sharing.
- Integrate gender considerations in the development of new materials and climate advisories.
- Work with local communities, including women's groups, to ensure their participation.
- Allocate resources for gender-related initiatives in the climate resilience program for irrigated land and dryland farming.
- Support sustainable, climate-smart income generation interventions for female and male farmers for the project.
- Inclusion of a Gender Specialist position / provision of advice within the project to implement gender-related activities and budgeting.

### Project Implementation:
By promoting women's participation in decision-making processes and specifically targeting disadvantaged women such as those from female-headed households (in rural areas 35% of the households were female-headed) and young women, the project will contribute significantly towards reducing the existing gender disparities. The project will facilitate equal access to skills, knowledge, training, and opportunities for taking up leadership and accessing finance by women, in this way the project aims to contribute to empowerment and social inclusion.

During project implementation, qualitative assessments shall be conducted on the gender-specific benefits including time use analysis in household surveys that can be directly attributed to the project. This will be incorporated into the project's monitoring and evaluation framework (M&E). Indicators to quantify the achievement of project objectives in relation to gender equality include:

### Stakeholder Engagement
The stakeholder consultation and engagement of existing institutions such as key Government Ministries responsible for agriculture, including irrigation, national Government institutions for gender equality, civil society working in the sector, and male and female farmers in agriculture (irrigation, dry land farming and land use) will be conducted to ensure that women's voices are heard within the country's social context and implementing and monitoring the gender aspects of the project.

The stakeholder engagement component of this annex captures the specific issues raised and difficulties that women face in responding to building climate resilience of vulnerable agricultural livelihoods in Matabeleland North, Midlands and Masvingo provinces, Zimbabwe. What follows are highlights of results from the consultations and key recommendations.

Specific issues raised include:

# Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## Gender and Land Rights

- Women’s land rights in the old established irrigation schemes were not adequately captured in the Irrigation Scheme Constitutions. However, relatively newly established irrigation schemes have more progressive constitutions that recognize women as co-holders of land and thus women have land rights.
- In old established irrigation schemes, women attain land upon the death of their husbands. However, such land rights may be revoked through cultural inheritance practices where the eldest son inherits all family property.
- In dry land farming areas, including resettlement schemes, the majority of women do not have land and are forced to access land through their husbands only with a few female-headed households having access to land.
- In communal lands including resettlements, farmers do not have title deeds, thereby limiting both male and female access loans and credit facilities.

## Gender and Marketing Arrangements

The majority of irrigation schemes in Zimbabwe use surface irrigation which delivers water by the gradient. The irrigation design is notably gender-responsive as it demands little labour for women and children.
- The dry land option for dry land farming is viewed as climate smart (through limited amounts of water efficiently) and is technologically gender-responsive. However, the initial installation costs are high and the system requires clean water sources to avoid blockages.
- NGO’s such as CARE and Oxfam have been involved in irrigation schemes that were commenced with limited water supply and involving manual labour for women using the bucket system.

## Governance

The consultation confirmed gender balance in male and female representation on Irrigation Management Committees (IMCs) and sub-committees. However, relying on the ground-indicated that irrigation schemes located in strong patriarchal societies have their top influential leadership positions in the IMCs occupied by male farmers.
- We are in these IMC’s but when it comes to power, we do not have power to make decisions. Most women who are members of the IMCs are there as a token and they are not involved in decision making. They view that women do not steal, and they often say no let her, hence their dominance in these posts!
- Female farmers are underrepresented.

The consultation findings also indicated existence of irrigation schemes that are led by female chairpersons and were noted to be performing very well.


## Gender and Sustainable Marketing Arrangements

Consultations revealed weak marketing arrangements for both dryland and irrigation farmers. Against a background of poor marketing linkages, and dwindling gains for the farmer, there is need for new approaches to improve market competitiveness at selected sites which would involve the participation of both male and female farmers.

## Gender and Climate Change Knowledge


---
**Page 162**
---

# Annex XIII (c) Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL

* Consultations revealed an urgent need for education for both male and female farmers on climate-smart agriculture for irrigated land, dry land farming and livestock production.

### Gender and Livestock

Both males and females have ownership of livestock, with males owning more livestock. The traditional practice of "Momba pumua" and the "she goat" given to the mother of the bride as part of the dowry is a cultural practice that gives men more control over livestock for women. However, these privileges are only enjoyed by older women whose daughters have married.

The consultations also noted that women in irrigated schemes and dry land farming buy their own cattle, which they raise on farms or gain from income generating initiatives and community saving clubs.

### Women Time Use

* How men and women spend time on productive and non-productive activities related to the project.

The project's design will work towards advocacy for women’s equal participation in agricultural activities.

## Gender Mainstreaming in the Project

On the other hand, the women’s empowerment approach explicitly targets female farmers, including from female headed households, with support for the identified agricultural activities. To this end, the project will adopt a gender mainstreaming approach as part of its overall framework; a women's empowerment result with respective and explicit project intervention support for women farmers, paying particular attention to female-headed or female-led households in the project area will be included. The resulting initiative for female farmers from female headed households from the project to support women farmers. The women’s empowerment agenda for the proposed project will be anchored on the following principles: gender equality, equity and inclusion.

The integrated agriculture project provides an opportunity to place gender equality and women's empowerment at the centre of agricultural policy, research, development, capacity building and the institutional development agenda.

### (iii) Mainstreaming in the SAF

The SAF draft specifically highlights Gender Equality, but ensured that the SAF is mainstreamed throughout the document. The day-to-day SAF activities will be executed by the identified facilitators. The gender focal person will ensure that all project activities are gender sensitive and that women’s empowerment is a key area on the project to develop skills and build the capacity of the PAFs, and MFIs in the implementation of the CAP. Where possible, the gender focal person will work with the PAFs and MFIs to identify specific gender-sensitive areas as integrated in project decision making structures. Alternatively, a gender mainstreaming toolkit informed from local context will be piloted to implement the SAF will be considered.

### (iv) Monitoring and evaluation

A young cow is given to the mother of the bride, as part of the bride price. Traditionally such cattle are valued as sacred animals and should not be allowed by the husband and弄乱ing families of the family.

# Annex XIII (c) Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL

Through onset analysis, data has been collated to establish a baseline. This data shall be monitored throughout implementation and evaluation. The analysis identified the differences between men and women within target populations. In order to monitor and evaluate progress of the project, the following outcomes can be measured:

### Quantitative Outcomes:
- Gender equity in leadership positions in Irrigation Management Committees;
- Female and male community members, including from female headed households, with technical skills for irrigation management;
- Gender parity in participation of training activities on CSA, and on the adoption and implementation of climate smart agriculture technologies;
- Gender balance and effective participation in agricultural governance structures in irrigated systems;
- Gender balance and effective participation of innovation platform to build climate-resilience and productivity of selected value chains;
- Women's access to and control over land and other productive resources;
- Business development services component targeting rural women entrepreneur groups;
- Women's access to credit, inputs, extension services, market information and are utilizing knowledge on supported projects;
- Reduced time constraints that arise from shared roles and responsibilities. This will be achieved through improved gender equality in the adoption of CSA technologies, efficient MCOs and improved food production in female headed households who constitute 38% of households in rural areas.

### Qualitative Outcomes:
- Opportunities to generate additional income. Women are more likely to respond to sustainable livelihoods (irrigation, dry land farming and livestock) that address their family's basic needs, such as better health education of children and nutrition, which are part of the proposed project. Additional income will render women more likely to access and health and educational services for themselves and their families.
- Improved communication and leadership skills for women participating in governance structures for the project. Women will be able to participate effectively in decision making roles including positions in Irrigation Management Committees, and leading training innovations and change management activities. As a result of improved gender equality in the adoption of rain fed farming and climate smart agriculture technologies, women will have increased self-esteem.
- Expanded engagement in public and project decision-making structures as a result of the project's capacity building trainings;
- Support for women to participate in educational activities which may include activities related to climate-smart agriculture, decision making, leadership, business, entrepreneurship, and finance, thereby enabling them to become more economically empowered.

### Impact Surveys:
Household surveys will continue till the end of the project to serve as evidence of the success of this project. The survey will be conducted in all villages where women are involved in rain fed farming and climate smart agriculture technologies.
